Reversible localizers: proofs, counterexamples, and an audit of
                   the proposed constructions

                                      15 November 2026


Working status: incomplete. We claim no priority. Each numbered statement below carries
its own status. Unresolved requested assertions are kept distinct from proved reductions.

Conventions. We fix a universe U and let Cat denote its small categories. We put
BX = |N X|, and W∞ consists of functors inducing weak homotopy equivalences on nerves.
Unless Part F says otherwise, a fundamental localizer means Cisinski’s definition: weak
saturation, the terminal-object axiom, and relative Theorem A. The latter says that for
              u
a triangle A −→ B → C, if A/c → B/c belongs to W for every c ∈ C, then u ∈ W . A
category X is W -aspherical when X → e belongs to W . A sieve is closed under sources,
and A/c uses arrows from the image of A to c. Homotopy cofibres of unpointed maps carry
their cone basepoint; suspension spectra of pointed spaces are reduced. All mapping spaces
and homotopy limits and colimits are derived. Statements include empty and disconnected
categories unless an explicit hypothesis excludes them.

1    The sphere-null counterexample
          n for the nilpotent 2-localization of S n , and set
We write S(2)
                                              n
                      N = {X ∈ S∗ : Map∗ (X, S(2) ) ≃ ∗ for all n ≥ 2}.

The mapping space in this definition includes all its components. Vanishing only of the
positive homotopy groups of its constant component would be a weaker condition.
A.1 (Proved). Desuspension, including the bottom dimension. For every pointed
CW complex X, ΣX ∈ N implies X ∈ N.

Proof. The precise classical input is the 2-local EHP homotopy fibre sequence

                             n   E   n+1     H2n+1
                            S(2) −
                                 → ΩS(2) −→ ΩS(2)  ,           n ≥ 1.

Here E is adjoint to suspension and H is the James–Hopf map. We opened its statement in
Boyde [20, Theorem 2.2], whose convention immediately before the theorem is that all spaces
are 2-local. The same statement, including the even-dimensional case, appears in Ravenel
[57, Theorem 1(ii), with p = 2]. We use an actual homotopy fibre sequence in all dimensions,
not merely the metastable exact sequence of homotopy groups.
    Apply the derived pointed mapping-space functor Map∗ (X, −). It preserves homotopy
fibres. Its values on the second and third terms are
                                     n+1                        2n+1
                          Map∗ (ΣX, S(2) ),          Map∗ (ΣX, S(2)  ),

both weakly contractible by hypothesis for every n ≥ 2. Their homotopy fibre is consequently
weakly contractible. For n = 2 the sequence is specifically S(2)   2  → ΩS(2)  3         5 ;
                                                                                   → ΩS(2)
no dimensional exception or limiting argument is involved. This proves the assertion
simultaneously on components and on every homotopy group.

A.2 (Proved). The closure properties actually needed. The class N is invariant
under pointed weak equivalence, contains the point, and is closed under retracts, all pointed
homotopy colimits, and all three two-out-of-three implications in a pointed homotopy cofiber
sequence. Moreover X ∈ N if and only if ΣX ∈ N. Every member is connected.

Proof. For a pointed diagram Xi , mapping out of its homotopy colimit gives the homotopy

from the same mapping-space definition. Suspension is a pointed homotopy colimit, and its
converse is A.1.

shows C ∈ N; if X, C ∈ N, it shows Y ∈ N. If Y, C ∈ N, apply the first implication to

that component and equal to the basepoint on every other component. Components of a CW

A.3 (Proved). The cofiber construction gives a reversible fundamental localizer.
Let A be a weak-equivalence-invariant class of pointed spaces containing the point, closed
under pointed homotopy colimits, retracts, and two out of three in homotopy cofiber sequences.
Define
               WA = {u : A → B in Cat : C(Bu) ∈ A},           C(f ) = Y qhX ∗.
Then WA is a fundamental localizer in the relative-Theorem-A convention and is reversible.
Its aspherical nonempty spaces are exactly the underlying pointed spaces in A. In particular
this applies to A = N, giving WS .

Proof. The cofiber of an identity is contractible. The pointed pushout-pasting law gives a
cofiber sequence C(f ) → C(gf ) → C(g) for composable unpointed maps. One can see it by
applying the pointed cofiber construction to f+ and g+ and collapsing the first cone inside
the iterated cone. Thus two out of three in A gives two out of three for WA .
    Functoriality of homotopy cofiber sends an arrow retract to a pointed homotopy retract,
so WA is closed under arrow retracts. This directly checks the retraction rule in the proposed
note. More explicitly, if i : X → Y , r : Y → X, ri = idX and ir ∈ WA , then the arrow
r : Y → X is a retract of ir : Y → Y : on sources both maps are identities and on targets
they are i and r. Hence r ∈ WA , and i ∈ WA follows from ri = idX . No asserted splitting of
a suspended cofiber is needed.
    A terminal object contracts the nerve of its category, checking the terminal-object axiom.
                                           u
For relative Theorem A take a triangle A − → B → D. The diagrams d 7→ A/d and d 7→ B/d
are covariant, and there are natural weak equivalences

                    hocolim B(A/d) ≃ BA,           hocolim B(B/d) ≃ BB.
                       d∈D                          d∈D

For completeness the projection from the Grothendieck construction of d 7→ A/d to A has
the left adjoint a 7→ (v(a), a, idv(a) ). The nerve homotopy-colimit comparison therefore gives
the first displayed equivalence, and likewise the second, naturally in u. The homotopy cofiber
of the induced map is the pointed homotopy colimit of the cofibers C(B(u/d)). To check
the basepoints, apply (−)+ to both diagrams and then take their pointed cofibers: both
operations preserve colimits. Equivalently the section of cone vertices in the unpointed
construction is collapsed. If every u/d belongs to WA , closure of A under pointed homotopy
colimits now proves u ∈ WA .
    The cofiber of X → ∗ is its unreduced suspension; for nonempty pointed X it is pointed-
homotopy-equivalent to its reduced suspension. The equivalence X ∈ A ⇐⇒ ΣX ∈ A follows
by applying cofiber two out of three to X → ∗ → ΣX. Hence u ∈ WA if and only if C(Bu)
is WA -aspherical. For a homotopy-pushout square the cofibers of its two parallel edges are
naturally equivalent by pushout pasting. The preceding criterion therefore gives the reverse
sieve-cover implication.
    This also checks the precise part of POLES B.2 and B.5 used here. For a cover by two
full sieves the nerve is literally the union of their nerves over the nerve of the intersection:
every string of arrows lies in the piece containing its last vertex. The resulting pushout is a

                                               3
homotopy pushout because both inclusions are simplicial monomorphisms. In the converse
direction, POLES’s category K(u) is the two-layer mapping collage with one cone point on
the source layer; its two full sieves are the mapping collage and the source cone. The mapping
collage retracts onto the target by an adjunction. Applying the reverse sieve implication to
that square gives the cofiber criterion, with the actual functor u recovered by this retraction.
    If the source of f is empty, then C(f ) = Y+ . If both source and target are empty, this
is the one-point space. The construction and relative-Theorem-A argument include these
cases. For the class N, a nonempty Y+ is disconnected and so does not belong to N by A.2;
in particular the empty category has not inadvertently become aspherical.

A.4 (Proved). The unstable separating witnesses. We have RP ∞ ∈ N and RP 2k ∈ /N
                                ∞                              2
for every k ≥ 1. Consequently RP → ∗ belongs to WS , whereas RP → ∗ does not, and
WS is nontrivial.

Proof. The exact input from the attached Miller paper is Theorem C on page 40: if a nilpotent
space Z has bounded mod-p cohomology, then Map∗ (BZ/p, Z) is weakly contractible [53,
Theorem C]. Each S(2)n , n ≥ 2, is simply connected and has mod-2 cohomology only in degrees

0, n. The theorem applies directly; an arithmetic-square argument is unnecessary. Since
BZ/2 ≃ RP ∞ , this proves the first assertion.
    For a CW complex of dimension d and a (d − 1)-connected target Z, cellular obstruction
theory identifies based homotopy classes into Z with H d (−; πd Z). Indeed maps on the
(d − 1)-skeleton are uniquely null up to the required lower homotopies; choices on d-cells are
a cellular cocycle, and homotopies change it by a coboundary. With d = 2k this gives

                            [RP 2k , S(2)
                                      2k
                                          ]∗ ∼                      ∼ Z/2.
                                             = H 2k (RP 2k ; Z(2) ) =

The last calculation uses the cellular cochain differential 2 into the top even cell. It is nonzero.
A.2 and A.3 translate these two membership tests into the claimed functors. Independently
                 2 shows that S 2 is not aspherical.
the identity of S(2)               (2)


A.5 (Proved). The stable subcategory generated by the infinite projective space.
In the stable homotopy category,

                                    loc(Σ∞ RP ∞ ) = loc(S/2).

Every reversible fundamental localizer containing RP ∞ → ∗ therefore contains RP 2 → ∗ after
applying RT . In particular WS 6= RT (WS ).

Proof. Put V = Σ∞ RP ∞ and L = loc(V ). Every localizing subcategory of spectra is a
tensor ideal: for fixed V 0 ∈ L, the spectra E with V 0 ∧ E ∈ L form a localizing subcategory
containing the sphere, and sphere cells generate spectra. This confirms the step of POLES
F.13 used below.
   The Snaith splitting we use is

                 Σ∞ QX ≃         (Σ∞ X)∧j
                             _
                                       hΣj     (X connected and well pointed).
                             j≥1

We opened Snaith [59, Theorem 1.1 and the filtration of Section 2]. Its n = ∞ configuration
space in degree j is contractible with free Σj action; its jth filtration quotient is thus
EΣj+ ∧Σj X ∧j . This identifies the theorem’s summands with those in the displayed formula.
The tensor-ideal and homotopy-colimit properties give Σ∞ QRP ∞ ∈ L.


                                                  4
    The Kahn–Priddy input is a section of spaces. Its opened formulations are Kahn and
Priddy [43, Theorem 3.1], the unnumbered main theorem on page 103 of Kahn and Priddy
[44], and Snaith [60, Theorem 7.5]: if f : RP ∞ → Q0 S 0 induces an isomorphism on π1 , its
infinite-loop extension fe : QRP ∞ → Q0 S 0 admits a map in the opposite direction whose
composite with fe is a 2-local equivalence. Localizing and composing with a homotopy inverse
makes (Q0 S 0 )(2) a retract of (QRP ∞ )(2) as a pointed space. We neither assert nor need an
infinite-loop section. Localization of these connected simple spaces induces localization of
their reduced suspension spectra, so

                                      Σ∞ (Q0 S 0 )(2) ∈ L.

   We spell out the passage from this space to a spectrum. If a connected grouplike H-space
Z has Σ∞ Z ∈ L, the same is true of its bar construction BZ: the reduced suspension
spectrum of each Z r is a finite wedge of smash products of Σ∞ Z, and the bar realization is a
homotopy colimit. This remains true at every iterated bar stage for an infinite-loop space.
For E = τ≥1 S(2) , these stages are Ω∞ Σr E. There is a canonical equivalence

                                E ≃ hocolim Σ−r Σ∞ Ω∞ Σr E.
                                        r≥0

For a fixed homotopy group the comparison becomes an isomorphism once r is large, by
Freudenthal applied to the increasingly connected spaces Ω∞ Σr E; the maps are the adjoints
of the spectrum structure maps. Thus E ∈ L.
    The same bar argument starting with BZ/2 gives all K(Z/2, r) and then, by the displayed
stabilization construction, HZ/2 ∈ L. Smash the canonical triangle τ≥1 S(2) → S(2) → HZ(2)
with S/2. Its first and third terms belong to L; hence so does its middle term S/2.
    For the reverse inclusion, V [1/2] = 0. Indeed its integral homology is 2-primary torsion,
and a connective spectrum with zero integral homology is zero: its first nonzero homotopy
group, if any, would be detected by the stable Hurewicz theorem. The kernel of (−)[1/2] is
loc(S/2). One explicit verification is that S/2r is built by extensions of S/2, while the fibre
of E → E[1/2] is the telescope of the spectra Σ−1 E/2r . Tensor-ideal closure then gives every
object in that kernel. This proves the equality.
    Finally, if W is reversible and contains RP ∞ → ∗, then T (W ) contains V , since the cofiber
of this map is a suspension of RP ∞ . It therefore contains S/2 and the reduced suspension
spectrum Σ∞ RP 2 ≃ Σ(S/2). The definition of R implies RP 2 → ∗ ∈ RT (W ). This confirms
the specific Galois step of POLES F.4–F.6: T (W ) ⊆ L is equivalent, by the generators in its
definition, to W ⊆ R(L); the spectrum assigned to a functor is exactly the reduced suspension
spectrum of its unpointed homotopy cofiber. A.4 supplies the separating nonmembership for
WS .

A.6 (Proved). The odd-prime desuspension repair. For an odd prime p put
                                               2n−1
Jp−1 (S 2n ) = Sb2n and let Zp consist of all S(p)            2n , n ≥ 1. Its null class N
                                                    and all Sb(p)                          p
is closed under desuspension. The resulting reversible localizer contains BZ/p → ∗ and
excludes M (Z/p, 1) → ∗.

Proof. The two Toda fibre sequences, with all spaces p-localized, are

              Sb2n −→ ΩS 2n+1 −→ ΩS 2np+1 ,        S 2n−1 −→ ΩSb2n −→ ΩS 2np−1 .

We opened their explicit statement in Bödigheimer and Henn [10, p. 80, item (ii)]; the first
also appears as Theorem 1(ii) of Ravenel [57]. Apply Map∗ (X, −) exactly as in A.1. If ΣX is
null for the entire displayed family, both loop-space terms of each sequence have contractible

                                               5
mapping space, proving nullity of each fibre. The n = 1 case includes S(p)    1 and J        2
                                                                                       p−1 (S )(p)
and therefore supplies the bottom step explicitly.
    Each target is nilpotent and has bounded mod-p cohomology: the James stage has
finitely many cells before localization, and an odd sphere, including the circle, has bounded
cohomology. Miller’s Theorem C therefore makes BZ/p null for all of them. The target
Jp−1 (S 2 )(p) is simply connected with π2 ∼
                                           = Z(p) , so obstruction theory on the two-dimensional
Moore space gives

                   [M (Z/p, 1), Jp−1 (S 2 )(p) ]∗ ∼
                                                  = H 2 (M (Z/p, 1); Z(p) ) ∼
                                                                            = Z/p.

It is not null. The rest of A.2–A.3 applies unchanged. This is the precise modification
required by the Toda sequences: at an odd prime their even-dimensional fibre is a truncated
James construction, not the even sphere. We do not replace that fibre by a sphere without
an additional argument.

A.7 (Proved). The stable comparison at every prime. For every prime p,

                                   loc(Σ∞ BZ/p) = loc(S/p).

Consequently the odd-prime localizer of A.6 is not RT -fixed, and its bounded-subcomplex
version of B.2 gives an accessible such example.

Proof. Put Lp = loc(Σ∞ BZ/p). We justify the only new input to A.5. The exact all-prime
splitting statement in Caruso et al. [22, Theorem F] supplies maps of pointed spaces
                                           jp0         s0p
                                   Q0 S 0 −→ QBΣp −→ Q0 S 0

whose composite is a p-local equivalence. The section jp0 is not asserted to be an infinite-loop
map. The original paper [44, unnumbered main theorem, p. 103] states the same splitting
and explicitly permits BZ/p in place of BΣp .
    Here is a derivation using the displayed form, which also checks the change of group.
The covering BZ/p → BΣp has degree (p − 1)!. Its stable transfer, followed by the covering
projection, induces multiplication by this degree on ordinary homology; the compatibility of
the stable and ordinary transfers is Kahn and Priddy [43, Proposition 2.1 and Remark 2.3].
The maps on reduced suspension spectra are obtained by removing their sphere summands.
After p-localization this composite is an integral-homology equivalence of connective spectra
and hence a stable equivalence, by the stable Hurewicz argument of A.5. Thus (Σ∞ BΣp )(p)
is a retract of (Σ∞ BZ/p)(p) and belongs to Lp . We have used a homology equivalence of the
composite, not the stronger and unnecessary assertion that the composite is multiplication
by the degree as a stable map.
    The Snaith splitting of A.5 now puts (Σ∞ QBΣp )(p) in Lp . The displayed section puts
  ∞
Σ (Q0 S 0 )(p) there. Iterated bars and stabilization give τ≥1 S(p) ∈ Lp . Iterated bars of BZ/p
give HZ/p ∈ Lp . Smashing the connective-cover triangle of S(p) with S/p then puts S/p
in Lp . Conversely (Σ∞ BZ/p)[1/p] = 0, so the telescope argument of A.5 gives the reverse
inclusion. Finally Σ∞ M (Z/p, 1) ≃ Σ(S/p), and A.6 excludes this Moore space from the
unstable aspherical class. The same countable witnesses belong, or fail to belong, to its ℵ1
bounded-subcomplex version.




                                                 6
2    Accessibility by bounded subcomplexes
We use CW complexes with a distinguished zero-cell as basepoint. The size of a CW complex
in this section means its number of cells. This convention avoids confusing the cell cardinality
of a CW complex with the generally larger cardinality of its singular simplicial set.
B.1 (Proved). The bounded-subcomplex closure lemma. Let A be a class of pointed
CW homotopy types containing the point, closed under pointed homotopy colimits and all
three two-out-of-three implications in homotopy cofiber sequences. Let κ be an uncountable
regular cardinal in U. Define Aκ by the condition that every pointed subcomplex of fewer than
κ cells is contained in a pointed subcomplex of fewer than κ cells belonging to A.
    This defines a class of homotopy types, is contained in A, contains every member of A
with fewer than κ cells, and has the same closure properties. It is closed under desuspension
and retracts. It is generated under pointed homotopy colimits by a U-small set, namely
representatives of the members of A with fewer than κ cells.

Proof. We first record the cardinal bookkeeping. The image of a subcomplex with fewer than
κ cells under a cellular map is contained in a subcomplex with fewer than κ cells. For each
closed cell its image is compact and hence lies in a finite subcomplex; taking their union and
all faces gives the assertion. The same statement holds for a cellular homotopy, since each
closed cell times the unit interval is compact. Countably many repetitions still involve fewer
than κ cells. Regularity also permits taking unions of fewer than κ such subcomplexes.
    For X ∈ Aκ , let PX be the poset of its small pointed A-subcomplexes. This poset is
κ-filtered: the union of fewer than κ objects is small and can be enlarged by the defining
property. Its union is X. This is also its homotopy colimit. Indeed a sphere or homotopy in
the union meets finitely many cells and hence lands in a common member. In the bar model
for the homotopy colimit it meets finitely many cells and index strings; a common upper
bound contracts this finite support into a single member. These two observations prove that
the natural comparison induces isomorphisms on all homotopy groups. Thus X ∈ A. A small
X ∈ A satisfies the condition by taking X itself.
    We next prove independence of the CW model. Let f : X → Y and g : Y → X be
pointed cellular homotopy inverses, and choose cellular homotopies from gf and f g to the
respective identities. Suppose X ∈ Aκ and start with a prescribed small pointed subcomplex
Y0 ⊆ Y . Repeatedly enlarge small subcomplexes on the two sides so that the next ones
contain the images under f, g and the tracks of both homotopies of the preceding ones. At
each stage additionally enlarge the X-side to a small member of A. The cardinal bookkeeping
above permits all choices. At the countable union we obtain small subcomplexes X∞ , Y∞ ,
containing the initial data, on which f, g and their inverse homotopies restrict. The chosen
A-subcomplexes on the X-side are cofinal in its sequential union, so X∞ ∈ A. Hence Y∞ ∈ A
as well. This proves Y ∈ Aκ . A pointed weak equivalence of CW complexes is a pointed
homotopy equivalence, so the class is well defined on homotopy types.
    For closure under pointed homotopy colimits, use the cellular bar model of a strict pointed
diagram F : I → CW∗ . Its cells are indexed by finite strings of arrows of I and cells of
the corresponding F (i); faces use the structure maps. A functorial realization of singular
complexes, if needed, makes all structure maps cellular without changing their homotopy
types, and the preceding paragraph transfers Aκ to those models. Begin with a small
subcomplex of the bar realization. The cells it meets specify fewer than κ objects, arrows,
and cells in the diagram. The subcategory J ⊆ I generated by those arrows has fewer than κ
arrows, since its morphisms are finite words in the chosen arrows.
    For every object of J, enlarge the prescribed small subcomplex of F (i) to a small A-
subcomplex. Enlarge again to include all images under arrows in J, then again to small


                                               7
A-subcomplexes, and repeat. There are fewer than κ objects and arrows, and only countably
many rounds. The resulting unions F 0 (i) ⊆ F (i) are small members of A and form a
J-subdiagram. Their pointed bar construction is a CW subcomplex of the original bar
construction: each face remains in the chosen diagram, degeneracies add no new cells, and the
basepoint section is a subcomplex before it is collapsed. It contains the prescribed cells, has
fewer than κ cells, and belongs to A by homotopy-colimit closure. This proves the required
local smallness of the original homotopy colimit.
    We give the simultaneous cofiber argument in detail. Model a pointed cofiber sequence by
a CW subcomplex inclusion X ⊆ Y and quotient C = Y /X, with quotient map q : Y → C.
For a small subcomplex Y0 ⊆ Y , put X0 = Y0 ∩ X and C0 = q(Y0 ); then C0 = Y0 /X0 with its
inherited CW structure. A prescribed small subcomplex of X can be included by adding its
cells to Y0 . A prescribed small subcomplex of C can be included by lifting its non-basepoint
cells to their unique cells of Y \ X and adding their boundaries in Y . These boundaries meet
only finitely many cells for each characteristic map. This yields a small larger Y1 , still with
X1 = Y1 ∩ X and C1 = Y1 /X1 .
    Suppose any chosen two of X, Y, C belong to Aκ . At stage r, enlarge the small subcomplex
in each of those two vertices to a small member of A, and then restore the three compatibility
equalities by the preceding operations. Repeat countably. At the union the triple is still

                        X∞ = Y∞ ∩ X,         C∞ = q(Y∞ ) = Y∞ /X∞ .

In each of the two designated vertices, the chosen A-subcomplexes are cofinal in that union.
Both vertices therefore belong to A. Its cofiber two-out-of-three property gives the third.
Starting with any prescribed small subcomplex of the third vertex, lifted if it is in C, proves
the defining property for that vertex. This treats all three implications, in particular the
sometimes omitted implication from Y, C to X.
    Here is also a direct desuspension check. If ΣX ∈ Aκ , begin with a small X0 ⊆ X and
choose successively
                           ΣX0 ⊆ K0 ⊆ ΣX1 ⊆ K1 ⊆ ΣX2 ⊆ · · · ,
where each Kr is a small member of A. A small subcomplex of a cellular suspension is
contained in the suspension of a small subcomplex, so the intervening Xr exist. At the union,
ΣX∞ = r Kr ∈ A; cofiber two out of three for X∞ → ∗ → ΣX∞ gives X∞ ∈ A. Its size is
           S

still less than κ. Thus X ∈ Aκ .
     Retracts follow from homotopy-colimit closure: if Y is a pointed homotopy retract of X,
the mapping telescope of the corresponding homotopy idempotent of X is equivalent to Y .
Finally all CW complexes with fewer than κ cells have underlying sets of cardinality at most
max(κ, 2ℵ0 ). There is a U-small set of their CW structures and maps up to isomorphism,
hence also a set of their homotopy-type representatives. The filtered presentation by PX
and the already proved closure show that the representatives lying in A generate exactly Aκ
under pointed homotopy colimits.

B.2 (Proved). An accessible reversible sublocalizer. With the hypotheses of B.1, the
class
                            WAκ = {u : C(Bu) ∈ Aκ }
is an accessible reversible fundamental localizer. A generating set consists of category-of-
simplices models of all maps between CW complexes with fewer than κ cells whose homotopy
cofibers belong to A.

Proof. B.1 and A.3 prove that it is a reversible fundamental localizer. There is a U-small set
Sκ of representatives of the indicated arrows, by the last paragraph of B.1. Applying the

                                               8
singular functor and then its category of simplices gives actual functors in Cat; the natural
realization comparisons preserve the specified arrows and their cofibers. The resulting set is
still U-small, even though its categories may have more cells or simplices than the original
CW complexes. Every member is in WAκ .
     Conversely let a map f have C(f ) ∈ Aκ . If its source is empty and its target is nonempty,
C(f ) = Y+ has S 0 as a pointed retract. Then S 0 ∈ Aκ , and pointed homotopy-colimit closure
makes this class all pointed spaces: cells build every pointed CW complex from S 0 . The
localizer is total, generated by ∅ → ∗. The empty identity causes no difficulty. We may
therefore assume the source is nonempty. Replace f by the inclusion X ⊆ Mf of one end of
its cellular mapping cylinder; its target deformation retracts onto the original target. Put
C = Mf /X and include a fixed source point in every subpair. Given any small subpair, its
quotient image is a small pointed subcomplex of C. Enlarge this image to a small C 0 ∈ A
and lift the noncollapsed cells of C 0 to Mf , including their boundaries and the prescribed
source cells. If Y 0 is the resulting subcomplex and X 0 = Y 0 ∩ X, then

                                          Y 0 /X 0 = C 0 ,

not merely a space mapping to C 0 . Thus X 0 ,→ Y 0 is a small arrow belonging to Sκ up to
its chosen model. Such subarrows are κ-filtered and cofinal among all small subarrows of
X ⊆ Mf . Their colimit, and their homotopy colimit as an arrow, is X ⊆ Mf .
    Every fundamental localizer is closed under homotopy colimits of arrows of spaces after
passage through categories of simplices. This is the relative-Theorem-A argument checked
in A.3, or explicitly the Grothendieck-construction argument of POLES B.2. Hence every
fundamental localizer containing Sκ contains f . It follows that WAκ is exactly the fundamental
localizer generated by the stated small set.
    The use of arbitrary arrows in this generating set matters. Replacing them by the object-
to-point maps would generally produce the proper nullification with the same aspherical
spaces, and would not prove the asserted equality of localizers.

B.3 (Proved). The accessible question has a negative stable-classification answer.
In ZFC, there exists an accessible reversible fundamental localizer that is not R(L) for any
localizing subcategory L of spectra. One example is WNℵ1 .

Proof. The countable CW complex RP ∞ belongs to N by A.4, hence to Nℵ1 by B.1. The
space RP 2 does not belong to N, and so does not belong to its subclass Nℵ1 . B.2 makes
the associated localizer accessible and reversible. A.5 shows that any localizer of the form
R(L) containing the first object-to-point map must contain the second. This contradiction
proves the assertion. The construction uses a fixed regular cardinal and explicit countable
enlargement arguments; no form of Vopěnka’s principle is used.

B.4 (Proved). Reversible closure of a small set is accessible. The smallest reversible
fundamental localizer containing any U-small set of functors is accessible. In particular the
smallest reversible localizer containing RP ∞ → ∗ is accessible and is not RT -fixed.

Proof. Take the intersection W of the reversible fundamental localizers containing the chosen
set S, and let A be its aspherical pointed spaces. Intersections preserve all the required axioms.
The cofiber criterion and forward homotopy-colimit closure imply that A has the hypotheses
of B.1: pointed homotopy colimits of aspherical spaces are aspherical, and the cofiber criterion
supplies all three cofiber implications. Choose an uncountable regular κ ∈ U exceeding the
cell cardinalities of models for the cofibers of every member of S. Then WAκ ⊆ W and it


                                                 9
contains S. It is reversible by B.2, so minimality gives the reverse inclusion. Thus W = WAκ
is accessible.
    For S = {RP ∞ → ∗}, A.3–A.4 give W ⊆ WS and therefore exclude RP 2 → ∗. A.5
puts that map in RT (W ). All intersections may be taken as sets in the next universe; the
resulting generating set in B.2 belongs to U. Equivalently one can define the smallest closure
by well-founded set-sized derivation trees for the localizer and reverse-square rules, which
gives the same separation-based construction in ordinary class notation.


3    The reversible closure of the infinite projective space
Let Wrev denote the smallest reversible fundamental localizer containing BZ/2 → ∗, and
let Arev be its pointed aspherical class. By B.4 it is accessible. The statements below use
QX = Ω∞ Σ∞ X for connected pointed X, and RPk∞ = RP ∞ /RP k−1 for k ≥ 1.
C.1 (Proved). An exact closure description and the R7 operations. The class Arev
is the least weak-equivalence-invariant class containing ∗ and RP ∞ and closed under pointed
homotopy colimits and two out of three in homotopy cofiber sequences. It is closed under
retracts, suspension and desuspension, smash product with an arbitrary pointed space, finite
products, extended powers, and Ωr Σr and Q on connected spaces. Moreover

                        X ∈ Arev     ⇐⇒     Ωr Σr X ∈ Arev       (r ≥ 1).

The converse with Q in place of Ωr Σr is false.

Proof. Let B be the least class specified by the first sentence. A.3 constructs a reversible
localizer with exactly this aspherical class, so minimality gives Arev ⊆ B. Conversely the
cofiber criterion for Wrev supplies the two-out-of-three property, and relative Theorem A
supplies homotopy-colimit closure, as checked in A.3 and B.4. Thus B ⊆ Arev .
    We verify the additional operations rather than assuming them from R7. Retracts follow
from telescopes, and suspension and desuspension from X → ∗ → ΣX. For fixed X in the
class, the functor X ∧ − preserves pointed homotopy colimits. Build an arbitrary pointed
CW complex Y by sphere cells, using X ∧ S n = Σn X; this gives X ∧ Y in the class. The
cofiber sequence X ∨ Y → X × Y → X ∧ Y then gives closure under finite products when
both factors belong to the class.
    If a finite group acts on a space in the class, its pointed homotopy-orbit construction is
a pointed homotopy colimit and remains in the class. More generally F+ ∧G X ∧j belongs
to the class whenever F is a free G-CW complex: filter F by free cells, whose successive
contributions are wedges of suspensions of X ∧j . This includes the extended powers and the
configuration-space quotients in the little-cubes filtration. The free little-r-cubes construction
Cr X has first stage X and successive quotients

                                        Cr (j)+ ∧Σj X ∧j .

Its skeletal filtration consists of cofibrations after well-pointed CW replacement. Two out of
three at each stage and sequential homotopy-colimit closure give Cr X in the class. May’s
approximation theorem, [50, Theorem 6.1], states that its canonical map Cr X → Ωr Σr X is a
weak equivalence for connected X, including r = ∞. This proves all the asserted forward
closures. For the finite-r converse, the adjunction triangle identity makes Σr X a homotopy
retract of Σr Ωr Σr X; apply retract closure and desuspend r times. C.3 below gives the failure
of the Q converse.
                                                                            p
    The fibre-sequence clause of R7 used below also holds: if F → E −      → B is a homotopy
fibre sequence, B is connected, and F is aspherical, then p ∈ Wrev . Express the fibration as

                                               10
the homotopy colimit over the fundamental infinity-groupoid of B of its fibres; the fibrewise
maps to points are all in the localizer, and their homotopy-colimit map is p. Equivalently, a
category-of-simplices model and relative Theorem A prove the same assertion. Two out of
three then gives E aspherical if and only if B is. This argument verifies the specified map,
not just an abstract identification of its fibre type.

C.2 (Proved). Eilenberg–Mac Lane spaces and the sphere’s infinite-loop
component. The spaces K(Z/2, n) belong to Arev for all n ≥ 1, and (Q0 S 0 )(2) belongs to
it. The discrete space K(Z/2, 0) does not.

Proof. The first space is the generator. The bar construction of a connected grouplike space
is the realization of the simplicial diagram of its finite powers. C.1 gives closure under those
powers and their pointed realization, so iterated bars give every K(Z/2, n), n ≥ 1. C.1
also gives QRP ∞ in the class. Its homotopy groups are the positive homotopy groups of
Σ∞ RP ∞ , which is 2-local: multiplication by any odd integer is a homology equivalence of
this connective spectrum and hence a stable equivalence. Thus the space is already 2-local.
The space retraction in the Kahn–Priddy theorem, in precisely the form checked in A.5,
puts (Q0 S 0 )(2) in the class. Finally Arev ⊆ N by A.3 and minimality, and A.2 excludes the
disconnected discrete two-point space.

C.3 (Proved). The stabilized finite Moore spaces become aspherical as spaces.
For every m ≥ 0, Q(Σm RP 2 ) belongs to Arev , although RP 2 does not.

Proof. Set E = τ≥1 S(2) . Its zeroth infinite-loop space is (Q0 S 0 )(2) , which belongs to the class
by C.2. Iterated bars therefore put

                                Zr = Ω∞ Σr E ∈ Arev         (r ≥ 0).
                                                                        2
All these spaces are connected. The cofiber sequence of spectra E −
                                                                  → E → E/2 is also a fibre
sequence, so applying Ω∞ Σr gives a homotopy fibre sequence Zr → Zr → Ω∞ Σr (E/2). Its
base is connected, because E/2 is 1-connective. The fibre-sequence rule proved in C.1 shows
that this base is aspherical.
    Next smash E → S(2) → HZ(2) with S/2. The result is the fibre sequence of spectra
E/2 → S/2 → HZ/2. For every r ≥ 1 its rth infinite-loop spaces give a homotopy fibre
sequence with connected base

                          Ω∞ Σr (E/2) −→ Ω∞ Σr (S/2) −→ K(Z/2, r).

The fibre and base are aspherical by the preceding paragraph and C.2. The same fibre
rule gives the middle term. Since Σ∞ RP 2 ≃ ΣS/2, that term with r = m + 1 is precisely
Q(Σm RP 2 ). Nonmembership of RP 2 follows from Arev ⊆ N and A.4. No inference from
stable membership to unstable membership has been used.

C.4 (Proved). The remaining listed asphericity tests. Among the stunted projective
spaces RPk∞ with k ≥ 1, exactly the case k = 1 is in Arev . No RP 2k , k ≥ 1, belongs to it. For
n ≥ 1, the homotopy fibre of any map S n → K(Z/2, n) does not belong to it; in particular
this holds for the mod-2 fundamental-class map in the question.




                                                 11
Proof. For k = 1 the stunted space is the generator. For k ≥ 2, if the quotient belonged
to the class, the cofiber sequence RP k−1 → RP ∞ → RPk∞ and C.1 would put RP k−1 in
the class. This is impossible. In even positive dimension A.4 supplies its nonzero map to
the corresponding 2-local sphere. In odd dimension d ≥ 3, the same obstruction-theoretic
                           d ] ∼ H d (RP d ; Z ) ∼ Z . For d = 1, Map (S 1 , S 2 ) ≃ ΩS 2 is
calculation gives [RP d , S(2) ∗ =            (2) = (2)                  ∗      (2)       (2)
not weakly contractible. Thus none is in N. If one uses the convention RP0∞ = RP+∞ , that

    Every S n , n ≥ 1, is outside N: for n ≥ 2 its localization map to S(2)n is essential, and

is in the class by C.2. If the fibre in question were in the class, C.1 would put S n there, a
contradiction. This does not require the map’s fibre to be finite or simply connected.

C.5 (Proved). Local spheres and an exact local-object test. Every S(2)            n , n ≥ 2, is

Wrev -local. A space Z is Wrev -local if and only if it inverts the set of small arrows in B.2
for A = Arev and a cardinal containing the generator. Such local spaces are closed under
homotopy limits, retracts, and cotensors; they are HZ-local and BZ/2-null.

Proof. If f : X → Y is in WS , its cofiber and all suspensions of its cofiber are in N. Use the
pointed arrow f+ : X+ → Y+ , whose pointed cofiber is C(f ). The cofiber exact sequence of
                                           j
mapping spaces shows that Map∗ (Σf+ , S(2)    ) is an equivalence for every j ≥ 2: its source and
target are grouplike loop spaces, the fibre is null, and the next cofiber term gives surjectivity
                                    j
on components. Hence Map(f, ΩS(2)     ) is an equivalence. Apply the unbased mapping-space
functor to the EHP fibre sequence of A.1 and compare the two domains X, Y . Homotopy
limits preserve these equivalences, proving Map(f, S(2)    n ) an equivalence. This argument

includes empty and disconnected domains without choosing a preferred component. Thus
the spheres are WS -local and therefore Wrev -local.
    The asserted set of arrows generates the localizer by B.2 and B.4. Mapping into Z detects
a fundamental localizer, so inverting the generators is necessary and sufficient. Mapping
spaces commute with limits in their target, giving homotopy-limit and retract closure; the
tensor–cotensor adjunction gives cotensors. Every reversible fundamental localizer contains
WZ , as checked by POLES B.6 and again by the literal-axiom argument in F.2, so every one
of its local objects is HZ-local. The defining generator gives BZ/2-nullity. Nullity alone is
not substituted for the stated map-locality test.

C.6 (Proved). The finite-target locality question. The 2-completion of every simply
connected finite CW complex is Wrev -local. The same is true of its 2-localization. The complete
proof is C.8–C.9 below.
    The initial resolving-class approach alone did not establish this assertion. The theorem of
Strom [63, Theorem 8 and Corollaries 9 and 11] places sufficiently iterated loop spaces of
finite complexes in a resolving class generated by spheres and gives an unlooped conclusion
in a strong resolving class. Local objects are not automatically closed under extensions
by arbitrary fibrations. C.8 instead uses the strong resolving class defined by nullity of
a fixed source, and C.9 recovers actual locality by finite smash powers and a convergent
loop–suspension resolution. The distinction between nullity and locality is retained at every
step.
C.7 (Refuted). Finite fundamental group and bounded cohomology do not suffice.
A connected space with finite fundamental group and bounded integral cohomology need not be
Wrev -local, even when it is a finite two-dimensional complex and is BZ/2-null. The finite
category witnessing this assertion has 35 objects and 159 arrows.


                                               12
Proof. Let P be the presentation complex

                                       ha, b | a5 b−3 , b3 (ab)−2 i.

The two cellular boundary columns in dimensions two and one are (5, −3) and (−2, 1). Their
determinant is −1, so P is connected and integrally acyclic. Its fundamental group is the
binary icosahedral group SL2 (F5 ) of order 120; the same presentation and order are recorded
in Hatcher [37, Example 2.38, p. 142]. We also verify the group identification independently
below. In particular P is not weakly contractible, while its integral cohomology vanishes in
all positive degrees.
    Every homology equivalence belongs to Wrev . Hence P → ∗ belongs to it. If P were
local, applying its mapping-space test to this arrow would make P weakly contractible:
more explicitly, the identity of P would be homotopic to a constant map, contradicting
its nontrivial fundamental group. Miller’s Theorem A nevertheless makes Map∗ (BZ/2, P )
weakly contractible, since P is finite dimensional. Thus neither generator-nullity nor the
additional fundamental-group conclusion of R11 repairs the proposed locality assertion.
    Here are the complete conventions for the finite witness, so that its verification does not
depend on accepting PRETOT. Write the two words as w0 = aaaaaBBB and w1 = bbbBABA,
with capitals denoting inverses. Take vertices v, c0 , c1 , loop edges a, b at v, and radial edges
rki from ck to v, indexed cyclically by letters of wk . For the triangle tki at a positive letter e,
set its ordered faces to (e, rk,i+1 , rki ); at a negative letter set them to (e, rki , rk,i+1 ). All these
formulas satisfy the semisimplicial identities. Their realization is the specified presentation
complex, by contracting one radial edge for each relation disk. Let P be its face-occurrence
category: objects are simplices, and an arrow to a d-simplex is an increasing injection into
[d] with the specified source face. Composition is composition of injections. The nerve is its
barycentric subdivision and has the same realization.
    The program CD_finite_checks.py constructs every object, arrow and composite, checks
all units and associativity triples, and computes the integral boundary matrices and Smith
normal forms. It obtains 35 objects, 159 arrows and integral homology ranks (1, 0, 0) with no
torsion. It also computes the complete Todd–Coxeter coset table of the displayed presentation
over the trivial subgroup, obtaining order 120. Finally it enumerates all determinant-one 2 by
2 matrices over F5 , finds matrices satisfying the two relations and generating all 120 elements,
and thereby checks that the presented group maps isomorphically onto SL2 (F5 ). The exact
tables, matrices and assertions are saved in code/outputs/CD_finite_checks.json.
    This confirms the particular PRETOT A.4/A.5 finite construction used here. Its
stronger identification with a punctured Poincaré homology sphere is unnecessary for the
counterexample, and no such identification has been assumed.

C.8 (Proved). Looped finite targets, and an unlooped H-space case. For every
simply connected finite CW complex K and every X ∈ Arev ,

                                Map∗ (X, K(2) ) ≃ ∗ ≃ Map∗ (X, K2∧ ).

The loop space Ω(K2∧ ) is Wrev -local. Moreover, if K is a connected finite H-space, then K2∧
itself is Wrev -local.

Proof. Fix X ∈ Arev ⊆ N, and write MX = {Z : Map∗ (X, Z) ≃ ∗}. This is a strong resolving
class: mapping spaces preserve homotopy limits, and a fibration with null base and null
fibre has null total mapping space. We apply the published Theorem 10 of Strom [63] to the
collection
                                           j
                                     E = {S(2) : j ≥ 1}.

                                                    13
Suspension and smash products preserve this collection up to weak equivalence, by [51,
Propositions 6.4.2 and 6.4.6]. Its suspensions belong to MX by the defining sphere tests.
Strom’s theorem therefore puts in MX every nilpotent space having finite cone length with
attaching spaces finite-type wedges of members of E.
    For completeness, the required finite cone-length assertion can be checked without choosing
a finite model of K with no 1-cells. Collapse a maximal tree so that the connected finite
CW complex K has one 0-cell. For each finite m, the space Vm = m               2
                                                                        W
                                                                          i=1 Σ K has a finite cell
decomposition with all nonbasepoint cells of dimension at least three. Its skeletal cofiber
sequences have simply connected terms and finite wedges of spheres of dimension at least
two as attaching spaces. Localizing these sequences at 2 gives cofiber sequences by [51,
Propositions 6.4.1 and 6.4.4]. It follows that (Vm )(2) has the finite cone length required above
and hence belongs to MX . The same source’s suspension and wedge comparisons give
                                                 m

                                    (Vm )(2) ≃         Σ2 (K(2) ).
                                                 i=1

Strom’s Proposition 13, with N = 2 and the nilpotent space K(2) , now implies K(2) ∈ MX .
This use of a strong resolving class concerns nullity only, and does not assert that arbitrary
local objects form such a class.
    Next X is rationally acyclic. Indeed the pointed class of rationally acyclic spaces contains
BZ/2 and is closed under pointed homotopy colimits and two out of three for cofiber sequences,
so C.1 puts all of Arev in it. The arithmetic square
                                                        h
                                      K(2) ≃ KQ × K2∧
                                                   (K2∧ )Q

is valid here. One can check it directly rather than assume a completion theorem for arbitrary
inverse limits: the homotopy groups of K are finitely generated by [51, Theorem 4.5.2];
localization and completion identify the other groups by Theorems 6.1.2 and 11.1.2 of that
source. For every finitely generated abelian group A, the exact sequence

                  0 −→ A ⊗ Z(2) −→ (A ⊗ Q) ⊕ (A ⊗ Z2 ) −→ A ⊗ Q2 −→ 0

follows from Q + Z2 = Q2 , Q ∩ Z2 = Z(2) , and flatness of Q2 . The homotopy-pullback
long exact sequence therefore identifies every homotopy group with that of K(2) , including
simple connectedness of the pullback. This proves the displayed square by Whitehead’s
theorem. Both rational terms are rational-homology-local, as follows either from their rational
Postnikov towers or from Theorem 6.1.1 of the same source. Mapping X into them gives
contractible spaces. Applying Map∗ (X, −) to the square thus identifies Map∗ (X, K2∧ ) with
Map∗ (X, K(2) ), proving the second nullity assertion.
    For an unpointed map f : A → B in Wrev , its based cofiber C(f ) lies in Arev . The rotated
cofiber sequence gives

              Map∗ (ΣB+ , K2∧ ) −→ Map∗ (ΣA+ , K2∧ ) −→ Map∗ (C(f ), K2∧ ) ≃ ∗.

Hence the first arrow is an equivalence on all components. By adjunction this is precisely
locality of Ω(K2∧ ).
    Finally let K be a connected finite H-space. Its multiplication may be chosen to have
a strict two-sided unit. Multiplying a finite word in a fixed left-associated order gives a
continuous map J(K) → K from the James construction: deleting any basepoint letter
leaves the value unchanged. Its restriction to the one-letter stage is the identity. The James
equivalence J(K) ≃ ΩΣK for connected K therefore makes K a homotopy retract of ΩΣK.

                                                 14
No associativity of its multiplication is needed for this space-level retraction. Completion
preserves the two maps and their retraction identity; [51, Proposition 11.2.4] gives

                    K2∧   a homotopy retract of             (ΩΣK)∧         ∧
                                                                 2 ≃ Ω((ΣK)2 ).

The suspension ΣK is a simply connected finite complex, so the last loop space is local by
the preceding paragraph. Closure under retracts proves that K2∧ is local. This settles the
unlooped assertion for finite H-spaces directly. The next argument removes the H-space
restriction for simply connected finite targets.

C.9 (Proved). Finite simply connected targets are local after localization or
completion. For every simply connected finite CW complex K, both K(2) and K2∧ are
Wrev -local. In particular the simply connected finite-target question recorded in C.6 has an
affirmative answer.

Proof. We first prove a nullity statement for finite wedges of smash powers, then use a
convergent loop–suspension resolution. This avoids interchanging completion with an infinite
totalization.
    Put Z = K(2) or Z = K2∧ . For positive integers d1 , . . . , ds , let
                                  s                            s

                                  _                            _

                                  j=1                         j=1

Thus F is a simply connected finite complex. We claim that

                           Map∗ (X, Y ) ≃ ∗        for every X ∈ Arev .                    (1)

If Z = K(2) , the localization comparisons for suspension, finite wedges and smash products
give Y ≃ F(2) , so this is C.8. Suppose instead that Z = K2∧ . The space Y is simply
connected and 2-local, by [51, Propositions 6.4.1, 6.4.2 and 6.4.6]. The map F → Y induced
by completion is a mod-2 homology equivalence: completion of the nilpotent space K has
this property, and smash products and finite wedges preserve it, by the Künneth theorem
over F2 . Consequently

Here completion inverts the indicated homology equivalence between simply connected spaces,
by the characterization in [51, Theorems 11.1.1–11.1.2].

                                                        h

                                                   (Y2∧ )Q

The theorem applies to all nilpotent 2-local spaces and explicitly does not require finite

acyclic and Map∗ (X, F2∧ ) ≃ ∗. Both rational vertices are rational-homology-local. Mapping

    The same rotated-cofiber argument used in C.8 now shows that ΩY is Wrev -local. Explicitly,
if f : A → B belongs to Wrev , then C(f ) ∈ Arev , and the fibre sequence

                Map∗ (ΣB+ , Y ) −→ Map∗ (ΣA+ , Y ) −→ Map∗ (C(f ), Y ) ≃ ∗

proves locality of ΩY on all mapping-space components.

    Let Yr,M be the finite wedge on the right of (3) with m ≤ M , and let the maps Yr,M +1 →

                                     ΣT r Z ≃ holim Yr,M .

Every ΩYr,M is local by (1) and cofiber rotation. Since loops and mapping spaces preserve

                                    T r+1 Z ≃ holim ΩYr,M
                                                  M

is local for every r ≥ 0.
     Finally, the loop–suspension completion theorem gives, for any simply connected pointed
space Z, the coaugmented cosimplicial resolution

                                      Z ≃ holim T q+1 Z.
                                              [q]∈∆

We use the derived resolution, so the displayed powers specify its terms up to weak equivalence.
Bousfield constructs it in [18, §7.9] and identifies its totalization with integral Bousfield–Kan
completion in §9.9; on simply connected spaces the coaugmentation is a weak equivalence.
The same precise conclusion, without finiteness hypotheses, is stated explicitly in [9, Theorem
1.8(b)]. Both choices of Z above are simply connected. All terms of this resolution are local
by the preceding paragraph, and local objects are closed under homotopy limits. Hence Z is
local, proving both assertions. In particular this last step uses an ordinary loop–suspension
resolution of the already completed space when Z = K2∧ ; it assumes no commutation of
suspension or totalization with completion.

                                                16
4    Classification: corrections and realization results

D.0 (Refuted). Desuspension-closed nullity does not characterize a localization.
Even for an accessible reflective class Z having all the closure properties of local spaces,
desuspension closure of {X : Map∗ (X, Z) ≃ ∗ for every Z ∈ Z} does not imply reversibility
of its associated localizer. The requested classification needs an additional condition on maps,
not only this condition on null objects.

Proof. Let S be representatives of all countable connected integrally acyclic pointed CW
complexes, and let P be nullification at the set of maps A → ∗, A ∈ S. Existence is the
usual set-generated nullification theorem, in the precise form of Bousfield [14, Theorem 1.1];
alternatively the set of category-of-simplices maps defines the proper localizer in Cisinski [30,
Theorem 6.1.11]. Let Z be its full class of local pointed spaces.
     Every connected acyclic CW complex is a filtered pointed homotopy colimit of countable
acyclic subcomplexes. To see this, start with any countable subcomplex. Its homology has
countably many classes; choose in the ambient acyclic complex a finite cellular filling for each
class, add the finitely many cells of each filling, and repeat countably. The union is countable
and acyclic. Starting with the union of countably many prescribed subcomplexes proves
filteredness. Thus P kills every acyclic space. Conversely every nullification generator is an
integral homology equivalence, so its generated localizer is contained in WZ and it kills only
nonempty acyclic spaces. Consequently the null class of Z is exactly the integral-acyclic class,
which is closed under desuspension by the suspension isomorphism in reduced homology. As
a set-generated local-object class, Z is reflective and closed under homotopy limits, retracts
and cotensors, and under sufficiently filtered homotopy colimits.
     Nevertheless its localizer is strictly smaller than WZ . An explicit detecting local object is
BΣ3 . A map from a connected acyclic space to this 1-type is classified by a homomorphism
from its perfect fundamental group to Σ3 . Every subgroup of Σ3 is solvable, so every such
homomorphism is trivial. The based mapping space into a 1-type from a connected source
has contractible components; hence BΣ3 is P -local.
     Let T be the presentation 2-complex with one vertex, loops a, b, and one 2-cell attached
by aba(bab)−1 . Let u : T → S 1 send both loops once around the circle; its attaching word
has total exponent zero, so it extends. The cellular boundary is the column (1, −1)t . Thus
H1 (T ; Z) = Z, H2 (T ; Z) = 0, and u is an integral homology equivalence. Its homotopy cofiber
is connected and acyclic. But the homomorphism
                         a 7−→ (12),      b 7−→ (23) from π1 T to Σ3
satisfies the braid relation and is nonabelian. It cannot factor through π1 u : π1 T → Z,
even up to conjugacy. Hence Map(u, BΣ3 ) is not surjective on components and u is not
a P -equivalence. Its cofiber is P -aspherical, so the cofiber criterion fails. This proves the
assertion directly; it also exhibits the distinction underlying the proper-aspherical shadow
of Cisinski’s Theorem 6.1.11. The finite relation and boundary calculations are checked in
code/CD_finite_checks.py.

D.1.1 (Proved). The exact single-target test and the incorrect infinite-loop lead.
For a pointed space Z, its null class is closed under desuspension if and only if
                      {X : Map∗ (X, ΩZ) ≃ ∗} ⊆ {X : Map∗ (X, Z) ≃ ∗}.                      (D.1.1)
Equivalently there is no pointed X whose mapping space into Z has weakly contractible
constant component and has another component. If Z is in the resolving class generated by
ΩZ, (D.1.1) holds. The suggestion that every infinite loop space satisfies it is false.


                                                17
Proof. The adjunction Map∗ (ΣX, Z) ≃ Map∗ (X, ΩZ) proves the equivalence with
desuspension closure. The latter mapping space is the loop space at the constant
map of Map∗ (X, Z), so weak contractibility means precisely that its constant component is
weakly contractible. A pointed mapping-space functor preserves homotopy limits, proving the
resolving-class sufficient condition. For the counterexample take Z = K(Z, n) and X = S n ,
n ≥ 1. Then Map∗ (ΣX, Z) is weakly contractible, while π0 Map∗ (X, Z) = Z. These Z are
infinite loop spaces. The loss of components in passing to a loop space is the exact error in
the proposed sufficient condition.

D.1.2 (Refuted). A single integral or localized sphere does not have the property.
For every n ≥ 1 and every prime p, the null classes of the individual targets S n and S(p)
                                                                                       n fail

desuspension closure. This does not contradict the family statement A.1.

Proof. For n = 1, take X = S 1 and use S(p)   1 = K(Z , 1). Assume n ≥ 2 and put d = n
                                                        (p)
when n is odd and d = 2n − 1 when n is even. Serre’s finiteness theorem, opened in Hatcher
[36, Theorem 1.21], says that πd S n is Z plus a finite group, all groups above degree d are
finite, and the only possible additional infinite group is πn S n = Z.
    For the localized target put
                                           p        p
                           X = Tel S d−1 −
                                         → S d−1 −
                                                 → S d−1 −→ · · · .
                                                                    


The mapping space is the homotopy limit of the tower Ωd−1 S(p) n with bonding maps induced

by degree p. Its homotopy-group towers are multiplication-by-p towers. On each finite p-group
these towers are pro-zero, so both limit and derived limit vanish. On Z(p) , the limit vanishes
and
                                      p      p
                           lim 1 Z(p) ←
                                                    
                                      − Z(p) ←
                                             − · · · = Zp /Z(p) 6= 0.
For clarity, this formula follows from the difference map (xi ) 7→ (xi − pxi+1 ) on the product:
summing pi ai in Zp identifies its cokernel with the displayed quotient; changing a preimage
changes the sum by an element of Z(p) .
    The homotopy-limit exact sequence therefore makes every positive homotopy group of
            n ) vanish, while its components contain, and in fact equal, this nonzero derived
Map∗ (X, S(p)

that conclusion. Here d − 1 ≥ 2, so the mapping tower consists of double-loop spaces and the
component calculation is a group calculation. Consequently its loop space Map∗ (ΣX, S(p)   n ) is

weakly contractible, but the original mapping space is not.
    For the integral target use the telescope whose successive degrees are 2, 3, 4, . . .. Every
finite homotopy-group tower is now pro-zero. The corresponding derived limit of the integral
tower is Z/Z
          b    6= 0, since the products of the bonding degrees are cofinal among the positive
integers under divisibility. The identical argument gives the integral counterexample. These
are countable CW complexes and actual phantom-component witnesses, not merely formal
failures to infer components from loops.

D.1.3 (Proved). The group targets. For any group G, including every nilpotent group,
the null class of K(G, 1) is desuspension-closed if and only if G = 1.

Proof. If G 6= 1, take X = S 1 . The based mapping space into K(G, 1) is the discrete group
G, up to weak equivalence, whereas the mapping space from ΣS 1 = S 2 is weakly contractible.
For G = 1 the null class consists of all pointed spaces, so the property holds.

D.1.4 (Proved). The target BU . The null class of BU is closed under desuspension.


                                               18
Proof. Bott periodicity gives Ω2 BU ≃ Z × BU , with the base component identified with BU .
The opened statement [39, Theorem 2.11] says that external multiplication by the Bott class
induces the double-suspension isomorphism in complex K-theory. Its representing Bott map
is consequently an isomorphism on every homotopy group of the indicated representing spaces,
and Whitehead’s theorem gives the space equivalence just used. Suppose Map∗ (ΣX, BU ) is
weakly contractible. Its loop space gives Map∗ (X, Ω2 BU ) ≃ ∗. If X had a component other
than its base component, the discrete factor Z would already give nonconstant based maps
to Z × BU , a contradiction. Thus X is connected. Every based map from X to the discrete
factor is zero, so Map∗ (X, Ω2 BU ) ≃ Map∗ (X, BU ). This proves the assertion. The use of
periodicity, rather than infinite-loop structure alone, is essential.

D.1.5 (Proved). The Moore-space classification. For prime p and n ≥ 1, the single-
target null class of the usual Moore space M (Z/p, n) is desuspension-closed except when
(p, n) = (2, 1). The simply connected odd-primary and mod-2 cases are proved in D.1.7 and
D.1.8, respectively; the fundamental-group cases, including the explicit RP 2 counterexample,
are proved in D.1.9. The finite-H-space question is completely decided in D.1.6.
    The telescope proof for spheres does not transfer to simply connected finite Moore targets:
their homotopy groups are finite p-groups, so it produces no nonzero lim1 component. The
affirmative Moore results below instead use explicit loop-space retractions, collection-level
finite-wedge constructions, and a convergent simply connected resolution.
D.1.6 (Proved). The complete finite-H-space test. Let Z be an H-space having the
homotopy type of a finite CW complex. Its single-target null class

                                    {X : Map∗ (X, Z) ≃ ∗}

is closed under desuspension if and only if Z is weakly contractible. Homotopy associativity
or commutativity of the multiplication is not assumed. The assertion includes disconnected
H-spaces, with the unit as basepoint.

Proof. We first suppose that Z is connected. A connected H-space is simple: its fundamental
group is abelian, and the action on its higher homotopy groups is trivial. Both assertions
follow by comparing concatenation with the multiplication, using the unit. The finite-type
theorem for nilpotent spaces [52, Theorem 4.5.2] then implies that every πi Z and every
integral homology group of the universal cover Ze is finitely generated. The rational H-space
theorem says that Z has the rational homotopy type of a finite product of odd-dimensional
spheres [52, Theorem 9.1.1 and Corollary 9.1.2]. This statement applies to H-spaces without
homotopy associativity: the source explicitly uses quasi-Hopf algebras for that reason.
    Suppose first that some rational homotopy group in degree at least two is nonzero, and
let d be its highest degree. Then d is odd and d ≥ 3, and

                             πd Z ∼
                                  = Za ⊕ T,          a > 0,   T finite.

All πj Z with j > d, as well as πd−1 Z, are finite. Define the countable pointed CW complex
                                            2          3        4
                           X = Tel S d−1 −
                                         → S d−1 −
                                                 → S d−1 −
                                                                      
                                                         → ··· .

Mapping out of this homotopy colimit identifies Map∗ (X, Z) with the homotopy limit of the
tower Ωd−1 Z whose maps on homotopy groups are successive multiplication by 2, 3, 4, . . ..
For a finite group in this tower the composites eventually vanish. Its inverse limit and its first
derived inverse limit are therefore zero. For the free group Za , the inverse limit is zero and
                                  2    3    4
                         lim 1 Za ←
                                  − Za ←    − ··· ∼
                                       − Za ←             a
                                                 
                                                   = (Z/Z)
                                                      b     6= 0.                       (D.1.6.1)

                                                19
Here Zb is the profinite completion. To see the formula, write b0 = 1 and bi = i−1 (j + 2), so
                                                                                   Q
                                                                                j=0
the subgroups bi Z are cofinal among all finite-index subgroups of Z. For the difference map
on the product, D(x)i = xi − (i + 2)xi+1 , the convergent profinite series i≥0 bi yi identifies
                                                                           P

its cokernel with Z/Z.
                    b     Telescoping shows that D(x) maps to x0 modulo Z; conversely the
divisibility of the successive remainders constructs a preimage whenever the series is integral.
The same calculation in each coordinate gives (D.1.6.1).
    The homotopy-limit exact sequences now give
                 πj Map∗ (X, Z) = 0      (j ≥ 1),     π0 Map∗ (X, Z) ∼
                                                                     = (Z/Z)
                                                                        b    a
                                                                               .
In the second formula the ordinary inverse limit of πd−1 Z is zero; the finite summand of
πd Z contributes zero derived limit. For j ≥ 1, the derived-limit term comes from πd+j Z,
hence vanishes, and the ordinary-limit term vanishes even when it comes from πd Z. Since
d − 1 ≥ 2, these are the usual group-valued loop-space exact sequences. Consequently
Map∗ (ΣX, Z) ≃ Ω Map∗ (X, Z) is weakly contractible, while Map∗ (X, Z) is not. This is the
required counterexample in the first case.
     It remains to handle the possibility that the only rational homotopy occurs in degree one.
The universal cover Ze inherits an H-space multiplication by lifting the multiplication of Z;
the unit homotopies lift as well. Its homotopy groups are torsion, so it is rationally acyclic.
Moreover its lifted CW structure has the same finite dimension as a finite CW model of Z.
Combining this bound with the finite generation already proved, its integral homology is
finitely generated in total.
    We use exactly Browder’s Corollary 7.2 [21, p. 47]: for a connected H-space with each
integral homology group finitely generated and homology zero above degree m, if Hm is
nonzero then Hm ∼      = Z. Its hypotheses require bounded finitely generated homology, not a
finite CW complex or a finite-sheeted covering. If Ze had any nonzero positive homology,
its highest such group would therefore contain nonzero rational homology. This contradicts
rational acyclicity. Thus Ze is integrally acyclic and simply connected, and is contractible by
the Hurewicz and Whitehead theorems.
     It follows that Z ≃ K(G, 1) for the finitely generated abelian group G = π1 Z. This group
is torsion-free. Indeed, if it contained a subgroup Cp , the corresponding covering of the
finite-dimensional CW model of Z would be a finite-dimensional K(Cp , 1). This contradicts
H j (Cp ; Fp ) 6= 0 in every degree j ≥ 0, obtained directly from the two-periodic cyclic resolution:
its two differentials are zero with trivial Fp coefficients. Hence G = ∼ Zr and Z is a torus up to
                                           1
homotopy. If r > 0, the space X = S has Map∗ (ΣX, Z) ≃ ∗, whereas Map∗ (X, Z) ≃ G is a
nontrivial discrete space. If r = 0, then Z is contractible. This finishes the connected case.
     Finally let Z have several components and let Ze be its identity component. This is
a finite connected H-space. If it is noncontractible, the preceding counterexamples have
connected source X, so both mapping spaces into Z are the same as the corresponding
based mapping spaces into Ze . If Ze is contractible but Z is disconnected, take X = S 0 :
then Map∗ (ΣX, Z) = ΩZe ≃ ∗ and Map∗ (X, Z) = Z is not weakly contractible. In the
remaining case Z itself is weakly contractible and every pointed mapping space into it is
weakly contractible, so the desired closure holds.

D.1.7 (Proved). The simply connected odd-primary Moore targets. Let p be odd
and let n ≥ 2. For the usual Moore space M (Z/p, n) = S n ∪p en+1 , its single-target null class
is closed under desuspension. There is no finiteness hypothesis on the source. More precisely,
for any d ≥ 3 and any pointed space X,
       Map∗ (X, ΩP d (p)) ≃ ∗     =⇒      Map∗ (X, P e (p)) ≃ ∗   for every e ≥ 3,         (D.1.7.1)
where P e (p) = S e−1 ∪p ee is indexed by its top cell.


                                                 20
Proof. Fix X and write
                                  RX = {Y : Map∗ (X, Y ) ≃ ∗}.
This class is closed under homotopy limits, loops and homotopy retracts. The proof has three
steps: obtain all sufficiently high looped Moore targets; use a convergent loop–suspension
resolution to remove their loops; and use finite cone cubes to lower their dimensions. All the
closure statements concern the whole based mapping space, including its set of components.
The precise retraction input and the bottom-dimensional case. For odd p the smash
splitting is
                  P a (p) ∧ P b (p) ≃ P a+b (p) ∨ P a+b−1 (p), a, b ≥ 2. (D.1.7.2)
It is the splitting used in the proof of [34, Lemma 1.5, pp. 13–14]; the hypotheses and formula
are also explicitly recorded in [19, Lemma 4.5]. We use two retraction theorems from the
original CMN paper:
                                                  _
                   ΩP 2a+2 (p) ≃ S 2a+1 {p} × Ω         P 4a+2ja+3 (p),    a ≥ 1,         (D.1.7.3)
                                                  j≥0

and the assertion that a mod-p homology monomorphism between loop spaces of bouquets of
simply connected mod-p Moore spaces has a left homotopy inverse. These are respectively
[34, Proposition 1.1, p. 9, and Proposition 1.7, p. 15]. The symbol S 2a+1 {p} denotes the
fibre of the degree-p map on the p-local sphere; only the Moore-bouquet factor will be used.
CMN’s retractions are p-local statements. Every simply connected finite mod-p Moore space,
and every finite wedge or smash of these spaces, has finite p-primary homotopy groups by
the Serre-class theorem. These spaces are therefore already p-local. The retractions used
here consequently apply to their ordinary homotopy types. The displayed range a ≥ 1 was
checked on the printed page of Proposition 1.1, including its endpoint.
    For clarity we do not use CMN’s odd-dimensional Corollary 1.9 outside its printed
range a ≥ 2. In particular the case P 3 (p) is established as follows. Let d ≥ 3 be odd, set
A = P d−1 (p), and let
                             [id, id] : Σ(A ∧ A) −→ ΣA = P d (p)
be the generalized Whitehead product. The lower Moore summand in (D.1.7.2), before
suspension, gives a map j : P 2d−3 (p) → A ∧ A. Put

                             h = [id, id] ◦ Σj : P 2d−2 (p) −→ P d (p).

We check that Ωh is a mod-p homology monomorphism. Write

             H∗ (ΩP d (p); Fp ) = T (u, v),    |u| = d − 2,      |v| = d − 1,   βv = u.

This tensor-algebra formula is the James theorem for the connected space A. The adjoint
Whitehead product has the graded commutator as its homology map. Since |u| is odd and |v|
is even, the two generators of H∗ (ΩP 2d−2 (p); Fp ) have images, after choosing the compatible
Moore orientations,
                                      2u2 ,      vu − uv.                              (D.1.7.4)
Here is a check that the second formula is independent of the chosen complementary summand.
Normalize the lower generator of j to u⊗u. Its upper generator then maps to c(v ⊗u)+b(u⊗v)
with c − b = 1, since β(v ⊗ u) = u ⊗ u and β(u ⊗ v) = −u ⊗ u. The commutator sends this
combination to (c − b)(vu − uv). It sends u ⊗ u to 2u2 . Thus the two images also satisfy the
required Bockstein relation.
   These images freely generate a tensor subalgebra. Indeed, with the lexicographic order
v > u, their leading words are uu and vu, respectively, with nonzero coefficients. Both words

                                                21
have length two. A concatenation of them has a unique division into two-letter blocks, so
distinct monomials in the two proposed generators have distinct leading words. This proves
injectivity. CMN Proposition 1.7 therefore makes ΩP 2d−2 (p) a homotopy retract of ΩP d (p).
Its source and target are loop spaces of simply connected Moore spaces even when d = 3; the
proposition has no additional connectivity restriction here. Consequently an even-dimensional
looped Moore space belongs to RX whenever the original ΩP d (p) does.
All sufficiently high looped Moore spaces. Apply (D.1.7.3) to that even-dimensional
Moore space, and retain two bouquet summands. For some integers a1 , a2 ≥ 3 we obtain

                                  Ω(P a1 (p) ∨ P a2 (p)) ∈ RX .                        (D.1.7.5)

For example the first two summands of (D.1.7.3) have top dimensions 4a + 3 and 6a + 3. The
Hilton–Milnor factor indexed by the basic commutator with k occurrences of the first letter
and one of the second is

                           ΩΣ P a1 −1 (p)∧k ∧ P a2 −1 (p) ,
                                                           
                                                                  k ≥ 1.

It is a homotopy retract of (D.1.7.5). Repeated use of (D.1.7.2) makes every ΩP D (p) with

                            k(a1 − 2) + a2 ≤ D ≤ k(a1 − 1) + a2                        (D.1.7.6)

a retract of this factor. Each integer in the interval occurs: the smash of k + 1 Moore spaces
has a Moore summand with each of the k + 1 possible dimension reductions 0, 1, . . . , k. The
lower endpoint for k + 1 minus the upper endpoint for k is a1 − 2 − k. Thus the integer
intervals overlap or are adjacent for all sufficiently large k, and their union contains every
integer above some bound N . We have proved

                                  ΩP D (p) ∈ RX      (D ≥ N ).                         (D.1.7.7)

The Hilton–Milnor theorem and its Moore-smash application used here are also given in [34,
Proposition 2.2 and its proof, pp. 18–19]; the interval calculation is the extra step which gives
every sufficiently large dimension.
    We will need a slightly stronger consequence. If W is a finite wedge of Moore spaces
of top dimension at least N , every Hilton–Milnor factor of ΩW splits into looped Moore
spaces of top dimension at least N . A word of length q has minimum possible top dimension
Pq
  i=1 ei − 2q + 2 ≥ q(N − 2) + 2 ≥ N . The factors’ connectivities increase with word length,
so the weak product supplied by Hilton–Milnor is weakly equivalent to the ordinary product.
Products and retracts are allowed in RX , proving ΩW ∈ RX . The same conclusion holds
for a finite-type wedge: truncate it by top dimension, use the projections that collapse the
tails, and take the homotopy limit. In each fixed homotopy degree this tower is eventually
constant, so its homotopy limit is the original wedge. This argument uses target connectivity,
not compactness of X.
Removing loops and lowering dimensions. Let K be any finite wedge of Moore spaces
of top dimension at least N , and put T = ΩΣ. The iterated James splitting from C.9 gives

                                        _ c_
                                           r,m

                             ΣT r K ≃            ΣK ∧m ,       r ≥ 0,                  (D.1.7.8)
                                        m≥1

where each multiplicity cr,m is finite. To recall why, ΣT V ≃ q≥1 ΣV ∧q ; distributing smash
                                                                   W

products and moving the single suspension between factors gives the induction. At a fixed
weight m there are only finitely many ordered decompositions of m into positive weights, so
all the multiplicities remain finite. By (D.1.7.2), the right side of (D.1.7.8) is a finite-type

                                               22
wedge of Moore spaces of top dimension at least N + 1. Its loop space therefore belongs to
RX by (D.1.7.7) and the preceding paragraph. Thus T r+1 K ∈ RX for every r ≥ 0.
   For every simply connected pointed space the derived loop–suspension resolution converges:

                                             K ≃ holim T q+1 K.                                        (D.1.7.9)
                                                     [q]∈∆

This is exactly [9, Theorem 1.8(b)], with r = 1 in that paper, or [18, Sections 7.9 and 9.9].
There is no finite-type hypothesis on the mapping source. Closure under homotopy limits
proves that RX contains every such finite wedge K.
    Finally we spell out the desuspension mechanism, so that this step does not assume the
property being proved. If V is simply connected and every finite wedge of ΣV lies in a
homotopy-limit-closed class R, form the strongly homotopy cocartesian s-cube generated by
s copies of V → CV . Each noninitial vertex is a finite wedge of ΣV , including the empty
wedge. Let Ys be the homotopy limit of the punctured cube. Then Ys ∈ R. The higher
Blakers–Massey theorem makes the comparison V → Ys increasingly connected as s increases.
The cone cubes have compatible projection maps, and hence V ≃ holims Ys ∈ R. This is the
Barratt argument in [64, Theorem 8, p. 7].
    Apply this argument first to every finite wedge of P N −1 (p), whose suspension is a finite
wedge of P N (p). It puts all these wedges in RX . Repeat, one dimension at a time, down
to top dimension three. Together with the already proved high-dimensional assertion, this
proves (D.1.7.1). Taking e = d = n + 1 and using the suspension–loop adjunction proves the
claim.

   The odd-prime hypothesis is used both in (D.1.7.2) and in the nonzero coefficient 2 in
(D.1.7.4). Neither step gives the mod-2 Moore assertion by substitution.
