From the matrix to the wedge retraction. Being a retract of a suspension, L has a
comultiplication
                     ∆L = (p11 ∨ p11 )∆ΣA∧11 i11 : L → L ∨ L
whose two projections are homotopic to the identity. Thus the two projections in (D.1.8.6)
give one actual map to a wedge:

              R = (r1 ∨ r2 )∆L : L −→ B ∨ B,                 J = j1 ∨ j2 : B ∨ B −→ L.

By (D.1.8.7), RJ is a mod-2 homology equivalence. It is a homotopy equivalence: its
mapping cone is simply connected, has finite 2-primary integral homology, and has zero
mod-2 homology, hence zero integral homology. Replacing R by (RJ)−1 R proves that B ∨ B
is a homotopy retract of L. Looping, and using the Wu retraction above, proves (D.1.8.1).
    For d = 3 use the explicit Moore-space retraction

                                       ΩP 6 (2)    of     ΩP 3 (2).                      (D.1.8.10)

It is the case n = 2, k = 1 of the formula ΩP n+1+k(2n−1) (2) → ΩP n+1 (2) stated in [27,
proof of Corollary 1.4, p. 3258], where the printed range is n ≥ 2, k ≥ 1. We only need
this endpoint, not the broader prime-indexed product formula in that paper’s Theorem 1.3.
Applying (D.1.8.1) with d = 6 then supplies the required two equal summands. This use of
the paper’s explicit P 3 (2) application does not identify an odd-degree telescope of RP 2 with
a CW complex having finitely many ordinary cells in each degree; that identification would
be false, since the telescope has π2 = Z(2) .
Why two equal summands suffice. Suppose now ΩP d (2) ∈ RX , where d ≥ 3. We have
just obtained an integer D > d with

                                    Ω(P D (2) ∨ P D (2)) ∈ RX .

Put K = P D−1 (2). The Hilton–Milnor word containing q − 1 copies of the first letter and one
copy of the second makes ΩΣK ∧q a retract for every q ≥ 2; the case q = 1 is either original
wedge summand. Consequently all these loop spaces belong to RX .
    If V is any finite wedge of copies of K, the same theorem shows that the loops on every
finite wedge of spaces ΣV ∧m belong to RX : all its factors have the form ΩΣK ∧q . Factor
connectivities increase with word length, so the weak product is weakly equivalent to the

                                                   25
product. The iterated James splitting (D.1.7.8), now used without any Moore smash splitting,
expresses Σ(ΩΣ)r V as a finite-type wedge of precisely these smash powers. Its truncation
tower has increasingly connected tails. Therefore all (ΩΣ)r+1 V belong to RX . The convergent
simply connected resolution (D.1.7.9) proves V ∈ RX .
    We have proved nullity for every finite wedge of P D−1 (2). The finite cone-cube argument
at the end of D.1.7 lowers this whole family by one dimension at a time, down to top dimension
three. In particular P d (2) ∈ RX , as 3 ≤ d < D. Moreover P D−1 (2) and its loop space now
belong to RX . Repeating the same construction with d = D − 1 increases the upper bound to
11(D − 1) − 15, and iteration makes these bounds unbounded. Thus every P e (2) with e ≥ 3
belongs to RX , proving the stronger assertion. Adjunction proves the requested desuspension
closure. The only homotopy limits taken in this proof are limits in the targets; the source X
has been arbitrary throughout.

D.1.9 (Proved). The Moore spaces with cyclic fundamental group. For odd p,
the single-target null class of M (Z/p, 1) = P 2 (p) is closed under desuspension, for arbitrary
source spaces. For p = 2 this assertion is false: P 2 (2) = RP 2 admits an explicit simply
connected factorial-telescope counterexample. Consequently, among the usual Moore targets
M (Z/p, n) with p prime and n ≥ 1, the only failure is M (Z/2, 1).

Proof. Let p be odd, put Y = P 2 (p), and suppose Map∗ (X, ΩY ) ≃ ∗. We may replace X by
a CW model. This hypothesis forces X to be connected: otherwise S 0 is a pointed retract of
X, and ΩY would be a retract of the contractible mapping space. That is impossible, since
π0 ΩY = Z/p.
    The universal cover V of Y satisfies
                                                p−1
                                                _
                                          V ≃         S2.                             (D.1.9.1)

Indeed Y is the presentation complex of ha | ap i, and its p-sheeted universal cover is a simply
connected finite two-dimensional complex of Euler characteristic p. Its only nonzero reduced
integral homology is free of rank p − 1 in degree two. Representatives of a homology basis
under the Hurewicz isomorphism give a homology equivalence from the displayed wedge; the
simply connected Whitehead theorem makes it a homotopy equivalence.
    Since X is connected, its based maps to ΩY land in the base component ΩV . Thus
ΩV belongs to RX = {Z : Map∗ (X, Z) ≃ ∗}. There are at least two sphere summands in
(D.1.9.1). Hilton–Milnor on these two summands gives a retract ΩS q+1 for every q ≥ 1: use
a word of length q with q − 1 occurrences of one letter and one of the other when q ≥ 2, and
a sphere summand when q = 1. Hence

                                    ΩS d ∈ RX          (d ≥ 2).                       (D.1.9.2)

    Here is the unlooping and finite-target step with its source hypotheses made explicit. If
W is a finite wedge of spheres of dimension at least two, iterated James splitting expresses
Σ(ΩΣ)r W as a finite-type wedge of spheres. Hilton–Milnor and (D.1.9.2) put the loops on
every finite truncation in RX . Increasing connectivity of the tails and closure under homotopy
limits put (ΩΣ)r+1 W in RX for every r ≥ 0. The simply connected resolution (D.1.7.9) now
gives W ∈ RX . In particular every S d , d ≥ 2, belongs to this class.
    The class RX is a resolving kernel and is closed under extensions by fibrations. We can
therefore apply [64, Corollary 12(a), pp. 8–9], which puts every simply connected finite complex
in RX as soon as the sphere dimensions include the indicated infinite arithmetic progression.
Its proof uses the collection-level wedge theorem, the cone-length theorem, and finite cone
cubes. It has no finite-type hypothesis on X; the finite-type source hypothesis in that paper’s

                                                26
Theorem 1 enters only for the stronger assertion about arbitrary finite-dimensional targets.
We only need the two finite targets

                                           V, ΣY ∈ RX .                                (D.1.9.3)

In particular ΩΣY ∈ RX .
    Consider any based map f : X → Y and the unit ηY : Y → ΩΣY . The composite ηY f is
nullhomotopic by (D.1.9.3). On fundamental groups the unit is the abelianization map

                     π1 Y −→ H1 (Y ; Z) ∼
                                        = H2 (ΣY ; Z) ∼
                                                      = π2 ΣY = π1 ΩΣY.

For Y = P 2 (p) it is an isomorphism Z/p → Z/p. Thus π1 f = 0, so f lifts to the universal cover
V . The lift is nullhomotopic by (D.1.9.3); hence so is f . We have proved that Map∗ (X, Y )
has only its constant component. Its loop space is the given contractible space Map∗ (X, ΩY ),
so all its positive homotopy groups also vanish. Therefore Y ∈ RX , as required. Notice
that no loop–suspension convergence statement has been applied to the nonsimply connected
space Y itself.
    For p = 2, put
                                            2     3     4
                                X = Tel S 2 −

This is simply connected. The integral S 2 calculation in D.1.2 gives

Every based map from X, or from a suspension of X, to RP 2 lifts uniquely through its
universal cover S 2 . The corresponding based mapping-space comparison is a weak equivalence.
Thus Map∗ (X, ΩRP 2 ) ≃ ∗ whereas Map∗ (X, RP 2 ) 6≃ ∗. This proves the failure and, together
with D.1.7–D.1.8, the complete stated classification.

D.2.1 (Proved). Every finite thick subcategory occurs. Let C ⊆ Ho(Sp)ω be a thick
subcategory of finite spectra and set L = loc(C). Then

                             T R(L) = L,      T R(L) ∩ Ho(Sp)ω = C.

Thus every Hopkins–Smith type function occurs for a reversible fundamental localizer. The
classification input meant here is exactly Hopkins and Smith [41, Theorem 7]: a thick
subcategory of finite p-local spectra is one of the chromatic subcategories Cn , including zero.
Our realization argument does not require that classification theorem.

Proof. Every finite spectrum is, after a sufficiently large positive suspension, the suspension
spectrum of a finite pointed CW complex. This follows by realizing its finite list of stable cell
attachments after a common suspension; stable homotopies between the attaching maps are
realized after increasing that suspension once more. Hence L is generated by the suspension
spectra it contains, and the checked formula T R(L) = loc(L ∩ {suspension spectra}) from
POLES F.5 gives the first equality.
    We recall the compactness argument for the second equality. A localizing subcategory
generated by a set of compact objects consists of filtered homotopy colimits of their finite
iterated cofibers, shifts and retracts. One can verify this by cellular approximation: at
stage zero take a coproduct of shifts of the generators mapping to a proposed object, and
successively attach such cells to kill the kernel on maps out of every generator. The sequential
comparison is an equivalence within the generated subcategory. At a fixed finite stage,
compactness lets each finite collection of attaching maps factor through a finite collection of

                                                27
cells of preceding stages. Induction on the finite stage shows that the resulting object is a
filtered homotopy colimit of finite constructions. Sequential colimits are themselves filtered,
so this remains true at the limit. If a compact object lies in this subcategory, its identity
factors through one of these finite constructions. It is a retract of that object and therefore
belongs to the original thick subcategory. The reverse inclusion is immediate. Apply this to
the finite generators C.

D.2.2 (Proved). All nonzero torsion finite thick subcategories occur without
RT -fixedness. Every nonzero thick subcategory C of torsion finite spectra occurs as the
finite part of T (W ) for an accessible reversible localizer W which is not RT -fixed. In fact
T (W ) = loc(C) can be imposed.

Proof. Choose a prime p for which C contains a nonzero p-primary finite spectrum. Use the
sphere family of A.1 if p = 2 and the Toda family of A.6 otherwise, and denote its reversible
null class by Np .
     We need one consequence of the unstable Kahn–Priddy argument. For every finite p-
primary spectrum F , all sufficiently positive infinite-loop spaces Ω∞ Σr F lie in Np . To prove
it, the space form of Kahn–Priddy for BZ/p, explicitly stated in the main theorem of Kahn
and Priddy [44, p. 103], gives (Q0 S 0 )(p) in the reversible closure of BZ/p. The proof of C.3,
with 2 replaced by p, then gives Ω∞ Σr (S/p) for all r ≥ 1. Finite p-primary spectra form
the thick subcategory generated by S/p: alternatively, if pa idF = 0, then F is a retract of
F/pa , which is built from S/p by finite cells and extensions. Such an exponent exists because
End(F ) is a finite p-group. A finite construction by shifts, cofibers and retracts can be
suspended far enough that all its infinite-loop-space fibre sequences have connected bases. At
a cofiber step the first and second spaces are already aspherical; the fibre rule in C.1 gives
the third. Shifts are absorbed in the common suspension and retracts are preserved. This
proves the consequence without asserting that arbitrary fibres of equivalences are aspherical.
     Write a finite torsion spectrum as the finite wedge of its primary summands; the stable
Chinese-remainder idempotents give this decomposition, and thickness puts each summand
of an object of C back in C. For each primary F ∈ C choose a finite simply connected XF
and rF with Σ∞ XF ≃ ΣrF F . If F is p-primary choose rF sufficiently large as above and use
the aspherical generator QXF → ∗. For the other primes use XF → ∗. These latter spaces
are null for the p-local target family because their p-localizations are contractible.
     Let W be the reversible closure of this set of object-to-point maps. It is accessible by
B.4. All its generators lie in WNp , so W ⊆ WNp . They also lie in R(L) for L = loc(C): in
the QXF case the Snaith splitting expresses its suspension spectrum as homotopy orbits of
smash powers of Σ∞ XF , all in the tensor ideal L. Thus T (W ) ⊆ L. Conversely the first
summand in the same splitting retracts onto Σ∞ XF , and the other-prime generators already
have that spectrum. Therefore T (W ) contains every generator of L and equals L.
     Finally choose a nonzero p-primary F ∈ C. Its finite space XF is not null for the target
family. Indeed the Spanier–Whitehead dual of its nonzero finite suspension spectrum is
nonzero, so some stable cohomotopy group of XF localized at p is nonzero. After suspending
the domain and taking a sufficiently large odd-dimensional sphere as target, the stable class
is represented unstably. Such odd spheres occur in both target families. Hence XF ∈        / Np ,
         ∞
while Σ XF ∈ L = T (W ). The map XF → ∗ lies in RT (W ) and not in W , as required.

D.2.3 (Proved). The full finite thick subcategory occurs without fixedness. There
is an accessible reversible localizer W with T (W ) = Ho(Sp) and W 6= RT (W ). Thus the full
finite thick subcategory also occurs.



                                               28
Proof. Use G.5: the smallest reversible closure of BZ/2 → ∗ and BZ[1/2] → ∗ is contained
in the non-total completed-sphere localizer, but its stable part contains S/2 and S[1/2], which
together generate the sphere. The completed EHP comparisons and nonmembership of RP 2
are proved there.

D.2.4 (Open). The zero finite thick subcategory. The remaining finite-part question
is whether a non-RT -fixed reversible localizer can have T (W ) ∩ Ho(Sp)ω = 0. If the stronger
condition T (W ) = 0 is imposed, the answer is no: W ⊆ R(0) = WZ and every reversible
localizer contains WZ , so W = WZ . This does not settle a nonzero stable part having no
nonzero finite objects.

D.2.5 (Proved). A rational stable part cannot give the missing separation. If a
reversible fundamental localizer W satisfies
                                    0 6= T (W ) ⊆ loc(HQ),
then T (W ) = loc(HQ) and W = R(loc(HQ)). In particular it is RT -fixed.

Proof. Write AW for its pointed aspherical spaces. The stable category loc(HQ) is precisely
the rational stable category: the rationalized sphere is HQ, and its shifts generate that
category. Since T (W ) is nonzero, some X ∈ AW has nonzero reduced suspension spectrum.
It is connective and rational, so its first nonzero stable homotopy group is its first nonzero
integral homology group, a nonzero rational vector space. All its reduced integral homology
groups are rational. Also X is connected, since otherwise its reduced H0 is a nonzero free
abelian group.
    Suspend X enough that Z = Σr X is simply connected and its first nonzero reduced
homology occurs in an odd degree d ≥ 3. The rationalization criterion for simply connected
spaces [51, Theorems 6.1.1–6.1.2] makes Z rational. Choose a ∈ Hd Z and a rational-linear
functional ` : Hd Z → Q with `(a) = 1. Hurewicz realizes a by S d → Z, which extends
through SQ  d since Z is rational. The universal coefficient theorem, using injectivity of Q,

realizes ` by Z → K(Q, d). For odd d the latter target is SQ   d , by [51, Corollary 6.7.3]. The

composite is the identity on the sole nonzero homotopy group, so SQ     d is a homotopy retract

of Z. It belongs to AW .
    For n ≥ 1, the natural rational homology comparison and simple connectivity give
    n       n+1
ΣSQ ≃ SQ        ; at n = 1 one may use the telescope model of K(Q, 1). Suspension and
desuspension closure therefore put all positive rational spheres in AW .
    We give the needed cellular step explicitly. Let Y be a simply connected rational space,
with a CW model having one vertex and no one-cells. Inductively rationalize the boundary
spheres of its cells. At a stage already constructed as a simply connected rational space, each
attaching map extends from its ordinary sphere to the rational sphere. Attach its rationalized
cone by a homotopy cofiber, doing all cells in the dimension by a wedge. The new space is
simply connected and has rational integral homology, hence remains rational; comparison
with the ordinary skeleton is a rational homology equivalence by the cofiber exact sequence.
At the sequential homotopy colimit the result is YQ ≃ Y . All boundaries, wedges, cofibers
and colimits used are in AW , so Y ∈ AW . For the two-cells the boundary is a wedge of
K(Q, 1)’s and its cofiber is simply connected. No nilpotence assertion about that wedge of
circles is needed.
    Finally, if U has rational reduced suspension spectrum, reduced H0 makes it connected, and
ΣU is simply connected with rational homology. The preceding argument and desuspension
give U ∈ AW . This is exactly the aspherical class of R(loc(HQ)); the opposite inclusion
already follows from W ⊆ R(T (W )). The reversible cofiber criterion identifies the localizers.
Rational spheres in their null class generate loc(HQ), giving the asserted stable part.

                                              29
D.2.6 (Proved). A concrete nonzero stable part with no finite spectra. For every
prime p, put
                            Lp = loc Σ∞ K(Z/p, 2) .
                                                 

Then Lp 6= 0 and Lp ∩Ho(Sp)ω = 0. The smallest reversible localizer containing K(Z/p, 2) → ∗
is accessible and has stable part exactly Lp .

Proof. The exact form of Lee’s theorem used here is the opened [67, Corollary 1.4.1]: for a
simply connected π-finite space A, the collapse gives an equivalence S ≃ F (Σ∞ A+ , S). Here
π-finite means finitely many components and finitely many nonzero homotopy groups, all
finite. Taking A = K(Z/p, 2) and splitting off the basepoint gives

                                       F (Σ∞ A, S) = 0.

The annihilator {E : F (E, S) = 0} is localizing, since mapping spectra take coproducts to
products, cofiber sequences to fiber sequences, and shifts to inverse shifts. It contains Lp . A
finite spectrum in this annihilator has zero Spanier–Whitehead dual, hence is zero by finite
duality. On the other hand H2 (A; Z) = Z/p, so the generator is nonzero.
    Let W be the specified smallest reversible localizer. It is accessible by B.4, and W ⊆ R(Lp )
implies T (W ) ⊆ Lp . Its aspherical generator gives the opposite inclusion. This proves all
assertions without asserting that W is nonfixed.

    These results narrow D.2.4 but do not settle it. Neither a nonzero finite space nor BZ/p
can serve as a missing aspherical object for this stable part. Replacing the generator by
QK(Z/p, 2) also gives no separator: the original Eilenberg–Mac Lane space is an infinite-loop
retract of its Q. The stable-cohomotopy claims in [15, Section 10.12 and Lemma 10.13]
additionally rule out B(Z/p∞ ) and CP ∞ as naive replacement candidates. A genuinely
unstable separator is still required.
D.3.1 (Proved). There is no largest non-total reversible localizer. Two distinct
primes already give reversible localizers WFp , WFq having no common non-total reversible
upper bound. Both lie between WZ and W0 . Here non-total means strictly smaller than all
functors; it is distinct from the technical properness condition of Part E.

Proof. The coefficient-change theorem gives WZ ⊆ WFp , and the actual map on degree-zero
homology over a field detects a bijection of components, giving WFp ⊆ W0 . Reversibility
follows from the cofiber criterion for ordinary field homology. The same statements hold for
q.
    Let Y = S 1 [1/p] be the telescope of degree-p circle maps, and let i : S 1 → Y be its
initial-stage map. The space Y is a K(Z[1/p], 1) and has reduced integral homology only
in degree one, where it is Z[1/p]. Consequently it is Fp -acyclic. Its homotopy cofiber C(i)
is connected and has reduced integral homology only in degree one, where it is Z[1/p]/Z.
Multiplication by q 6= p is an automorphism of that group. The integral universal coefficient
sequence therefore makes C(i) Fq -acyclic.
    In any reversible upper bound, both Y and C(i) would be aspherical. Cofiber two out
of three would make S 1 aspherical. Desuspension would make S 0 aspherical, and pointed
cell constructions would then make every pointed space aspherical. The empty-to-point map
has cofiber S 0 , so the cofiber criterion includes it as well. Thus the localizer would be all
functors. This proves the assertion without inferring totality merely from a full stable part,
which would be invalid.




                                               30
D.3.2 (Open). The fixed stable part and its projective-space interval. For
L = loc(S/2) the lower endpoint is the accessible Wrev of B.4 and the upper endpoint is
R(L) = WZ[1/2] . They are distinct by A.4–A.5. The existence of a strict intermediate
reversible localizer, and the requested set-size assertion for an arbitrary fixed stable part,
remain active questions here.


5     The intrinsic fibrations of integral homology

E.1 (Refuted). The proposed comma-category criterion. The equivalence in Part
E.1 is false. In fact, its stated sufficient conditions do not imply membership in FibWZ , even
for a map between finite posets with contractible fibers and comma categories.

Proof. Let B = [2], and let E have four objects a, b, d, c and precisely the nonidentity arrows

                                      a −→ b ←− d −→ c.

Define p(a) = 0, p(b) = p(d) = 1, and p(c) = 2. The strict fibers are {a}, the interval d → b,
and {c}. The comma categories E/0, E/1, E/2 are respectively {a}, the span a → b ← d,
and E. Their nerves are trees. Every map in condition (i) is therefore an integral homology
isomorphism. The systems in condition (ii) are H0 = Z and Hq = 0 for q > 0, so (ii) holds
for every integral homology equivalence by its very definition.
    Let v : [1] → [2] select the arrow 0 → 2, and let u : [0] → [1] select its initial object. The
functor u is a weak homotopy equivalence over B. Its pullback along p is

                                           {a} −→ {a, c},

where the target is discrete. This map is not an isomorphism on H0 . The file
code/EH5_fence_counterexample.py specifies all objects, arrows, and compositions,
checks functoriality and the comma categories, and computes integral homology using
integer Smith normal forms. Its output gives fiber and comma Betti numbers (1, 0, . . .) and
pullback Betti numbers (2, 0). Thus the gap precedes the question about necessity of (ii):
the strict-pullback spectral sequence asserted in the prompt is not justified by its condition
(i).

Definition 5.1 (Hypercentral coefficient modules). For a left ZG-module M , define invariant
submodules transfinitely by

                     Z0 M = 0,
                   Zα+1 M = {m ∈ M : (g − 1)m ∈ Zα M for every g ∈ G},
                               [
                     Zλ M =         Zα M      (λ a limit).
                              α<λ

We call M hypercentral if this filtration exhausts M . The filtration stabilizes by an ordinal
of cardinality at most |M |+ , so no proper-class iteration is involved.

E.2 (Proved). An exact characterization of the coefficient systems. Let B be
a space of CW type, and let M be a local system of abelian groups on B. The following
conditions are equivalent:

    1. For every integral homology equivalence u : Y 0 → Y over B, the map H∗ (Y 0 ; u∗ M ) →
       H∗ (Y ; M ) is an isomorphism.

                                                31
  2. For every component and basepoint b ∈ B, the Zπ1 (B, b)-module Mb is hypercentral.

If π1 (B, b) is finitely generated, condition (2) is equivalent to the assertion that for every
m ∈ Mb there is a finite r with I r m = 0, where I is the augmentation ideal. A uniform
bound r on all elements is unnecessary. This answers both specified first cases B = S 1 and
B = S1 ∨ S1.

Proof. The submodules Zα Mb define local subsystems on each component; each successive
quotient has trivial monodromy. An integral homology equivalence induces an isomorphism
with every constant abelian coefficient group, by the integral universal coefficient exact
sequence. The long exact homology sequences of a short exact sequence of local systems show
that the property in (1) is closed under extensions. Singular chains with local coefficients
commute with filtered unions of coefficient modules. Transfinite induction, using this
observation at every limit ordinal, proves (2)⇒(1). Restriction along Y → B does not affect
the argument: the displayed filtration still has trivial successive quotients after restriction.
    For the converse, put G = π1 (B, b) and let Z∞ Mb be the stable value of the filtration. If
m0 ∈/ Z∞ Mb , stability implies that some g1 ∈ G has (g1 − 1)m0 ∈  / Z∞ Mb . Recursively choose
gi so that
                                  mi = (gi − 1)mi−1 ∈/ Z∞ M b .                           (E.2.1)
All mi are nonzero. Let T be a countable rose, with one loop for each gi , mapped to B by
those loops. Put V = T ∨ S 2 . The universal-cover cellular chain group of V relative to T is a
free rank-one group-ring module in degree two. For each i, choose a map hi : V → V fixing T ,
whose action on this relative module is multiplication by gi − 1. Such a map is obtained by
pinching the 2-sphere into two spheres, transporting the first around the indicated loop, and
mapping the second with degree −1. Equivalently, π2 (T ∨ S 2 ) is the free group-ring module
on the attached sphere, so the element gi − 1 supplies the specified attaching map.
    The projections V → T and their composites with hi are homotopic relative to T , because
T is aspherical. Choose these homotopies and form the mapping telescope Y = Tel(h1 , h2 , . . .),
with its resulting map to T and hence to B. The inclusion of the first copy of T gives a
map u : T → Y over B. Relative integral homology of the telescope is the direct limit of Z
in degree two under the zero maps, and is therefore zero. Hence u is an integral homology
equivalence.
    With the pulled-back system M , however, cellular relative chains give
                               
                               colim M −g1 −1     g2 −1          
                                       b −−  → Mb −−−→ Mb −→ · · · ,       j = 2,
              Hj (Y, T ; M ) =                                                           (E.2.2)
                               0,                                         j 6= 2.

This follows also from the ordinary mapping-telescope chain complex: the map 1 − shift
on the direct sum of the relative chain groups is injective, and its cokernel is the displayed
direct limit. The class of m0 in this limit is nonzero by (E.2.1). This contradicts (1), proving
hypercentrality. The construction stays in the fixed universe and uses a countable CW test,
regardless of the cardinality of G or Mb .
    Suppose now that G has a finite generating set. If every (g − 1)m lies in Zω Mb , it suffices
to check the finitely many generators. Their values lie in a common Zr Mb , and the identities
for a product or inverse show the same for every g ∈ G. Thus m ∈ Zr+1 Mb . Consequently
Zω Mb is already stable, and Zr Mb = {m : I r m = 0}. This proves the last assertion.
    For an explicit failure of uniform nilpotence over the circle, put

                                              Z[t, t−1 ]/(t − 1)r .
                                        M
                                  M=                                                     (E.2.3)
                                        r≥1



                                                 32
Every element belongs to finitely many summands and is killed by some power of t − 1,
whereas no one power kills all summands. Pull this system back along the map S 1 ∨ S 1 → S 1
that collapses the second circle for the second requested first case.

E.3 (Proved). The homology spectral sequence under the correct hypothesis.
For a functor p : E → B, assume that for every n ≥ 0, every σ : [n] → B, and every vertex
i ∈ [n], the strict-fiber inclusion
                                    Eσ(i) −→ E ×B [n]                             (E.3.1)
is an integral homology equivalence. Then its fiber homology groups form local systems Mq on
BB. For every simplicial set K → N B there is a natural strongly convergent first-quadrant
spectral sequence
                        2
                      Es,q = Hs (|K|; Mq ) =⇒ Hs+q (|N E ×N B K|; Z).                  (E.3.2)
There is also the relative sequence for a map K 0 → K over N B, with relative homology
on both sides. The local system Mq is the homology of the homotopy fibers of Bp and is
canonically identified with b 7→ Hq (E/b).

Proof. For a simplex σ : ∆[n] → K, put Pσ = N E ×N B ∆[n]. Its chain complex is a covariant
diagram on the simplex category ∆/K. The hypothesis implies that every map in this
diagram induces a homology isomorphism: compare a vertex of the source simplex with its
image vertex in the target simplex, and apply two out of three to (E.3.1). Hence its degree-q
homology is a local system on ∆/K and on |K|.
    The augmentation from the homotopy colimit of the Pσ to N E×N B K is a weak equivalence.
One direct verification uses the bisimplicial replacement: over a fixed simplex x of the target,
the category of factorizations of its image through simplices of K has the identity factorization
as an initial object. Every augmented row is consequently a contractible resolution of the
indicated simplex. Taking diagonals proves the assertion. The normalized chains of this
replacement form a first-quadrant double complex. Filtering by the simplex-category degree
gives (E.3.2); the homology of its horizontal complex is precisely homology with the displayed
local system. Taking mapping cones gives the relative version. There are only finitely many
terms on each total-degree diagonal, which proves strong convergence without any finite-type
assumption.
    In particular, strict pullback along a weak equivalence of simplicial sets over N B is an
integral homology equivalence, because weak equivalences preserve homology with every local
system. To identify the fibers, replace N B by a Kan complex S through a weak equivalence,
and pull its path fibration ending at b back to N B. The resulting space Cb → N B is weakly
contractible, and N E ×N B Cb models the homotopy fiber of Bp. The map from the vertex b
to Cb is a weak equivalence over N B, so the preceding observation identifies the homology
of the strict and homotopy fibers. Pullback along the contractible simplex resolution of
B/b, or equivalently the same spectral sequence over N (B/b), identifies this group with
Hq (E/b). The construction is natural under an arrow b → b0 and gives the stated monodromy
identification. Finally compare N E → N B with an ordinary Kan-fibration replacement over
a Kan replacement of N B. On every vertex fiber the comparison is the homology isomorphism
just established. Comparing (E.3.2) with the ordinary fibration spectral sequence therefore
proves that the strict-to-derived pullback comparison is a homology equivalence for every base
K → N B. Applying the same comparison to mapping cones proves the relative assertion,
with its specified maps.

E.4 (Proved). An exact intrinsic description of FibWZ . A functor p : E → B belongs
to FibWZ if and only if (E.3.1) holds and every local system Mq (b) = Hq (Eb ; Z) furnished by


                                               33
E.3 is hypercentral. Thus the characterization uses all finite-simplex pullbacks and an explicit
transfinite module filtration; it does not quantify over integral homology equivalences of bases.

                                                            i
Proof. If p is intrinsic, pull back the weak equivalence [0] −
                                                             → [n] over each σ to obtain (E.3.1).
We explain why its intrinsic property then also applies to simplicial homology equivalences
over N B. For K → N B the last-vertex functor ∆/K → B has nerve a simplicial set weakly
equivalent to K over N B. The comparison is the natural last-vertex map. Its strict pullback
along N E → N B is a homology equivalence by E.3. If u : K 0 → K is a homology equivalence,
the functor ∆/u belongs to WZ . The intrinsic hypothesis and these two comparisons imply
that its simplicial pullback along N E → N B is a homology equivalence as well.
    Suppose that some Mq is not hypercentral. Choose the telescope u : T → Y of E.2 for that
module. It can be represented over N B: choose a simplicial model over a Kan replacement S
of N B, replace its map by a Kan fibration, and pull back along N B → S. Right properness
of the ordinary Kan model gives weak equivalences of these models to the original spaces,
compatibly with u. Consequently the preceding paragraph applies. In the relative spectral
sequence (E.3.2), formula (E.2.2), now for every Mj , puts the entire second page in column
s = 2. There are no possible nonzero differentials. Therefore
                                                      g1 −1   g2 −1
              Hq+2 (E ×hB Y, E ×hB T ; Z) ∼
                                                                       
                                          = colim Mq −−−→ Mq −−−→ · · · 6= 0,            (E.4.1)

where the notation denotes the pullback models just specified; the strict models have the
same homology by E.3. This contradicts preservation of u. Thus every Mq is hypercentral.
This one-column test is the step that prevents spectral-sequence differentials from concealing
a failure in an individual fiber homology group.
    Conversely, assume (E.3.1) and hypercentrality. For any functor u : Y 0 → Y over B
in WZ , E.2 makes the maps of second pages in (E.3.2) isomorphisms. Strong convergence
and the comparison theorem give an integral homology equivalence E ×B Y 0 → E ×B Y .
This is exactly the defining condition for FibWZ . The argument includes empty fibers and
disconnected bases; all coefficient modules are then interpreted componentwise, and the zero
module is hypercentral.

E.5 (Proved). The trefoil obstruction R40 . If BB ≃ S 1 , the basepoint map ∗ → B has
no factorization as an integral homology equivalence followed by an intrinsic WZ -fibration.
The same assertion holds for homology with coefficients in any field. In particular the trefoil
obstruction R40 is valid.

Proof. Suppose such a factorization has total space E, so that BE is acyclic and connected.
The finite-simplex consequence of intrinsic preservation gives the homology spectral sequence
of E.3, also over a field. Let k denote Z or a field, and put S = π0 F , where F is the homotopy
fiber. Then H0 (F ; k) = k[S] is a permutation module for π1 B = hti. The edge terms over
the circle give
                             H0 (E; k) = k[S]t ,     H1 (E; k)  k[S]t .                  (E.5.1)
The first equality makes the action on S transitive. The second makes its invariant submodule
zero. A finite orbit has the nonzero invariant sum of its basis vectors, over any of these
coefficient rings. Thus S is an infinite orbit, and k[S] ∼
                                                         = k[t, t−1 ] with its regular action.
                                                   1
   Let T be the trefoil exterior and a : T → S its abelianization map. The complement
has integral homology that of a circle, for example by Alexander duality, so a is a homology
equivalence. Its group has the Wirtinger presentation

                    π1 T = hx, y | xyxy −1 x−1 y −1 i,   a∗ (x) = a∗ (y) = t.

                                                34
For clarity the required Alexander module is computed here, rather than inferred merely from
an Alexander polynomial. The infinite cyclic covering cellular complex through dimension
two has                                                            !
                                                       1 − t + t2
                        d1 = (t − 1, t − 1),   d2 =                  .
                                                     −(1 − t + t2 )
Since t − 1 is not a zero divisor, ker d1 is generated by (1, −1). Consequently

                          H1 (T ; k[t, t−1 ]) ∼
                                              = k[t, t−1 ]/(t2 − t + 1) 6= 0.              (E.5.2)

The two Fox derivatives follow by differentiating the six letters of the relator; they are
also checked in code/EH5_trefoil.py. The polynomial has nonzero leading and constant
coefficients in every field, so it is never a Laurent unit. The standard three-crossing Wirtinger
calculation and its Alexander matrix are independently displayed in EH5 [1, Example 2.35].
Only the group presentation is needed for this H1 calculation; no asphericity theorem for
knot complements is being assumed.
    After pulling E → S 1 back along a, the term E1,0   2 of its spectral sequence is (E.5.2). No

differential enters or leaves this term in a first-quadrant homology spectral sequence. Thus
the new total space has nonzero H1 , whereas E has zero H1 . This contradicts intrinsic
preservation of a. Categories of simplices and E.3 supply the same contradiction for strict
categorical pullbacks.
    There is also a shorter integral check from E.4: the regular module Z[t, t−1 ] has no nonzero
invariant elements, hence its transfinite central filtration is zero. It cannot be one of the fiber
modules of an intrinsic WZ -fibration.

E.6 (Proved). The localization-unit criterion R10(a). Let W be an accessible
fundamental localizer, and let ηX : X → LX be its localization unit on spaces. Then W is
proper if and only if every homotopy fiber of every ηX is W -aspherical.

Proof. For an accessible proper localizer, Cisinski’s Cisinski [29, Theorem 6.1.11] identifies
W with the localizer generated by its aspherical objects. The associated localization is
therefore nullification. Its local spaces are closed under dependent sums over a local base:
apply Map(A, −) to such a sum for each nullifying object A, and use Map(A, Z) ≃ Z first
on the base and then on every fiber. Thus the units of the associated modality have W -
aspherical homotopy fibers. Equivalently, in the right proper localized simplicial model, pull a
localization unit back along the path fibration of its local target. That is a localized fibration,
because it is an ordinary fibration between local spaces. Right properness identifies its total
space, the unit fiber, with a point in W .
     Conversely let P be the localization generated by all W -aspherical objects. It exists and is
accessible by Cisinski [29, Proposition 6.1.8]. Suppose every unit fiber is W -aspherical, hence
P -aspherical. For a P -local space Z, write the domain of a unit as the homotopy colimit of
its fiber diagram over its full target space. Then

               Map(X, Z) ≃ holim Map(fiby ηX , Z) ≃ holim Z ≃ Map(LX, Z).                  (E.6.1)
                               y∈LX                         y∈LX

The middle equivalence is natural in paths and higher coherences of LX; it is not a product
over the set of points. Thus every unit is a P -equivalence. Naturality of units and two out of
three imply W ⊆ P , while P ⊆ W by definition. Hence W = P , and Theorem 6.1.11 gives
properness. This checks the entire localization-fiber assertion used in R10(a).

A fixed catalogue for the factorization question. Here L = LHZ and ηT : T → LT
denotes its unit. Work first with a simplicial map f : K → B, where B is Kan, and put

                                    λ = max(ℵ0 , |K|, |B|).

The size of a simplicial set means the cardinality of all its simplices. Fix a set Cλ (B) = {pi :
Vi → B}i∈I of representatives of the following explicitly specified maps:

   1. |Vi | ≤ λ, and every finite Kan horn lifting problem for pi has a solution;

   2. for every vertex b and every q ≥ 0, the monodromy module Hq ((Vi )b ; Z) satisfies

                                  Zλ+ Hq ((Vi )b ; Z) = Hq ((Vi )b ; Z).                 (E.8.1)

There are at most 2λ isomorphism types of such maps. Condition (E.8.1) is a bounded
transfinite calculation: every module in question has cardinality at most λ, so its upper
central filtration stabilizes before λ+ . Thus this definition of the catalogue uses only horn

lifting, ordinary fiber homology, and the filtration of E.2. It makes no reference to an unknown
factorization or to FibWZ .

over that base. For α ∈ Ai (f ) the unit and this specified homotopy give a canonical map
ai,α : K → Pi .

                   zx = αηK (x),        bx = f (x),       γx : ηB (bx ) ≃ Lpi (zx ).

These spaces, with their transports and higher coherences, form a family on the fundamental
infinity-groupoid of K. In particular, the homotopy limit below retains the specified coherences;
replacing it by a condition on the individual fibers would change the test.
E.8 (Proved). A bounded localization-unit-fiber test for all maps. The map
f : K → B factors as an integral homology equivalence followed by a map preserving all
integral homology equivalences under homotopy base change if and only if

                    for some i ∈ I and α ∈ Ai (f ),          holim Di,α (x) 6= ∅.            (E.8.4)
                                                              x∈K

This is a set-sized test: the catalogue is fixed by B and λ, independently of f , and its
membership conditions are the two displayed conditions above. All remaining obstructions in
(E.8.4) concern maps between the localization-unit fibers in (E.8.3).
    For an arbitrary functor f : X → Y , apply the test to N X → N Y → Ex∞ N Y and to
λ = max(ℵ0 , |N X|, |N Y |). Then (E.8.4) is equivalent to the existence of an actual strict
factorization
                             u     p
                           X−→E−  → Y,      u ∈ WZ , p ∈ FibWZ .                    (E.8.5)
Thus the answer includes all functors, without a fibration or Grothendieck-construction
hypothesis on the given map.

Proof. We first prove the size assertion which makes this criterion bounded. Suppose
         p
K→V −    → B is a homotopy factorization of the required kind. Replace p by an ordinary Kan
fibration and represent the first map strictly over B. The Kan-fibration version of E.4, with
the same Serre spectral sequence and telescope proof, says that every Hq (Vb ; Z) is hypercentral.
Start with the simplicial subset V0 ⊂ V generated by the image of K. Inductively construct
Vn ⊂ Vn+1 by making these three choices in V :

   1. For every horn in Vn over a simplex of B, include the simplices of one filler supplied by
      p.

   2. For every integral simplicial cycle in Vn which bounds in V , include the finite support
      of one chain bounding it.

   3. For every vertex b of B and every integral simplicial cycle in (Vn )b which bounds in Vb ,
      include the finite support of one chain bounding it inside Vb .

                                                  37
Take the simplicial closure of all these choices at each stage. There are at most λ choices:
horns have finitely many nondegenerate simplices, integral chains have finite support and
integer coefficients, there are countably many degrees, and |B0 | ≤ λ. Consequently |Vn | ≤ λ
for every n.
    Put V∞ = n Vn . Every finite horn occurs at a finite stage, so V∞ → B is Kan. The
                 S

second and third clauses give injections

                  H∗ (V∞ ; Z) ,→ H∗ (V ; Z),        H∗ ((V∞ )b ; Z) ,→ H∗ (Vb ; Z).     (E.8.6)

Indeed a cycle is supported at one finite stage, and a boundary in the indicated ambient
complex acquires a chosen bounding chain at the next stage. The total injection is also onto:
every class of H∗ (V ) comes from H∗ (K), and K → V already factors through V∞ . Thus
K → V∞ is an integral homology equivalence. The second injection is a map of monodromy
modules, since both maps to B are Kan fibrations. A submodule of a hypercentral module is
hypercentral: transfinite induction gives Zβ N = N ∩ Zβ M for N ⊂ M . Therefore every fiber
homology module of V∞ is hypercentral. No surjectivity on fiber homology is needed. We
have produced a member of the catalogue through which the desired factorization passes.
    For completeness, an upper central filtration on a set of at most λ elements cannot have
a strict successor increase at every stage below λ+ . At its first stationary stage it remains
stationary. Hence hypercentrality is exactly (E.8.1). Labeling the simplices of a set of size at
most λ in each degree gives at most 2λ choices of simplicial operators and maps to B. This
proves all catalogue bounds, inside the fixed universe.
    We next identify the stated obstruction. In any homotopy commutative square

This follows directly by writing a homotopy fiber as a point together with a path to its

                                       K ×hPi Vi −→ K,                                  (E.8.7)

and its homotopy limit is precisely the space of homotopy sections of (E.8.7).
    This is also a concrete obstruction test on the cells of |K|. Choose points in the spaces
(E.8.3) over the vertices, then compatible paths over edges. At an n-cell, trivialize the
pulled-back family over its contractible characteristic disk. For n ≥ 2 the boundary lift
specifies a class in πn−1 of the corresponding space (E.8.3); it must be zero, and one then
chooses a nullhomotopy. For edges the two endpoint points must lie in the same component
after transport. The choices must extend each other through all skeleta. Compatible choices
produce a section by the CW weak topology, and a section supplies them. This formulation
includes the inverse-limit compatibility in (E.8.4); nonemptiness of each separate finite-stage
problem would not suffice.

    Conversely, a section of (E.8.7) is a map u : K → Vi over B together with ηVi u ≃ αηK . It
may be represented strictly over B, because K is cofibrant and pi is Kan. Localizing this

                                                38
identity gives Lu ≃ α under the canonical unit equivalences. Thus u is an integral homology

   ∞
Ex N Y has at most λ simplices: each finite subdivision of a simplex is finite, and there
are countably many iterations. An intrinsic factorization of the functor gives a homotopy
factorization over this Kan replacement by E.3–E.4, so the necessity already proved applies.

Since Vi → B is Kan, ordinary right properness shows that

                                    V 0 = N Y ×B Vi −→ Vi

is a weak equivalence. We obtain a strict simplicial diagram N X → V 0 → N Y , with first map
an integral homology equivalence and second map having the required homotopy preservation
property. To realize the original source X itself, use the ordinary proper Thomason model
structure, as established with its natural unit and counit equivalences in [29, Theorem 5.2.12].
Applying c Sd2 Ex2 gives a strict diagram

              QX −→ C −→ Y,            QX = c Sd2 Ex2 N X,      C = c Sd2 Ex2 V 0 .

The counit q : QX → X is a weak equivalence, and naturality makes the composite to Y
equal to f q. The natural equivalences in that theorem identify the displayed diagram with
N X → V 0 → N Y in homotopy.
    Factor QX → C, in the slice over Y , as an ordinary Thomason cofibration QX → C0
followed by an ordinary trivial fibration C0 → C. Form D = X qQX C0 in that slice. Left
properness makes C0 → D a weak equivalence. By two out of three in homology, the induced
map X → D is an integral homology equivalence, and D → Y has the same derived fibers
and monodromy as V 0 → N Y . Replace D → Y by an ordinary Thomason fibration D0 → Y
through a weak equivalence D → D0 . Right properness makes every vertex-to-simplex
pullback map for D0 → Y a weak equivalence. Its derived fiber homology is hypercentral,
so E.4 gives D0 → Y ∈ FibWZ . The strict factorization X → D0 → Y proves (E.8.5). This
argument uses actual slice diagrams throughout.

E.8.1 (Proved). The boundary cases for the factorization problem. For connected
CW spaces the following assertions hold. Every map to a connected space Y admits a
factorization as an integral homology equivalence followed by a homotopy map with the
intrinsic preservation property if and only if H1 (Y ; Z) = 0. In particular the basepoint map
∗ → Y admits such a factorization if and only if H1 Y = 0. If both X and Y are HZ-local,
then f : X → Y admits such a factorization if and only if its homotopy fibers have hypercentral
homology as π1 Y -modules. For any factorable map, H1 f is surjective. The converse fails:
the retraction S 1 ∨ S 1 → S 1 is not factorable.

Proof. A hypercentral integral permutation module Z[S] has trivial action on S. Indeed
restrict to the cyclic subgroup generated by any g. Hypercentrality restricts, and E.2 says
that (g − 1) acts locally nilpotently. If gs 6= s, the elements (g − 1)n [s] are nonzero for every
n: for an infinite orbit this follows by their distinct extreme basis vectors, and for a finite
orbit by tensoring with C and diagonalizing its cyclic permutation, which has a nontrivial
root-of-unity eigenvalue. Thus gs = s for every g, s.
    For a connected total space E over connected Y , the action on π0 F is transitive, with
stabilizer the image of π1 E → π1 Y . E.4 therefore makes the fiber connected and this
                                                                 u
fundamental-group map surjective. In a factorization X −        → E → Y with u a homology

                                               39
equivalence, H1 u is an isomorphism, proving surjectivity of H1 f . The basepoint case gives
the necessary condition H1 Y = 0.
    Suppose conversely that H1 Y = 0. Attach 2-cells to Y along a set of generating loops,
obtaining a simply connected space Y2 . The relative group H2 (Y2 , Y ) is free on those cells.
Since H1 Y = 0, the map H2 Y2 → H2 (Y2 , Y ) is onto. By Hurewicz choose based 2-spheres
representing lifts of its basis, and attach 3-cells along them. The resulting j : Y → Y + has
simply connected target, and its relative cellular complex is the identity map between the
free groups in degrees three and two. Hence j is an integral homology equivalence. Every
simply connected space is HZ-local: the groups K(A, n) are local by the universal coefficient
theorem, the Postnikov tower of a simply connected space is built from them by homotopy
fibers, and local spaces are closed under homotopy limits. Thus j is an HZ-localization unit.
Its homotopy fibers are acyclic. To verify this directly, use the Serre spectral sequence over
the simply connected base Y + . If q > 0 were the least degree with nonzero reduced fiber
homology, then the term E0,q 2 could only be hit by a transgression from the base row. Every

base-row class survives because H∗ Y → H∗ Y + is onto in every degree. Thus E0,q 2 would give
                                   +
a nonzero kernel of Hq Y → Hq Y , a contradiction. The fiber is connected by the homotopy
exact sequence.
    For any f : X → Y , form the canonical homotopy pullback
                                     E = Y ×hY + LHZ X,                                 (E.8.8)
using the localized map LHZ X → Y + . The projection E → LHZ X has the acyclic fibers just
proved, so is a homology equivalence by the Serre spectral sequence. The canonical X → E
is consequently a homology equivalence by two out of three. The other projection E → Y is
a pullback of a fibration over the simply connected space Y + . Its fiber homology has trivial
monodromy, and E.4 gives the intrinsic preservation property. This also handles disconnected
X, componentwise; an empty source uses the empty total space.
                                                                     u     p
    Now assume that both endpoints of f are HZ-local, and let X −    →E−  → Y be a requested
factorization. The restriction map
                               MapY (E, X) −→ MapY (X, X),
where these are derived mapping spaces in the slice over Y , is an equivalence. It is the map of
homotopy fibers of the two restriction maps with targets X and Y , both equivalences because
these targets are local and u is a homology equivalence. Thus the identity of X extends to
r : E → X over Y , with ru ≃ idX over Y . The map f is a homotopy retract of p in the slice.
Preservation of homology equivalences under homotopy base change is closed under such
retracts, so E.4 applies to f . The converse is its ordinary fibration replacement.
    Finally suppose the projection X = S 1 ∨ S 1 → S 1 had a factorization, with fiber F
and total space E. The fiber is connected by the first paragraph. Over the circle the Serre
spectral sequence has only columns zero and one, so H2 E = 0 implies H1 (F )t = 0. A
nonzero hypercentral module has a nonzero invariant element, by taking the first nonzero
stage of its central filtration. Hence H1 F = 0. The degree-one sequence then gives H1 E =  ∼ Z,
                      ∼
contradicting H1 E = H1 X = Z .∼  2

At height one this is an equivalence in every degree, not just above a range. This last clause
is explicit in the cited theorem.
    Take V = Σ4 S(p) . Then Z = (QS 4 )(p) is 3-connected, so Pτ2 Z = ∗. The paragraph
preceding Corollary 4.5 in Bousfield [12] identifies L1 S(p) ≃ (LKU S)(p) ; its Corollary 4.5 then
gives, for odd p,
                        π−3 L1 S(p) = 0,   π−2 L1 S(p) = Q/Z(p) =: Cp∞ .                   (E.9.4)
Consequently π1 Z 0 = 0 and π2 Z 0 = Cp∞ . The latter group is torsion, so Pτ2 Z 0 = ∗ also.
Equation (E.9.3) therefore identifies L1 Z with Z 0 , and in particular

                                π1 L1 Z = 0,       π2 L1 Z = Cp∞ .                          (E.9.5)

    For completeness this is also the KU -localization of Z, not merely its p-local variant.
The target L1 Z is KU -local since every KU -equivalence is a KU(p) -equivalence. The source
and target are p-local nilpotent spaces. For a prime q 6= p they are mod-q acyclic: their
reduced homology groups are Z(p) -modules, so multiplication by q is invertible. Lemma
10.6 of Bousfield [15] then makes them KU/q-acyclic. Their map is a rational and a KU/p
equivalence because it is a KU(p) -equivalence. The arithmetic detection statement in Bousfield
[15, Theorem 10.1] makes it a KU -equivalence. Thus (E.9.5) computes LKU Z.
    Choose a map f presenting KU -homology localization. On connected spaces, the checked
loop criterion identifies WKUlc with Σf -equivalences: Bousfield [15, Theorem 5.2] identifies

the specified unit after looping as

                                   ΩX −→ ΩLΣf X ≃ Lf ΩX.                                    (E.9.6)

Here ΩX ≃ Z. The map induces an isomorphism on components and, by (E.9.5), both loop
spaces are simply connected. Hence X, LΣf X, and the homotopy fiber F of their unit are
simply connected. The homotopy exact sequence, using π2 ΩX = 0, now gives

                                      π2 F = π1 ΩF ∼
                                                   = Cp∞ .                                  (E.9.7)

The space K(Cp∞ , 1) is L1 -local by Bousfield [17, Theorem 7.4]: for a connected Postnikov
H-space that theorem preserves the p-localized homotopy group in degree one, removes
torsion only in degree two, and rationalizes degrees above two. It is consequently KU -local as

                                                 41
well. The nonzero identity homomorphism π1 ΩF → Cp∞ represents a nonconstant component
of Map∗ (ΩF, K(Cp∞ , 1)). Thus ΩF is not KU -acyclic. The loop criterion says exactly that
F is not WKUlc -aspherical. Applying E.6 proves nonproperness.

    All spaces in (E.9.1)–(E.9.7) admit small simplicial models. Passing to their categories of
simplices therefore supplies the same localization-unit obstruction for fundamental localizers
on Cat; the conclusion does not rest on a finite-dimensional or finite-type approximation.


6    The literal axioms: absolute localization is weaker

F.1 (Refuted). Absolute Theorem A does not imply relative Theorem A, even
with Loc (8) and (9). There is a strongly saturated class W † of small-category functors
satisfying XVI Loc (2), (3), (8), and (9), but satisfying neither Loc (3 bis) nor Loc (60 a).
A finite counterexample to relative Theorem A has a three-object indexing poset and a map
from a 77-object poset to a 53-object poset. For a base with a terminal object, in particular
[1], relative Theorem A is tautological.

Proof. For an abelian group A, write Tor(A) for its torsion subgroup. Define W † to consist of
the functors u : X → Y satisfying the following four requirements: Π1 (Bu) is an equivalence
of fundamental groupoids; Hn (Bu; Z) is an isomorphism for every n ≥ 3; H2 (Bu; Q) is an
isomorphism; and Tor H2 (Bu; Z) is an isomorphism. No local coefficient systems occur in
this definition.
    Each requirement is the inverse image of a class of isomorphisms, or of equivalences of
groupoids. Consequently W † satisfies two out of three, the rule involving uv and vu, and
retract closure. It is strongly saturated as well: each of the indicated functors factors through
         −1
Cat[W † ], so any arrow invertible there passes every test. It contains W∞ .
    We first identify its aspherical objects. If X → e belongs to W † , then BX is nonempty,
connected, and simply connected. Its homology vanishes above degree two. The group
A = H2 (BX; Z) has A ⊗ Q = 0 and Tor(A) = 0. The first equality says that every element
of A is torsion, and the second therefore implies A = 0. Thus BX is simply connected and
acyclic. A first nonzero homotopy group would contradict the Hurewicz theorem, and hence
BX is weakly contractible. The converse is immediate. In particular a category with a
terminal object is W † -aspherical. If u : X → Y has all comma categories X/y W † -aspherical,
they are weakly contractible, so Quillen’s Theorem A puts u in W∞ ⊆ W † . This is exactly
the absolute Loc (3), with the required variance. The theorem used is [56, §1, Theorem
A]: contractibility of all the indicated comma-category classifying spaces implies that the
specified map BX → BY is a homotopy equivalence. Its opposite-category form gives the
same assertion for the other comma convention.
    Here are completely specified finite witnesses. Let Cm be the polygonal simplicial circle
with vertices 0, . . . , m − 1 and edges {i, i + 1}, indices modulo m, and let Lm = Cm ∗ {am , bm },
where the last two vertices span no edge. This is the simplicial suspension of a polygon, and
its realization is S 2 . The simplicial map

                  h : L8 −→ L4 ,         i 7−→ i mod 4,       a8 7−→ a4 ,   b8 7−→ b4

has degree two. Indeed, the oriented integral fundamental chain is
                           m−1
                           X                                     
                    zm =         [am , i, i + 1] − [bm , i, i + 1] ,   h∗ z8 = 2z4 .
                           i=0

Let P and Q be the posets of nonempty faces of L8 and L4 , ordered by inclusion, and let
f : P → Q send a face to its image under h. Their nerves are the barycentric subdivisions

                                                    42
of the displayed spheres; P has 50 elements and Q has 26. The functor f belongs to W † ,
since its only nonzero positive homology map is multiplication by two on H2 = Z, which is a
rational isomorphism, and both torsion groups are zero.
    Let J have three objects 0, 1, 2 and just the nonidentity arrows 0 → 1 and 0 → 2. Consider
the diagrams
                                   f                         id
                        D = (Q ←  − P −→ e),        E = (Q ←  − Q −→ e)
and the natural transformation with components (f, idQ , ide ) in the order (0, 1, 2). The
induced functor                      Z       Z
                                  v : D −→ E
                                               J         J
                                     R
is a functor over J. Explicitly, J D has disjoint P and Q layers and a point t; besides the
original orders it has p ≤ q exactly when f (p) ≤ q, and p ≤ t for every p. There are no
comparisons between Q and t. The target has the same description with P = Q and f = idQ .
These prescriptions define all arrows and compositions, since the categories are posets.
    For each j ∈ J, evaluation
                        Z
                       (       D)/j −→ D(j),       (i, x, α : i → j) 7−→ D(α)x
