is left adjoint to x 7→ (j, x, idj ), with identity counit. The evaluation squares commute with v.
Hence v/j is in W † for all j, because its component comparison    R
                                                                     is f , idQ , or ide . On the other
hand, the twoRfull sieve pieces P ∪ Q and P ∪ {t} identify B J D with the homotopy cofibre
of Bf , and B J E with the homotopy cofibre of an identity. Their reduced cellular homology
is respectively Z/2 in degree two and zero in every degree. Thus v fails the torsion-H2 test.
Relative Theorem A is false for W † .
     The same source collage refutes Loc (60 a). In its sieve-cover square the top inclusion
P → P ∪ Q is in W † , because P ∪ QR retracts onto Q by a left adjoint and its composite is
f . The parallel inclusion P ∪ {t} → J D has contractible source and target with H2 = Z/2,
and is not in W † .
     The program code/FGH_literal_axioms.py constructs the two simplicial complexes, all
face-poset orders, the two collage orders, and every functor just specified. It checks reflexivity,
antisymmetry, transitivity, functoriality, the sieve cover, the evaluation adjunctions and
their commutative squares, and the integral equality h∗ z8 = 2z4 . It finds 521 arrows in the
77-object source and 321 in the 53-object target, including identities. The integral cofiber
                                                           2
calculation is the two-term cellular differential Z −     → Z in degrees three and two; it is not
inferred from a rational rank computation.
     For completeness we verify both extra size axioms. Nerves commute with filtered colimits,
since an n-simplex is a finite composable string of arrows. Fundamental groupoids commute
with filtered colimits: paths and the elementary relations between paths are finite data.
Integral and rational homology commute with filtered colimits of simplicial sets, by exactness
of filtered colimits of abelian groups. Finally, Tor commutes with filtered colimits. If a
representative a is torsion in the colimit, some equation na = 0, n ≥ 1, already holds at
a later stage, and equality of two torsion representatives is likewise witnessed at a later
stage. Thus every test defining W † commutes with filtered colimits, and filtered colimits of
equivalences of groupoids are equivalences. This proves Loc (8).
     We give the bounded-subcategory argument for Loc (9), rather than treating it as a formal
consequence of the word “accessible”. Fix u : X → Y in W † and countable subcategories
X0 ⊆ X, Y0 ⊆ Y with uX0 ⊆ Y0 . A countable enlargement can supply all witnesses to
the following countable collection of requests: paths witnessing essential surjectivity of the
fundamental groupoid; lifts of its arrows and witnesses to their equality; fillings of integral
cycles in degrees at least three; rational cycle lifts and fillings in degree two; and lifts and

                                                   43
fillings of torsion classes in degree two. Every request is a finite chain, a finite zigzag, or
a finite homotopy of zigzags, together with an integer denominator or a torsion equation.
Because u passes the relevant global test, the requested witness exists in X or Y and uses
only finitely many arrows. Adjoin all these arrows and close under identities and composition;
the enlargement remains countable. Iterate the construction countably many times. Every
finite request in the union occurs at a finite stage and is answered at a later stage, so the
restricted union functor belongs to W † . In the torsion-surjectivity request we also adjoin a
finite chain witnessing that the chosen source class is torsion; this point prevents replacement
of torsion by rational homology.
     The countable W † -subarrows of u therefore form a filtered cofinal family of countable
subarrows, and their colimit is u. There is a small set of isomorphism representatives of
all functors between countable categories. Consequently W † is the filtered-colimit envelope
of its members in this set. Together with Loc (8), this is precisely XVI Proposition 5.3.1
and Corollary 5.3.2’s formulation of Loc (9), with the countable bound. In fact the same
saturation of a countable family gives an ℵ1 -filtered presentation.
     Finally, if the base S has terminal object s, then S/s ∼   = S over S. Hence X/s ∼     = X,
      ∼              ∼
Y /s = Y , and v/s = v. The hypothesis at s already asserts the conclusion. The suggested
base [1] cannot witness failure.

F.1.1 (Proved). Forward gluing and filtered colimits recover relative Theorem A.
Let W satisfy the literal XVI Loc (1), (2), and (3). If W also satisfies Loc (60 a) and Loc (8),
then it is a fundamental localizer: in particular it satisfies relative Theorem A, Loc (3 bis).
Loc (9) is unnecessary for this implication. Thus the assertion discussed in XVI manuscript
page 118, footnote 56, holds under weaker size hypotheses than those stated there.

Proof. By Cisinski [30, Theorem 6.1.18], W contains W∞ . We verify the simplicial-localizer
axioms directly, thereby avoiding an implicit use of relative Theorem A in a homotopy-colimit
argument.
   Write i∆ X = ∆/X for the category of simplices of a simplicial set X, and set

                                V = {f : X −→ Y : i∆ f ∈ W }.

The class V has two out of three and contains all weak homotopy equivalences, because
the natural comparison B(∆/X) ≃ |X| identifies the weak homotopy type of a category of
simplices. In particular V contains all maps having the right lifting property against all
monomorphisms.
   We next prove that a monomorphism in V remains in V after every pushout. Let X ,→ Y
be such a monomorphism, let X → Z be arbitrary, and put T = Y qX Z. Apply the
double-collage construction to the span of categories
                       f    g
                   B←
                    −A−
                      → C,            A = ∆/X,      B = ∆/Y,     C = ∆/Z.

Thus Mf has the disjoint A and B layers, with arrows a → b equal to B(f a, b) and no
arrows in the other direction; define Mg in the same way. Their union P = Mf qA Mg has
no arrows between the B and C layers. The two pieces are full sieves covering P . The
retractions Mf → B and Mg → C are left adjoints and hence belong to W∞ . Since f ∈ W ,
two out of three gives A → Mf ∈ W . Loc (60 a) now gives Mg → P ∈ W , and consequently
C → P ∈ W.
    Every chain in P lies in one of its two sieve pieces, so

                                   N P = N M f qN A N M g .

                                              44
This is a homotopy pushout after realization: both attaching maps are inclusions of simplicial
subsets. The collage retractions identify its homotopy type with the homotopy pushout of
BB ← BA → BC. The compatible functors from the three layers to ∆/T give a canonical
functor P → ∆/T . It is a weak homotopy equivalence, since the original simplicial pushout
Y qX Z is a homotopy pushout when X → Y is a monomorphism. Composing C → P with
this weak equivalence proves ∆/Z → ∆/T ∈ W , as required.
    The functor i∆ commutes with filtered colimits. Its objects are simplices and its arrows
are simplicial operators satisfying one equality, so both objects and arrows, and their defining
relations, are finite data in such a colimit. Loc (8) therefore makes V closed under filtered
colimits of arrows. It follows that its monomorphisms are closed under every small transfinite
composition. More explicitly, at a successor stage use composition and at a limit ordinal λ
apply filtered-colimit closure to the arrows X0 → Xβ , β < λ. Transfinite induction supplies
all these arrows before the limit step.
    We have checked exactly the three axioms of a ∆-localizer in Cisinski [30, Definition 1.4.1]:
two out of three, inclusion of trivial fibrations, and closure of the monomorphisms in the class
under pushouts and transfinite compositions. Every ∆-localizer is regular by that source’s
Proposition 3.4.25. All its representables are weakly contractible, so it is a test localizer.
Theorem 4.2.15 and Remark 4.2.16 therefore associate to V the fundamental localizer N −1 V
on categories. These results do not assume accessibility.
    Finally N −1 V = W . For every category A the natural last-vertex functor ∆/N A → A is
a weak homotopy equivalence. Its naturality square for u : A → B, together with W∞ ⊆ W
and two out of three, gives

                        u∈W       ⇐⇒     i∆ N u ∈ W     ⇐⇒     N u ∈ V.

Hence W itself is the fundamental localizer just obtained. This proves relative Theorem A
without any invocation of Loc (9), a model structure for W , or an unproved gluing theorem
for W -equivalences.

F.2 (Proved). The reverse single-square axiom alone forces integral homology
equivalences. Let W satisfy the literal XVI Loc (1), (2), and (3). If it satisfies Loc (60
b), then WZ ⊆ W . In particular the same conclusion holds under the cube axiom Loc (6 b),
including for nonconnected sources. Neither relative Theorem A nor forward gluing is needed.

Proof. Cisinski’s Theorem 6.1.18 [30] says exactly that the weak homotopy equivalences
form the least class satisfying the weak-fundamental-localizer axioms, namely the indicated
saturation, terminal-object, and absolute-Theorem-A axioms. Thus W∞ ⊆ W under
the present assumptions. The proof of that theorem uses its Proposition 6.1.16 and the
contractibility of horns; it does not insert relative Theorem A into the hypotheses.
    For a functor g : A → B use the collage Mg = A q B with mixed arrows a → b given by
B(g(a), b) and with no arrows from B to A. Its retraction r : Mg → B is left adjoint to the
inclusion of B, and ri = g for the inclusion i : A → Mg . Adjoin a point t receiving one arrow
from every object of A, with no arrows between B and t, and denote the result by K(g). The
full subcategories Mg and A. = A ∪ {t} are sieves covering K(g), with intersection A. On
nerves their union is literal: every chain lies in the sieve containing its final object. Their
realizations form a homotopy-pushout square, since the two inclusions of the intersection are
CW-subcomplex inclusions. The specified retraction BMg → BB and contraction BA. → ∗
identify BK(g) with the homotopy cofibre C(Bg). This checks the part of POLES B.3 used
here without invoking its additional assertion about W -maps of cofibres.
    Suppose K(g) → e is in W . Then A. → K(g) is in W by two out of three, because
the cone category has a terminal object. Applying Loc (60 b) to the displayed sieve cover

                                               45
gives A → Mg in W , and composition with r ∈ W∞ gives g ∈ W . We have proved only the
implication we need:
                         K(g) is W -aspherical =⇒ g ∈ W.
    Now let g be an integral homology isomorphism. If A is empty, H0 (BB; Z) = 0 forces
B empty too, so there is nothing to prove. Otherwise H0 (Bg) being invertible implies
that the actual function π0 (BA) → π0 (BB) is bijective: it induces the stated isomorphism
between free abelian groups on those sets. This argument applies to arbitrary infinite sets of
components. The cofibre C = BK(g) is connected, because every component of BB meets
BA and hence is joined to the cone point. The augmented mapping-cone homology sequence
gives He ∗ (C; Z) = 0. The unreduced suspension of this connected CW complex is simply
connected by van Kampen and acyclic by the suspension homology sequence, hence weakly
contractible by Hurewicz. It follows that K(K(g) → e) → e is in W∞ . Apply the one-way
criterion first to K(g) → e and then to g. This proves the assertion using Loc (60 b) alone.
    The manuscript’s degenerate cube proves Loc (6 b)⇒Loc (60 b), so this also checks
R8. Alternatively its two-cone argument can be made insensitive to disconnectedness as
follows. For a nonempty homology equivalence Bg, the double suspension Σ2 Bg is a homology
equivalence between simply connected CW complexes, hence is a weak equivalence. Apply
the reverse cube to the map of two-cone covers of ΣBg, and then to the covers of Bg. At
each application the two cone maps have contractible source and target and so are in W∞ ;
the union map has already been shown to be in W . These two applications give g ∈ W .
This avoids silently applying the simply connected Whitehead theorem to a suspension of a
disconnected space.

6.1   Strict higher dimensions and the audit of 317
We write Mn for the structure asserted in 317, Ln = cn Sd2 , Rn = Ex2 Nn , and Bn X = |Nn X|.
Here n = ∞ means strict globular ω-categories. The symbols Wn in this subsection mean the
weak equivalences of Mn ; they do not mean the truncation localizers also denoted by Wn in
the historical discussion.
F.3a (Proved). Audit of the higher Thomason transfer in 317. The arguments
in the supplied source of 317 establish the proper combinatorial structure and the Quillen
equivalence Ln a Rn that are used below. The following audit records the interfaces on which
the transfer depends. It concerns the supplied mathematical proof; no Lean file was supplied
or checked.

Proof. The strict reflection τ≤n identifies parallel n-cells which bound (n + 1)-cells and then
imposes the resulting congruence. This is the left adjoint to inclusion of strict n-categories
in strict ω-categories. In particular it is a reflection, not the operation of forgetting cells
above dimension n. Section 2 of 317 applies the directed-chain construction to strong Steiner
complexes. The exact external results needed here are Steiner’s Theorem 5.11, giving the
equivalence on complexes with loop-free unital bases, and Theorem 6.1, giving the atomic
presentation, together with Ara–Maltsiniotis Theorem A.15 and A.16: the Gray tensor
extends the tensor on strong Steiner complexes, preserves all small colimits in each variable,
and has the two internal homs. These statements were opened in [2, 62]. The same appendix,
Proposition A.29, says that an internal hom into an n-category is an n-category. Thus the
reflected cylinder bijection used in 317 has the required codomain in every finite dimension;
it is not an assumption that nCat is closed under the unreflected Gray tensor.
     For completeness we checked the two chain calculations that turn this machinery into
relative homotopies. The positive Alexander–Whitney diagonal on an ordered simplex is
natural for all order maps, including degeneracies; composing with a map to the interval gives

                                              46
the simplicial homotopy induced by a right Gray cylinder. For the relative prism, if f ≤ g
agree on a sieve A ⊂ E and g is constant outside A, the cross term on a basis simplex is

                          h[w0 , . . . , wp ] = [f (w0 ), . . . , f (wp ), g(wp )].

Repeated entries are interpreted as zero. On the part lying in A this is zero in positive degree
and the interval factor is sent through its augmentation. On the remaining part, canceling the
alternating faces gives dh = hd + (−1)p (G − F ), with F, G the endpoint chain maps. This is
exactly the signed tensor differential. The remaining penultimate-face terms coincide because
g is constant on the new part and A is a sieve. Consequently the cylinder is stationary on the
entire higher subcategory Un (A), as required for gluing along an arbitrary attaching functor.
This checks Section 3, including its stationarity condition, rather than only the effect on
vertices.
     In Section 4 the slice functors taking the zero and one fibres of an n-category over [1]
preserve colimits: their right adjoints adjoin respectively a terminal object over one and an
initial object over zero. The object functor likewise preserves colimits, using the chaotic
category as its right adjoint. The initial and final vertex segments of an oriental are full; this
follows from the endpoints of its atomic generators and the retractions clamping vertices to
the segment. These facts compute the labelled pieces of a pushout even when its attaching
functor identifies objects. For a cover by full sieves, an oriental simplex lies in the member
containing its last vertex, since every vertex has an arrow to that vertex. The dual statement
uses the first vertex for cosieves. Thus Street nerves of these covers are actual simplicial
unions along monomorphisms. This verifies the gluing hypothesis used in the next two
sections.
     For Section 5, let A ⊂ E be a sieve with right adjoint r : E → A. If E − A has greatest
element x, the maps equal to the identity on A and constant respectively at x and r(x)
on the new part give the relative zigzag idE ≤ g ≥ k. The prism just checked remains
stationary after pushing out against any Un (A) → X, and gives the retraction homotopy
proving couniversality. For a finite new part, deleting two distinct maximal new elements gives
the two full pieces needed for induction and simplicial gluing. For an infinite new part, finite
subsets closed under ir form a filtered family; the attached category X is retained in every
member. Nerves preserve these filtered colimits because each oriental is finitely presented.
The same induction, starting with a greatest element, proves the unit N P → Nn cn N P for
every poset P .
     Section 6 applies this to the flag posets of subdivided boundaries and horns. Their
inclusions are sieves, and on the cosieve generated by an old flag the largest old subflag is the
stated right adjoint. First attaching this cosieve gives the couniversal weak equivalence. The
remaining pieces form full cosieve covers, so their nerves give the monomorphic gluing square.
It follows both that a pushed-out subdivided horn is a weak equivalence and that a boundary
attachment preserves a weak equivalence between the objects to which it is attached. These
are the two different assertions used for transfer and for left properness.
     The presentability appendix treats strict categories as models of a finitary essentially
algebraic theory. Its presentation construction simultaneously imposes equations and creates
compositions whenever the equations make their boundaries match. Thus it avoids the
incorrect procedure of merely quotienting a previously constructed free category. All formation
rules have finitely many premises, also in the countable-sorted ω-case. Finite presentations
therefore give a small dense set of finitely presentable objects, and filtered colimits are
computed cellwise. This supplies the smallness and filtered-colimit assertions used in Section 7.
     Finally Ln I-injectives and Ln J-injectives are respectively Rn−1 (TrivFib) and Rn−1 (Fib)
by adjunction. Relative Ln J-cell maps are weak equivalences by the horn assertion and
filtered-colimit closure. The small-object factorizations and the usual arrow-retract argument

                                                    47
consequently give both weak factorization systems. Right properness follows because Rn
preserves pullbacks and detects weak equivalences, and simplicial sets are right proper. Left
properness follows by the boundary-attachment assertion, transfinite composition and retracts.
The unit after Sd2 is proved by boundary induction, using the already checked poset unit on
each cell; composing with the subdivision comparison gives the derived adjunction unit. Since
Rn detects weak equivalences, this proves the Quillen equivalence. These are the contents of
317, Sections 2–7 and Appendix A, used here. At n = 1 the model structure and properness
also agree with Thomason’s Theorem 4.9 and Corollary 5.5 [65].

F.3b (Proved). The correspondence for set-generated localizations. Restriction
along Cat ⊂ nCat gives an inclusion-preserving bijection between the weak-equivalence classes
of left Bousfield localizations of Mn at small sets of maps and the accessible fundamental
localizers of Cat. For an accessible fundamental localizer W , the inverse is

                                   W (n) = {f : i∆ Nn (f ) ∈ W },                               (F.3.1)

where i∆ K = ∆/K is the category of simplices. Equivalently Bn (f ) belongs to the spatial
localization corresponding to W . The same assertion holds if “at small sets” is replaced by
“combinatorial”.

Proof. We use two precise existence statements. Cisinski’s Theorem 4.2.15 identifies
fundamental localizers modelled by a test category with its test localizers and preserves
accessibility. For the test category ∆, its inverse is i−1
                                                         ∆ ; the ordinary nerve gives the forward
class on Cat, by Remark 4.2.16 and the test-functor statement used there. Theorem 1.4.3
identifies accessible simplicial localizers with the cofibrantly generated model structures
having monomorphisms as cofibrations. Thus accessible fundamental localizers correspond
exactly to set-generated localizations of spaces. We use this specific form of [31].
    Barwick’s Theorem 4.7 states that a left proper combinatorial model category localized
at a small set of homotopy classes has a left proper combinatorial model structure, with the
original cofibrations, the fibrant local objects as fibrant objects, and the local equivalences as
weak equivalences [7]. The theorem uses derived mapping spaces and does not require the
original model category to be simplicial. It applies to Mn by F.3a.
    Let S be a small set of simplicial maps generating the spatial localization associated to W .
Every simplicial set is cofibrant, so the derived images of its maps under Ln are represented
by the actual maps Ln S. For a fibrant Z in Mn , the derived mapping adjunction gives

                              MaphMn (Ln K, Z) ≃ MaphsSet (K, Rn Z).

It follows, for each map in S and each such Z, that Z is Ln S-local exactly when Rn Z is
S-local. The Quillen equivalence in F.3a is essentially surjective on fibrant homotopy types.
Thus testing a map of Mn against every local object is exactly testing its image in spaces
against every S-local object. This proves formula (F.3.1) and its existence.
    Conversely, a set of maps in Mn represents a small set of maps in spaces under the
same equivalence. The mapping-space argument in the opposite direction proves that its
local-equivalence class is the inverse image of that spatial class. On an ordinary category,
Nn is exactly the ordinary nerve. The natural last-vertex comparison i∆ N A → A is a weak
equivalence; its compatibility with functors shows that the restriction in (F.3.1) is exactly
W . In particular no choices of replacements change this class. This proves surjectivity and
injectivity.
    For the last assertion, a combinatorial localization has a small set J 0 of generating trivial
cofibrations. Its fibrant objects are precisely the original fibrant objects local with respect to J 0 :

                                                  48
one direction follows from the lifting property, and the other from the characterization of local
equivalences by derived mapping spaces and the two weak factorization systems. Equivalently,
apply the standard generating-trivial-cofibration proof of [45, Proposition A.3.7.4], after
replacing the derived mapping spaces by cosimplicial resolutions. This proof uses no simplicial
enrichment of Mn . Hence this localization has the same weak equivalences as localization at
J 0.

F.3c (Refuted as an unrestricted ZFC assertion). A nonaccessible model
localization. Suppose the indexing universe U contains no measurable cardinal. In
every strict dimension 1 ≤ n ≤ ∞, there is a left Bousfield localization of Mn whose
weak-equivalence class restricts to a nonaccessible fundamental localizer of Cat. Thus the
unrestricted wording in Part F.3, which does not require the localized model structure to
be combinatorial, has a counterexample in this ZFC-compatible setting. Under Vopěnka’s
principle for the relevant U-classes, the unrestricted correspondence is a bijection. The
unconditional size-correct correspondence is F.3b.

Proof. Put Aλ = Zλ /Z<λ for infinite U-small cardinals λ, where Z<λ consists of the vectors
whose support has cardinality less than λ, and let A be their class. A group is reduced
when it admits no nontrivial homomorphism from any Aλ . Reduced groups are closed under
subgroups, products and extensions. For the extension assertion, a map Aλ → G into an
extension of two reduced groups first has trivial image in its quotient and then trivial image
in its kernel.
    For a U-small group G let
                       \
              R(G) =       {ker(a) : a : G → H, H reduced},       P G = G/R(G).

This construction is small: it suffices to use quotient images of G, and their normal kernels
form a subset of its power set. The diagonal embedding into the product of these reduced
quotients shows that P G is reduced. Every map from G to a reduced group factors uniquely
through P G, giving a functorial reflector. It applies to groupoids by quotienting each isotropy
group by its characteristic radical; conjugation carries these radicals to one another, so the
arrow torsors and composition descend.
   We need the following additional fact to produce an actual model structure, rather than
merely a homotopy reflector:
                                       R(R(G)) = R(G).                                    (F.3.3)
Indeed R(R(G)) is characteristic in the normal subgroup R(G), and hence normal in G. The
quotient G/R(R(G)) is an extension of the reduced groups P (R(G)) and P G, so is reduced.
The universal property of R(G) forces R(G) ⊆ R(R(G)). The reverse inclusion is immediate.
Thus P (R(G)) = 1.
         (n)
   Let Π1 X be the fundamental groupoid obtained by reflecting a strict n-category to an
ordinary category and inverting all arrows, and regard
                                                    (n)
                                       Qn X = P Π1 X

as a strict n-category concentrated in invertible dimensions zero and one. There is a natural
strict functor X → Qn X. The zero, one and two simplices of the Street nerve give exactly
the same presentation of the fundamental groupoid: its edges are the 1-cells, and a triangular
2-simplex imposes the relation between its two composite boundary paths. Consequently
                                     (n)
                       Π1 (Bn X) ≃ Π1 X,        Bn Qn X ≃ N P Π1 (Bn X).


                                               49
The functor Qn preserves Wn , and its unit is idempotent up to the canonical isomorphism.
Its local homotopy types are precisely the 1-types with reduced fundamental groups.
    We apply the following exact form of the Bousfield–Friedlander criterion, Stanculescu’s
Theorem 1.1 [61]. For a model category and a coaugmented endofunctor Q, assume that
Q preserves weak equivalences, that QηX is a weak equivalence and ηQX is monic in the
homotopy category, and that Q-equivalences are preserved by pullback along original fibrations
between original fibrant Q-local objects. Then there is a left Bousfield model localization with
the original cofibrations and the Q-equivalences as weak equivalences. No small generating
family is assumed. We opened the corrected primary version, including its factorization proof.
    The first two hypotheses were just verified. For the third, an original fibration between
fibrant objects has strict pullbacks which Rn = Ex2 Nn identifies with homotopy pullbacks
of spaces. It therefore suffices to check the asserted property for a homotopy pullback of a
Q-equivalence X → B along E → B, where E, B are local 1-types. Work on one component
of E. The map on fundamental groups g : G → H induced by X → B is a surjection
                                                                 ∼
with kernel R(G): since H is reduced it factors as G → P G −     → H. Surjectivity makes the
homotopy pullback connected over this component. Since π2 B = 0, its fundamental group is

                                G ×H K −→ K,          K = π1 E.

This projection is surjective with kernel R(G). Every map from this pullback group to a
reduced group kills its kernel, by (F.3.3), and hence factors uniquely through K. Since K
is reduced, applying P to the projection is an isomorphism. The same argument on each
component proves the required Q-equivalence. The criterion now constructs the promised
model localization of Mn . In particular its existence does not rely on an unproved transfer
of a proper-class reflector to a model structure.
    It remains to verify that the restriction WQ to Cat is a fundamental localizer and is
nonaccessible. Mapping into all reduced groupoids detects P Π1 -equivalences, by the reflector
universal property and Yoneda for groupoids. These are exactly the derived mapping-space
tests against the local 1-types. For any fixed target space such tests define a fundamental
localizer: the two elementary axioms are immediate, and relative Theorem A follows from
BA ≃ hocolims∈S B(A/s) and the corresponding formula for B, since mapping into the fixed
target turns this homotopy colimit into a homotopy limit. Intersecting these classes over the
local targets proves that WQ is fundamental.
    Proposition 6.2 of Casacuberta–Scevenels–Smith states that the absence of measurable
cardinals is equivalent to Z being A-reduced [24]. The statement relativizes to U: a nonzero
map Aλ → Z would produce a measurable cardinal at most λ. Therefore every WQ -map
induces isomorphisms on integral cohomology in degrees zero and one. Degree zero follows
from the bijection of components; degree one follows, on each component, from Hom(P G, Z) =
Hom(G, Z).
    Suppose a U-small set S generated WQ . Choose an uncountable regular κ ∈ U larger than
the total number of simplices in the sources and targets of N S. The functor Tκ (B) = B κ /B <κ
on abelian groups is exact: both products and the union of the bounded-support subproducts
preserve short exact sequences, and the quotient does so by the snake lemma. If a free chain
complex C∗ has fewer than κ basis elements in total, the interchange of its two coordinate
sets gives
                                Hom(C∗ , Aκ ) ∼
                                              = Tκ Hom(C∗ , Z).
For the kernel assertion here, the union of the supports of fewer than κ basis values still has
size less than κ; this is exactly where regularity is used. Thus H j (C∗ ; Aκ ) ∼
                                                                                = Tκ H j (C∗ ; Z).
Applying this in degrees zero and one to N S shows that Z = K(Aκ , 1) is S-local. Indeed its
mapping spaces have components H 1 (−; Aκ ), fundamental groups H 0 (−; Aκ ), and no higher
groups; all their components are translates.

                                               50
    The fixed-target test against Z is a fundamental localizer containing S, so it must contain
WQ . But the one-object groupoid Aκ → ∗ belongs to WQ , because P Aκ = 1. Its mapping-
space test against Z fails: the identity of K(Aκ , 1) and the constant map induce different
maps on π1 , since Aκ 6= 0. This contradiction proves nonaccessibility. It also gives a direct
bounded proof of the required form of the obstruction in [24, Theorems 6.3–6.4].
    Finally an arbitrary model localization has its original-fibrant local objects as a full
homotopy subcategory, and its weak equivalences are detected by derived mapping spaces
into them. This follows using the same cofibrant replacements and original cosimplicial
resolutions: the cofibrations and trivial fibrations have not changed, and an original resolution
remains a resolution in the localized structure. After the equivalence with spaces in F.3a,
restriction to Cat is therefore a fundamental localizer by the preceding relative-Theorem-A
argument. Under the class form of Vopěnka used in Part H, Cisinski’s Scholie 4.2.17 makes
it accessible. F.3b then supplies its set-generated higher model, and the mapping-space
tests make the weak-equivalence classes equal. This proves the positive assertion under that
principle. The two additional set-theoretic hypotheses are being distinguished explicitly;
automatic accessibility in ZFC has not been inserted into the statement of 317.

F.3d (Proved). Reversibility and spectral classes transport exactly. A full sieve of
a strict n-category is the full subcategory on a set of objects closed under taking the source of
a 1-cell whose target is in that set. A two-sieve Mayer–Vietoris square consists of a cover
X3 = X1 ∪ X2 of this kind and X0 = X1 ∩ X2 . For a map of two such squares, higher Loc (6 b)
is the assertion
                              f1 , f2 , f3 ∈ W (n) =⇒ f0 ∈ W (n) .
For the localizers in F.3b this holds exactly when W satisfies ordinary Loc (6 b). For a
localizing subcategory L of spectra put
                               Rn (L) = {f : Σ∞ C(Bn f ) ∈ L}.                           (F.3.2)
Then W (n) = Rn (L) if and only if W = R(L). Therefore the accessible classification question,
including the negative answer of Part B, has the same answer in every strict dimension.

Proof. The last-vertex argument in the audit shows that applying Nn to a two-sieve cover
gives a union of simplicial subsets. Applying i∆ gives a cover by full ordinary sieves: every
face or degeneracy of a simplex lying in one member still lies in that member. Thus a higher
Mayer–Vietoris cube becomes an ordinary Mayer–Vietoris cube, with its four comparison
functors exactly i∆ Nn fi . Formula (F.3.1) proves the implication from ordinary Loc (6 b) to
the higher version. Conversely ordinary categories and their full sieves are special cases of
the higher ones, so restriction proves the reverse implication. This argument also proves the
correspondence for the forward gluing axiom and for the reverse single-square axiom, without
mistaking an arbitrary algebraic pushout for a homotopy pushout.
    The category-of-simplices comparison gives a natural weak equivalence B(i∆ Nn X) ≃ Bn X.
It is natural in the specified map f , hence induces an equivalence of its homotopy cofibers,
including the convention for empty sources. Formula (F.3.2) is consequently exactly the
inverse-nerve class of R(L). Restriction gives necessity, and (F.3.1) gives sufficiency. In
particular applying F.3b to the accessible counterexample of Part B produces the corresponding
counterexample in every dimension. No assertion that arbitrary Rn (L) is set-generated is
needed for this equivalence.

F.3e (Proved). What properness gives for the fibration axioms. For every
1 ≤ n ≤ ∞, right properness in 317 implies Loc (7 F ) for Wn , with strict pullbacks and with
                   FibWn = {p : every base change along p preserves Wn }.

                                               51
For n = 1, W1 = W∞ also satisfies Grothendieck’s stronger Loc (7) and Loc (7 D). Their
proof uses the simplex test for the ordinary nerve, in addition to properness. In higher
dimensions, the direct conclusion of the proper model structure is the same Loc (7 F ); 317
supplies no higher definition of the Cat-fibration or perfect-functor classes occurring in the
literal other two axioms.

Proof. Every model fibration lies in FibWn by right properness. Factor any strict n-functor
as a model trivial cofibration followed by a model fibration. This gives a Wn -map followed
by a member of FibWn , the existence assertion in Loc (7 F ). Composition and strict base
change preserve this class by its definition. Every map X → ∗ is in it, because the Street
nerve preserves products and products with a fixed simplicial set preserve weak equivalences.
Isomorphisms are in it; pullbacks of its maps and of its weak equivalences exist and have the
required properties. Finally factoring the diagonal provides the path-object axiom. These
verifications give the stated Brown category of fibrations, including the terminal and empty
cases.
    For ordinary categories, let p : E → B satisfy Grothendieck’s weak-fibration test (Ff),
XVI ms. 27: pullback preserves weak equivalences between categorically contractible bases.
Every ordinal category [m] is contractible. Hence, for every simplex [n] → B and order map
[m] → [n], (Ff) gives a weak equivalence

                                    E ×B [m] −→ E ×B [n].

Since N [m] = ∆[m] and N preserves pullbacks, these are exactly the simplex pullback tests in
Rezk’s Theorem 4.1(4), specialized to the topos of sets [58]. That theorem makes N p sharp,
which by definition means that all its base changes preserve weak equivalences. Taking nerves
again shows p ∈ FibW∞ . Thus FibweakW∞ ⊆ FibW∞ , the strongest weak-fibration formulation of
Loc (7) at XVI ms. 32. The Cat-fibrations with homotopically constant transports and the
perfect functors are subclasses of this weak-fibration class, so the displayed inclusion gives
Loc (7) in its Cat-fibration formulation and Loc (7 D). For the former subclass one can check
(Ff) directly by the Grothendieck construction of the fibre diagram; for the latter it is the
smooth-and-proper base-change test in the definition of perfect functors. Thus no claim that
an arbitrary model fibration is a Grothendieck Cat-fibration is being made.
    For n > 1, an oriental is not the ordinal category [m], and Nn Om is not ∆[m]. Thus
repeating the preceding proof with orientals would replace the actual simplex pullbacks by
different ones and would require a new comparison theorem. Moreover the manuscript’s
classes CatfibW and Parf W are classes of ordinary functors, not definitions on strict n-functors.
If they are transported by the ordinary category-of- simplices construction, the corresponding
nerve-based statements follow from the preceding paragraph by definition; that does not
identify an intrinsic higher Cat-fibration class. The exact dimension-independent implication
of 317 itself is therefore the proved Loc (7 F ). Left properness gives pushout invariance
along model cofibrations, and supplies no additional assertion about those stronger fibration
classes.


7    Stable derivators and unstable suspension

G.1 (Proved). Every strong stable derivator has a stable cofiber localizer. For
every strong stable derivator D, with the spectrum action specified below, the class

                    LD = {V ∈ Ho(Sp) : V ⊗ M = 0 for every M ∈ D(e)}



                                               52
is a localizing subcategory and
                                        WD = R(LD ).
Consequently the reversible localizer WS of Part A is not the constant-coefficient localizer of
any strong stable derivator.

Proof. We use the canonical action of the derivator of spectra. Cisinski–Tabuada, [33,
Theorem A.11 and Appendix A.3], give the following precise universal property: for a small
category A and a triangulated derivator D, restriction along the spectral Yoneda embedding
identifies cocontinuous morphisms HO(SptA ) → D with D(Aop ). For A = e it assigns to M
the cocontinuous morphism V 7→ V ⊗ M taking the sphere spectrum to M . The theorem is
A.11 in the published paper; the opened arXiv version numbers the same statement A.15.
The paper’s strong triangulated derivators include the strong stable derivators considered
here. No presentation of D by a model category is assumed.
    This action is exact and preserves arbitrary coproducts in the spectrum variable. Its
kernel for a fixed M is therefore triangulated and closed under coproducts, and intersecting
these kernels gives the claimed localizing subcategory. For a small category A, preservation
of homotopy colimits identifies

                       (Σ∞ (BA)+ ) ⊗ M ∼
                                       = (pA )! p∗A M,      pA : A → e.

Indeed the spectrum on the left is the homotopy colimit over A of the constant sphere
spectrum, and its action takes this diagram to the constant diagram of value M . In the
presheaf convention one uses Aop ; canonical reversal of a nerve simplex gives the natural
homeomorphism of classifying spaces needed to write the displayed formula with BA.
   For u : A → B, adjunction identifies the comparison on morphisms between constants
with
                   HomD(e) ((pB )! p∗B M, N ) −→ HomD(e) ((pA )! p∗A M, N ).
Thus the definition at I ms. 61 says, by Yoneda and for every M , that the specified map

                            Σ∞ (BA)+ ⊗ M −→ Σ∞ (BB)+ ⊗ M

is invertible. Its cofiber is Σ∞ C(Bu) ⊗ M , since both suspension spectra and the action
preserve the indicated homotopy pushout. In a stable category a map is invertible exactly
when its cofiber is zero. This is the asserted equality, including empty categories and
disconnected spaces. Part A proves that WS is not fixed by RT , whereas every R(L) is fixed
by RT by the Galois identities checked there; hence WS cannot be WD .

G.2 (Proved). Every accessible fundamental localizer has an ordinary coefficient
realization. Let W be an accessible fundamental localizer, in the relative-Theorem-A
convention. The derivator HOTW of W -local spaces satisfies

                                        WHOTW = W.

The realizing derivator need not be stable.

Proof. Cisinski’s Theorems 4.2.15 and 1.4.3 construct the corresponding accessible localization
of simplicial sets; let L : S → SW be its reflector. First W is stable under product with
any small category. For f : A → B and C, apply relative Theorem A over C to f × C. Its
comma-category comparison at c is f × (C/c), whose projection to f is an objectwise weak
equivalence because C/c has a terminal object. Two out of three supplies the hypotheses.


                                              53
Passage through the test-category correspondence gives the same statement for products
with spaces.
     If Z is local, then Map(K, Z) is local for every space K: testing a map f ∈ W against
it is the same as testing f × K against Z. In particular each mapping space between local
objects is local. A map in W consequently induces bijections on all hom-sets between constant
diagrams in HOTW . Conversely, taking the first constant to be the terminal space gives, for
every local Z, the bijection
                                     [BB, Z] −→ [BA, Z].
By the localization adjunction these are the maps [LBB, Z] → [LBA, Z]. Yoneda in Ho(SW )
makes the specified LBu an isomorphism, which is precisely u ∈ W . This checks the previously
asserted realization rather than assuming that every abstract derivator comes from localized
spaces. The construction also covers the total localizer.

G.3 (Proved). Suspension for the sphere localizer is conservative, neither faithful
nor full. In the pointed WS -homotopy category, suspension is conservative, neither faithful
nor full. Thus it has the same behavior in any pointed derivator of WS -local spaces, as in the
question. These assertions also hold for the accessible smallest reversible localizer containing
RP ∞ → ∗ constructed in Part B.

Proof. For a reversible localizer, the cofiber criterion and desuspension closure give, for every
pointed map f ,
                                    Σf ∈ W ⇐⇒ f ∈ W,
since C(Σf ) ≃ ΣC(f ). Every arrow in the formal pointed localization is represented by a roof
   f     w
X− →Z←    − Y with w ∈ W . To see the existence of such roofs, replace a map to be pushed out
by its mapping-cylinder cofibration and use the homotopy pushout. Cobase change preserves
W , because it preserves the homotopy cofiber. This moves any inverse in a finite zigzag
past the following forward arrow; iteration produces the claimed roof. If its suspension is
invertible, strong saturation says Σf ∈ W , and the displayed implication gives f ∈ W . For
WS , strong saturation also follows directly from its mapping-space description established
below; for the accessible localizer of Part B it follows from its localization model. Equivalently,
in a local-space presentation, derived suspension is LΣ, and the same computation on a
representing map proves conservativity directly.
   We need an actual local target to detect failure of faithfulness. For an unpointed map
f : X → Y in WS , apply the pointed mapping-space functor to the cofiber sequence of
f+ : X+ → Y+ . Cofiber rotation gives the fibre sequence
                             n                     n                      n
                Map∗ (ΣY+ , S(2) ) −→ Map∗ (ΣX+ , S(2) ) −→ Map∗ (C(f ), S(2) ).

The last term is contractible, so the first arrow is an equivalence on all components. By
adjunction this is the map Map(Y, ΩS(2) n ) → Map(X, ΩS n ). Hence ΩS n is W -local for
                                                          (2)            (2)    S
every n ≥ 2. By the James fibre sequence checked in A.1,
                            n        n+1      2n+1

and closure of local objects under homotopy limits, S(2)
WS equals the class of maps inducing equivalences on mapping spaces into every 2-local
sphere: one implication has just been established, and the other follows by taking the based
fibre at the constant map. G.6–G.7 supply the separate accessibility argument for this whole
class.


                                                54
    Consider the Whitehead square a = [ι2 , ι2 ] : S 3 → S 2 . Its composite with S 2 → S(2)2 is

nonzero: π3 (S 2 ) = Z is generated by the Hopf map η, and a = ±2η, so localization gives
the nonzero element ±2 ∈ Z(2) . For clarity, the coefficient two can be read directly from
the attaching map of the top cell of S 2 × S 2 : after folding the two 2-sphere summands, the
square of the degree-two cohomology generator has coefficient two. Its Hopf invariant is
therefore two. The Hopf fibration identifies π3 (S 2 ) with π3 (S 3 ) = Z, and its generator has
Hopf invariant one. These are the specific classical sphere facts used here; see [37, Example
4.51, pp. 380–381, and the proof of Corollary 4J.4, p. 474].
            2 is local, maps into it factor through the W -localization, so the image of a
    Since S(2)                                                S
there is not zero. But Σa = 0. Indeed the Whitehead product is the attaching map in
the cofiber sequence S 3 → S 2 ∨ S 2 → S 2 × S 2 ; after suspension the inclusion of the wedge
has a homotopy retraction, given by the two suspended projections and the suspension
comultiplication. Exactness of this cofiber sequence makes the suspended attaching map null,
and composing with the fold gives Σa = 0. Thus suspension is not faithful. For the smaller
accessible reversible localizer from Part B, the same sphere remains local because its weak
equivalences form a subclass of WS , and the same argument applies.
    Fullness already fails in the formal localization; a right adjoint to suspension need not
be assumed for this conclusion. Let i : S 2 → C(a) be the cofiber inclusion. Since Σa is
nullhomotopic, the cofiber of Σa splits as ΣS 2 ∨ Σ2 S 3 . Under the natural identification
C(Σa) ≃ ΣC(a), projection onto the first summand gives a homotopy retraction r : ΣC(a) →
ΣS 2 of Σi. If localized suspension were full, there would be a morphism g : C(a) → S 2 with
Σg = r. Consequently Σ(gi) = id, and conservativity would make gi invertible. But ia = 0 in
the cofiber sequence. It would follow that a = (gi)−1 g(ia) = 0, contradicting the preceding
paragraph. This proves nonfullness independently of the reflector construction established in
G.4 and G.6–G.7.
    The comparison with R9 is now precise. Its nontriviality hypothesis applies, since S(2)   2

is a noncontractible local object. We confirm the step of GALOIS B.6 used here. In this
pointed coefficient category, the identity and zero endomorphisms of S(2)    2 are distinct. For

a nontrivial acyclic group G, the coherent free G-object obtained by left Kan extension
from the unique object of BG has regular permutation action on the coproduct indexed by
G. Assigning the identity to one summand and zero to the others detects a nonidentity
permutation. Thus it cannot be constant, and BG → ∗ is not a locally constant coefficient
equivalence. Its suspension is weakly contractible, since BG is connected and integrally
acyclic. The map of double-cone covers from BG to the point therefore has both cone maps
and the total map in W lc , but not its intersection map, refuting Loc (6 b). The map of covers
(BG, BG, Cw , Cw ) → (BG, Cv , Cw , ΣBG) has its intersection and total maps in W lc , while
BG → Cv is not, refuting Loc (6 c). These are precisely the two separate countercubes in
GALOIS B.6. Hence its locally constant coefficient localizer fails both axioms even though
the underlying WS -homotopy suspension is conservative. The cofiber tests defining WS must
not be identified with this locally constant coefficient class.

G.4 (Proved). The full sphere localizer has a small-universe reflector. The entire
WS admits an accessible reflective localization on SU in ZFC, and therefore the pointed
derivator requested in Part G exists. The proof is G.6–G.7 below. Consequently every
conclusion of G.3 applies to that actual derivator without an additional existence assumption.
   The identification of WS as coorthogonality to the set of 2-local spheres, by itself, does
not prove accessibility. G.6 separates the rational and completed-sphere conditions; G.7
constructs compact Kan models of the completed spheres and proves a countable subcomplex
bound, to which B.2 applies.

G.5 (Proved). A non-total accessible reversible localizer with full stable part.
There is a non-total accessible reversible fundamental localizer W∗ such that

                         T (W∗ ) = Ho(Sp),      RT (W∗ ) = Mor(Cat).

One may take the smallest reversible localizer containing the two maps

                               BZ/2 −→ ∗,           BZ[1/2] −→ ∗.

In particular its intersection with finite spectra is the entire finite stable homotopy category,
while W∗ is not fixed by RT .

Proof. Write Sb2n for the Bousfield–Kan 2-completion of S n . We first supply the completion
comparison needed for the homotopy fibre sequences

                            Sb2n −→ ΩSb2n+1 −→ ΩSb22n+1 ,     n ≥ 2.                         (4)

Let F be the homotopy fibre of the integral James–Hopf map H : ΩS n+1 → ΩS 2n+1 . The
usual nullhomotopy of HE gives a map S n → F . The precise 2-local EHP theorem checked
in A.1 says that this map becomes an equivalence after localization at 2, hence is a mod-2
homology equivalence. All three spaces F , ΩS n+1 and ΩS 2n+1 are simply connected and have
finitely generated homotopy groups: the loop-space assertions follow from Serre finiteness,
and those for F follow from the fibre long exact sequence (for simple connectedness also use
π2 ΩS 2n+1 = 0). These are exactly the finite nilpotence hypotheses in [51, Theorem 4.5.2
and Theorem 11.2.6]. The latter theorem, including its final simply connected-base clause
on page 220, therefore identifies the completion of F with the fibre of the completed map
H. Proposition 11.2.4 of the same source identifies the completions of the connected loop
spaces with the loop spaces of the completed spheres. Finally S n → F induces an equivalence
on completions by Theorems 11.1.1–11.1.2, since it is a mod-2 homology equivalence. This
proves (4) with the specified maps. Equivalently, their homotopy-group comparison can be
checked by the natural formula πi (X2∧ ) = πi (X) ⊗ Z2 and exactness of tensoring with the
torsion-free group Z2 ; Theorem 11.1.2 and Lemma 10.1.4 justify that formula under precisely
the finiteness conditions just verified.
    Define
                   c2 = {X ∈ Ho(S∗ ) : Map (X, Sbn ) ≃ ∗ for every n ≥ 2}.
                   N                        ∗     2

It is closed under pointed homotopy colimits. Sequence (4) shows that it is closed under
desuspension: if ΣX is in the class, both mapping spaces into the loop-space terms are
contractible, and their homotopy fibre is Map∗ (X, Sb2n ). Suspension closure follows directly
by adjunction. Cofiber rotation then gives two out of three for cofiber sequences. Thus the
checked construction A.1–A.3 applies and gives a reversible fundamental localizer
                                    c2 = {f : C(f ) ∈ N
                                    W                 c2 }.

The completed spheres are actual W   c2 -local objects: repeat the cofiber-rotation argument
in G.3 to obtain locality of their loop spaces, and then use (4). In particular this is an
all-component mapping-space assertion, not merely a test on the fibre over the constant map.
    The completion map S n → Sb2n is a mod-2 homology equivalence, so Sb2n is nilpotent with
mod-2 cohomology in only degrees 0, n. Miller’s Theorem C, in the exact form verified
in A.4, puts BZ/2 in N  c2 . Also BZ[1/2] is the mapping telescope of the degree-two self
          1
maps of S . Its reduced mod-2 homology vanishes, since in degree one the direct system
      0     0
is F2 −

Theorems 11.1.1–11.1.2]; hence Map∗ (BZ[1/2], Sb2n ) is contractible. Both generators therefore

          c2 .
   Let W∗ be their smallest reversible closure. Part B proves that it is accessible, and
minimality gives W∗ ⊆ Wc2 . Obstruction theory on the two-dimensional complex RP 2 gives

                               [RP 2 , Sb22 ]∗ ∼
                                               = H 2 (RP 2 ; Z2 ) ∼
                                                                  = Z/2,

using π1 Sb22 = 0 and π2 Sb22 = Z2 . Thus RP 2 is not in Nc2 , and RP 2 → ∗ is not in W  c2 by
suspension and desuspension closure. This proves that W∗ is non-total.
    Its stable part nevertheless contains both S/2 and S[1/2]. The first follows from BZ/2 → ∗
by the independently checked Kahn–Priddy argument of A.5. For the second, suspension
spectra preserve the circle telescope and give

                                      Σ∞ BZ[1/2] ≃ ΣS[1/2].

The cofibres of the two generating maps have one further suspension, which is immaterial
in a localizing subcategory. To see directly that these two spectra generate every spectrum,
the cofiber of S → S[1/2] is the homotopy colimit of the cofibres of 2r : S → S. Each such
cofiber S/2r belongs to loc(S/2), by induction using the triangles for successive powers of two.
The cofiber triangle therefore puts S in loc(S/2, S[1/2]), and the sphere generates Ho(Sp).
Hence T (W∗ ) = Ho(Sp) and RT (W∗ ) is the total localizer, whereas W∗ is non-total as proved
above.

G.6 (Proved). The sphere localizer is the intersection of completed-sphere and
rational equivalences. Every rational Eilenberg–Mac Lane space K(Q, d), d ≥ 0, is
WS -local. Consequently, with N

    The group D2 is a nonzero rational vector space. Multiplication by an odd integer is
invertible on both groups in the quotient. Multiplication by two on D2 is onto because one
may subtract an integer from a 2-adic integer to make it divisible by two; it is one-to-one
because 2a ∈ Z(2) and a ∈ Z2 imply a ∈ Q ∩ Z2 = Z(2) . Finally D2 6= 0, since Z2 is
uncountable and Z(2) is countable. Choose a one-dimensional rational direct summand. The
induced maps make K(Q, n − 2) a homotopy retract of Pn , so it is local. Odd n can be
arbitrarily large. Taking loop spaces, which are homotopy limits of local objects, gives locality
of K(Q, d) for every d ≥ 0.
    It follows that every map in WS is a rational homology equivalence. Indeed locality of all
these Eilenberg–Mac Lane spaces gives isomorphisms on ordinary rational cohomology in all
nonnegative degrees. The universal coefficient theorem over the field Q identifies these maps
with the linear duals of the corresponding homology maps; a linear map of vector spaces is an
isomorphism if its dual is, including for infinite-dimensional vector spaces. Thus WS ⊆ WQ .
If X ∈ N, A.3 makes X → ∗ a WS -equivalence, and so X is rationally acyclic.

G.7 (Proved). The full sphere localizer is accessible in ZFC. The null classes of

Consequently both W  c2 and WS are accessible reversible fundamental localizers in ZFC. A

countable CW complexes whose cofibers lie in its displayed null class. In particular WS itself
is the accessible counterexample asked for in Part B, and the pointed localization derivator in
Part G exists in the stipulated small universe.

Proof. We give a strict compact model and a compactness argument for small subcomplexes.
The proof does not identify the homotopy category of an arrow category with the arrow
category of a homotopy category.
    Compact Kan models. Let K = ∆n /∂∆n , n ≥ 2, and let R be the reduced free simplicial
F2 -module functor, regarded as a monad on pointed simplicial sets. The standard Bousfield–
Kan resolution has cosimplicial terms

                                   X q = Rq+1 K        (q ≥ 0).

The precise construction and its identification with F2 -completion are in [18, §7.7,
pp. 1032–1033]. Each simplicial degree of each X q is finite, since K has finitely many
simplices in each degree and a free vector space on a finite set over F2 is finite. Also each X q
is a Kan complex, being a simplicial abelian group.
    Here is a direct check that this cosimplicial resolution is Reedy fibrant. Its codegeneracies
si are linear for the outer R-module structures, as are all cofaces di with i ≥ 1. A matching
tuple in degree q is determined by compatible elements (a0 , . . . , aq−1 ) in degree q − 1. Start
with c = 0. If si c = ai for i < j, replace c by

                                       c + dj+1 (aj − sj c).

The identities sj dj+1 = id and si dj+1 = dj si for i < j show that this corrects the jth equation
and preserves the previous ones: the latter assertion uses si aj = sj−1 ai . This construction
is linear and simplicial. Thus each matching map is a split surjection of simplicial abelian
groups, hence a Kan fibration. In cosimplicial degree zero the assertion is just the Kan
property already noted.
     Totalization is right Quillen for the Reedy structure; see the statement immediately
before [18, Proposition 8.1, p. 1034], or check the horn lifting adjunction against the Reedy
cofibrations ∆• ⊗ Λk [m] → ∆• ⊗ ∆[m]. Therefore

                                       Zn = Tot(R•+1 K)

is Kan and is a model of Sb2n . In simplicial degree m, its set of simplices is the closed
compatibility subspace of    Y
                                 HomsSet (∆q × ∆m , Rq+1 K).
                                q≥0

Each factor is finite: the product of the two standard simplices has finitely many nondegenerate
simplices. Give each factor the discrete topology. The displayed product is compact metrizable,
its compatibility equations are closed, and all face and degeneracy operators are continuous.
Thus Zn is a simplicial compact metrizable Hausdorff space with underlying Kan complex.
This argument supplies an actual strict simplicial model, not just compact topologies on
homotopy classes.


                                                59
   A bounded extension lemma. Suppose Z is any pointed Kan complex whose levels have
compact metrizable Hausdorff topologies and continuous simplicial operators. For a pointed
simplicial set A, the set
                               HZ (A) = HomsSet∗ (A, Z)
is a closed subspace of the product of the target levels indexed by simplices of A, hence is
compact Hausdorff. For countable A it is metrizable. Restrictions are continuous.
    Fix a pointed simplicial set X and a countable pointed simplicial subset A ⊆ X. Let CA
be the image of HZ (X) → HZ (A). This is compact and closed. For A ⊆ L ⊆ X obtained by
adjoining finitely many nondegenerate simplices and all their faces, let CL,A be the image of
HZ (L) → HZ (A). Then                        \
                                       CA = CL,A .                                        (6)
                                                L

Indeed a compatible assignment on A belongs to every image on the right precisely when
every finite set of additional simplex equations can be solved. In the compact product of all
the target coordinates indexed by X, those equations and the prescribed coordinates on A
are closed conditions with the finite intersection property. Compactness gives a simultaneous
solution, which is the required map X → Z.
    Choose a countable basis of HZ (A). The basic open sets whose closures miss CA cover
its complement: compact metrizable spaces are regular, so the basis may be chosen to have
this property. For each such open U , equation (6) and compactness of U show that finitely
many of the sets CL,A already have intersection disjoint from U . Their finite union of added
simplices gives one countable enlargement LU with this property. The union of these LU is a
countable enlargement A0 ⊆ X such that

                                 im(HZ (A0 ) → HZ (A)) = CA .                                 (7)

The same construction can be performed simultaneously for a countable family of such targets
by taking the countable union of the enlargements.
   Apply this to the countable family

where m = 0 means Zn,0 = Zn . These are Kan models of Ωm Zn and again have compact

at any countable A0 ⊆ X, iterate (7) to obtain countable

Every map h : A∞ → Zn,m extends to X. To see this, its restriction to Ai extends globally
