The phrase a-equivalence in (3) means the explicit Hom test (H5.0.1). Thus this is an equality
describing FibWa on all functors, with no restriction to coverings or to finite categories.

Proof. We first record why condition (1) handles strictness. For a simplex σ : ∆[n] → K
over N B, set Pσ = N E ×N B ∆[n]. The augmentation hocolim∆/K Pσ → N E ×N B K is a
weak equivalence, by the simplex resolution used in E.3. For each target BH, (1) says that
the contravariant diagram Map(Pσ , BH) has equivalences as transition maps. Its homotopy
limit is the space of sections of a locally constant diagram over |K|. A weak equivalence of
bases induces an equivalence of these section spaces. Thus strict pullback preserves weak
equivalences in Wa . Pulling back a path fibration from a Kan replacement of N B identifies
strict and homotopy fibers in Wa . A comparison with an ordinary fibration replacement,
simplex by simplex and then by the same homotopy-limit formula, identifies every strict
pullback with its derived pullback in Wa . The last-vertex comparison for categories of
simplices therefore transfers the intrinsic property between categorical bases and arbitrary
spaces over BB.
    For a space map e : X → Y , first truncation gives a comparison

                                 Π1 (X ×hY Z) −→ Π1 X ×hΠ1 Y Π1 Z.                        (H5.6.4)

It is bijective on components and surjective on each isotropy group. Its kernel is the image of
the appropriate boundary from π2 Y ; this follows either from the homotopy exact sequence,
or from the path model of both pullbacks. If (2) holds, every representation of the isotropy
of the left side in an allowed H kills this kernel: restrict it first to the corresponding fiber
π1 Fb . Thus (H5.6.4) is a Wa -equivalence for every Z → Y .
     The homotopy pullback of j BGj → BH along BK → BH has exactly the component set
                                 `

in the target of (H5.6.2), and its isotropy at the representative h is the target group in (H5.6.3).
Consequently (3) says precisely that these groupoid pullbacks preserve all a-equivalences.
Componentwise decomposition covers disconnected groupoids as well. Conditions (1)–(3),
(H5.6.4), and two out of three now prove sufficiency.
     For necessity, an intrinsic p satisfies (1) by pulling back [0] → [n]. Hence it has the derived
preservation property just proved. Pull it back over the universal cover of a component of BB.
The inclusion of its basepoint is a W1 -equivalence, so its pullback gives (2), by the homotopy
exact sequence. Finally any groupoid map R → Q is realized over BB by BB ×hBQ BR → BB;
its first truncation is R. A Wa -equivalence R0 → R gives a Wa -equivalence of these base spaces.
Their total pullbacks are BE ×hBQ BR0 and BE ×hBQ BR, whose fundamental groupoids are
the corresponding groupoid pullbacks, because BQ is a 1-type. Intrinsic preservation yields
exactly (H5.6.2)–(H5.6.3).

H5.7 (Refuted). Local groups alone do not classify all larger localizers. The
assignment W 7→ {G : BG is W -local} is not injective, even on accessible fundamental

                                                 70
localizers containing W1 . Three different such localizers have exactly the trivial group as a
local group.

Proof. The first is W0 , the maps bijective on components, generated by S 1 → ∗. Its
local spaces are discrete sets. The second is W−1 , the maps preserving emptiness versus
nonemptiness, generated by S 0 → ∗. Indeed locality means that the diagonal Z → Z × Z is
an equivalence; its solutions are the empty space and the contractible spaces. Maps detected
by them are exactly those preserving emptiness. The third is the all-map localizer, generated
by ∅ → ∗, with contractible local spaces. In each case BG is local exactly when G = 1,
whereas
                                  W1 ⊂ W0 ( W−1 ( Fl(Cat).                           (H5.7.1)
For completeness W−1 satisfies relative Theorem A directly. In a triangle A → B → C,
suppose every A/c → B/c preserves emptiness. If B is nonempty, choose b ∈ B and let c
be its image; then B/c is nonempty, hence so are A/c and A. If B is empty then so is A.
Weak saturation and the terminal-object axiom are immediate. This checks the convention
of fundamental localizer in this paper.

H5.8 (Proved). The accessible component-preserving correspondence. On
the interval W1 ⊆ W ⊆ W0 , accessible fundamental localizers correspond exactly to small
orthogonality classes of groups. Equivalently they correspond to full reflective, accessibly
embedded subcategories of groups. A localizer associated to D has local spaces the arbitrary
disjoint unions of BG with G ∈ D, and its maps are component bijections inducing
isomorphisms after the group reflector. The local groups are closed under all small limits
and retracts, and under λ-filtered colimits for some regular λ. They need not be closed under
extensions.

Proof. Localizing at W1 first restricts to groupoids. The component condition ensures that
every discrete set is local and that localization preserves components. Hence localization is
groupwise, as also proved in Casacuberta et al. [25, Theorems 2.6–2.7 and 3.3]. A small set of
groupoid generators may be replaced by the set of its component group homomorphisms. H5.1
then identifies its local isotropy groups with their ordinary orthogonality class. Conversely a
small set {Gi → Hi } of group maps, together with S 2 → ∗, generates the stated fundamental
localizer. The local group reflector exists by the ordinary small-orthogonality construction in
the locally presentable category of groups; Casacuberta et al. [25, Theorem 3.1] identifies its
inverted maps with the mapping-groupoid tests.
     Limits and retracts preserve orthogonality. Choose a regular λ for which all generator
groups are λ-presentable; their Hom functors commute with λ-filtered colimits, proving
the asserted closure. Conversely a reflective accessibly embedded subcategory is a small
orthogonality class: choose a sufficiently large regular presentability rank and take the reflector
units on its set of presentable groups. Expressing an arbitrary group as the corresponding
filtered colimit proves orthogonality to every unit. Extension closure fails already for the
abelian local groups: the integral Heisenberg group is a nonabelian central extension of Z2
by Z.
     In this correspondence the first three cases occupy their indicated varieties. In the
remaining cases the exact class is ( ⊥ Ca )⊥ , the orthogonality closure of the stipulated coefficient
groups. It is essential to retain this closure rather than identify it with underlying pro-
completions. H5.7 explains the additional component information required outside the
displayed interval.




                                                 71
H5.9 (Proved). The rational unipotent case depends on set theory. For the
rational unipotent completion exactly as defined in LM8 B.6, with tests U (Q) for finite-
dimensional unipotent Q-groups, the following statements hold. If the fixed universe U
contains no measurable cardinal, then Wunip is not accessible. If Vopěnka’s principle holds
for U, then Wunip is accessible. In the latter case a generating set consists of S 2 → ∗
and all homomorphisms between groups of cardinality less than some regular κ ∈ U which
induce bijections on Hom(−, U (Q)) for every finite-dimensional unipotent Q-group U , realized
by classifying-space maps and categories of simplices. Thus an unconditional affirmative
answer to H.5(i) for this case cannot be deduced in ZFC with the stated universes; an
unconditional negative answer is incompatible with the indicated Vopěnka hypothesis. The
resulting independence assertion is relative to the consistency of the corresponding universe
and large-cardinal hypotheses.

Proof. We give the obstruction algebra and the smallness argument in full. For a commutative
unital Q-algebra R, let

            H(R) = R3 ,       (a, b, c)(a0 , b0 , c0 ) = (a + a0 , b + b0 , c + c0 + ab0 )     (H5.9.1)

be its Heisenberg group. Its center and commutator subgroup are both C(R) = {(0, 0, c) :
c ∈ R}, since [(a, 0, 0), (0, b, 0)] = (0, 0, ab) and every c is c · 1. It is uniquely divisible and
nilpotent of class two. In particular H(Q) is an allowed finite-dimensional unipotent test
group.
    We first establish an elementary detection lemma. If R has no nonzero finite-dimensional
unital Q-algebra quotient, then every homomorphism

                                         ρ : H(R) −→ U (Q)                                     (H5.9.2)

into an allowed target kills C(R). Indeed use exponential and logarithm, which are finite
polynomial expressions for unipotent matrices in characteristic zero; their inverse and
multiplication properties are Milne [54, Propositions 14.32 and 14.36]. The Zariski closure of
the image still has class at most two, since the corresponding iterated-commutator identity is
a polynomial identity and holds on its dense subgroup. A group homomorphism respects
unique roots. On a class-two image, addition in its Lie algebra is recovered by multiplying
group elements and correcting by the half-commutator. Hence the three maps

              A(r) = log ρ(r, 0, 0),    B(s) = log ρ(0, s, 0),         C(t) = log ρ(0, 0, t)

are Q-linear and satisfy [A(r), B(s)] = C(rs). If C is nonzero, choose a linear functional ` on
the finite-dimensional Lie algebra of U such that f = `C : R → Q is nonzero. The bilinear
form (r, s) 7→ f (rs) has finite rank, since it factors through A : R → Lie U in its first variable.
Therefore
                              If = {r ∈ R : f (rs) = 0 for all s ∈ R}                      (H5.9.3)
has finite codimension. It is an ideal: if r ∈ If and a ∈ R, then f ((ar)s) = f (r(as)) = 0. It is
proper because f 6= 0, so 1 ∈/ If . The quotient R/If contradicts the hypothesis. This proves
the detection lemma, for targets of every nilpotence class.
   Let κ be any infinite regular cardinal in U, and put

                        Rκ = Qκ /J<κ ,           J<κ = {r : | supp(r)| < κ}.                   (H5.9.4)

The denominator is an ideal. Under the no-measurable hypothesis, Rκ has no nonzero
finite-dimensional unital quotient. For otherwise a maximal ideal in that quotient gives a
unital map h : Rκ → K, where K/Q is a finite field extension. In fact K = Q. To see this,

                                                    72
represent any r ∈ Rκ by rational coordinates ri . If the minimal polynomial of h(r) were
irreducible of degree greater than one, it would have no rational zero. Its value on (ri ) would
then be a unit in Qκ , with its pointwise reciprocal as inverse, whereas its image in K would
be zero. This is impossible. Thus every h(r) is rational; the map is onto its residue field,
which proves K = Q.
                                                                                            h
    For A ⊆ κ write eA for its characteristic idempotent. The composite Qκ → Rκ −           →Q
defines an ultrafilter
                                     D = {A : h(eA ) = 1}.
More explicitly, Boolean identities for the eA prove the ultrafilter axioms, and h kills all eA
with |A| < κ; thus D is uniform and nonprincipal. For any rational function r = (ri ), put
a = h(r). The set {i : ri = a} belongs to D. If it did not, its complement would belong
to D; defining the reciprocal of r − a on that complement and zero elsewhere would give
(r − a)s = e{ri 6=a} , with images 0 = 1. Apply this to a function taking distinct integer values
on the parts of any countable partition of κ. Some part must belong to D. This is equivalent
to countable completeness of D.
    The general ultrafilter implication is also stated in Bardyla et al. [4, Theorem 1.1]. We
include its short proof to ensure that the resulting measurable cardinal lies inside U. Choose
the least cardinal µ ≤ κ for which κ has a partition into µ parts none in D; the singleton
partition proves existence. Countable completeness makes µ uncountable. Minimality makes
D closed under intersections of fewer than µ members: a contrary family, partitioned according
to the first failed member and its total intersection, would give a smaller partition with
no part in D. The induced ultrafilter on the set of µ partition classes is a nonprincipal
µ-complete ultrafilter. Hence µ is measurable, a contradiction. We have proved the assertion
about finite-dimensional quotients of Rκ without any compactness assumption.
    The detection lemma now implies that the quotient

                             qκ : H(Rκ ) −→ H(Rκ )/C(Rκ ) = Rκ2                          (H5.9.5)

belongs to the group equivalence class defining Wunip : every allowed representation kills its
kernel, and the factorization through a quotient is unique. The source group, however, is not
local for this class. Its identity map cannot factor through qκ , because [(1, 0, 0), (0, 1, 0)] =
(0, 0, 1) 6= 1.
     Suppose now that a set S ∈ U of functors generated Wunip . Choose an uncountable
regular κ ∈ U larger than the numbers of simplices in the nerves of all their sources and
targets. Products of copies of BH(Q) are S-local, since this allowed test group is Wunip -local.
There is a canonical group isomorphism
                                         ∼ colim H(Q)κ\A ,
                                  H(Rκ ) =                                               (H5.9.6)
                                               <κ
                                             A∈[κ]

where the transition maps forget coordinates. Its indexing poset is κ-filtered by regularity.
Indeed both sides identify coordinate families which agree outside a set of cardinality less
than κ, and the multiplication (H5.9.1) is coordinatewise. Nerves of groups commute with
filtered colimits, and those nerves are Kan complexes. Mapping out of any simplicial set with
fewer than κ simplices commutes with κ-filtered colimits: in each simplicial degree a map
and all its required equalities involve fewer than κ simplices. Filtered colimits preserve weak
equivalences of Kan complexes, as seen by representing a sphere, disk, or homotopy in one
stage. It follows that BH(Rκ ) is S-local. If S generated the stated localizer, it would be
Wunip -local, contradicting (H5.9.5). This proves nonaccessibility.
     Under Vopěnka’s principle the positive assertion is Cisinski [29, Scholium 4.2.17], whose
exact conclusion is that every fundamental localizer is accessible. Its application is legitimate


                                               73
because H5.1 and LM8 B.6 establish the relative Theorem A, not merely absolute asphericity
closure. Given a small generating set, bound the sizes of its source and target fundamental
groups by a regular κ. Since W1 is already generated by S 2 → ∗, replacing each generator
by its component fundamental-group homomorphisms shows that the set described in the
statement contains a generating set and is contained in Wunip . It is therefore itself a generating
set. The two implications distinguish the fixed finite-dimensional test definition from the
different localization at all uniquely divisible nilpotent groups; those targets cannot be
substituted.


10     Simple types, adjunctions, and strict globular expansions
Throughout this section a poset is regarded as a category with one arrow x → y when x ≤ y.
Its order complex is denoted by K(P ), and sd P is the poset of nonempty finite chains of P ,
ordered by inclusion. A categorical homotopy is a finite zigzag of pointwise inequalities of
monotone maps. A beat point is an element whose strict lower set has a greatest element or
whose strict upper set has a least element; an empty strict lower or upper set does not satisfy
the relevant condition.
I.1 (Proved). Adjunctions and finite cores. For finite posets P, Q, the following
conditions are equivalent: they are connected by a zigzag of maps each having a left or a right
adjoint; they are categorically homotopy equivalent; their beat-point cores are isomorphic.
This includes the empty poset.

Proof. Deleting a downbeat point x, with greatest strict predecessor y, has retraction r(x) = y
and r(z) = z otherwise. The inclusion i : P − x → P satisfies i a r, since ri = id and ir ≤ id.
For an upbeat point the inequalities reverse, and r a i. Repeated deletion terminates at a
poset with no beat points. All the comparison maps are categorical homotopy equivalences.
    If a finite beat-free poset R has h ≤ idR and h 6= idR , choose a minimal moved element x.
Every z < x is fixed, so z = h(z) ≤ h(x) < x; hence h(x) is the greatest strict predecessor
of x, a contradiction. The dual argument excludes h ≥ idR with h 6= idR . No nonconstant
zigzag can therefore leave idR . If two such posets R, S are homotopy equivalent by f, g, the
homotopies gf ≃ idR and f g ≃ idS imply the equalities gf = idR and f g = idS . Thus f is
an order isomorphism. Applying the core retractions to any homotopy equivalence proves
both uniqueness of the core and the stated implication. Conversely isomorphic cores connect
P and Q by the adjunctions just constructed. Finally an adjunction f a g always gives
id ≤ gf and f g ≤ id, so every adjunction zigzag consists of homotopy equivalences. The
empty case follows because a nonempty poset has no map to the empty poset. This supplies
the particular form of the finite-space core theorem used here; compare [5, Section 2].

I.1a (Proved). A finite collapse with a nontrivial core. There is a specified 22-element
beat-free poset P whose nerve is simplicially collapsible. Thus P and a point have collapsible
nerves and nonisomorphic cores.

Proof. Use six symbols a, a0 , b, b0 , c, c0 . Let K have the seven triangular facets obtained by
choosing one symbol from each pair, except abc. Include every face of these facets. Define
P to consist of all six vertices, all nine edges other than ab, ac, bc, and all seven facets,
ordered by inclusion. This describes every object, arrow and composite. Each vertex is below
at least two incomparable retained edges; every retained edge has two vertices below and
two distinct facets above; each facet has at least two incomparable retained edges below.
Consequently no element is a beat point. The exact finite collapse certificate for K(P )


                                                74
is generated and independently checked at each removal by code/I_finite_checks.py;
the ordered pairs of deleted free faces and their unique codimension-one cofaces are in
code/outputs/I_finite_checks.json. All pairs have precisely these defining properties,
and the remaining complex is one vertex. The verification uses only the displayed subset
order. In particular it does not infer collapsibility merely from acyclicity. Since P is its own
core, I.1 separates it from a point.

I.2 (Refuted). Adjunctions plus subdivision do not generate simple types. The
equivalence relation on finite posets generated by adjunctions and last-element subdivision
maps is strictly finer than simple homotopy of nerves. Its exact description is

                P ∼Q      ⇐⇒      core(sdr P ) ∼
                                               = core(sds Q)     for some r, s ≥ 0.

The poset of I.1a and a point refute the proposed equivalence.

Proof. The needed subdivision invariant is categorical contractibility: [6, Corollary 4.18]
states that a finite poset is categorically contractible if and only if its subdivision is. We verify
the ingredients in the form used here. A beat deletion induces deletion of a dominated vertex
of the order complex; conversely a strong simplicial collapse induces a categorical homotopy
equivalence of face posets. For finite complexes, a complex and its barycentric subdivision
are simultaneously strongly collapsible: take a minimal strong core L, and observe during
any proposed dominated-vertex collapse of sd L that the vertices corresponding to vertices
and maximal faces of L cannot be the first such vertices removed. A maximal face cannot
be dominated because all its vertices remain; a vertex cannot be dominated because that
would put another vertex in every maximal face containing it, contrary to minimality of L.
Thus the collapse can reach a point only when L is a point. Finally a beat-free poset has a
minimal order complex: if a vertex x were dominated by y, then every element comparable
with x would be comparable with y. If x < y, choose a maximal z in [x, y). Every w > z is
comparable with y, and maximality of z forces w ≥ y, making z an upbeat point. The case
y < x is dual. These are the arguments of Theorem 4.15 and Proposition 4.17 of that source,
and they prove the required invariant. Adjunctions preserve categorical contractibility by I.1.
I.1a therefore cannot connect to a point by the proposed moves, although its nerve collapses
to a point.
    For the asserted exact relation, subdivision preserves categorical homotopy equivalences
of finite posets. To see this directly, a pointwise comparison of monotone maps can be
performed one source vertex at a time, in decreasing order for an upward comparison. The
induced simplicial maps of order complexes are then contiguous at each step. A contiguity u, v
induces the pointwise comparisons F(u) ≤ [σ 7→ u(σ) ∪ v(σ)] ≥ F (v) on face posets. Thus
subdivision preserves the equivalence. Any finite zigzag can now be given common subdivision
exponents: for example, sda P ≃ sdb R and sdc R ≃ sdd Q combine to sda+c P ≃ sdb+d Q. I.1
proves the core formulation. Every allowed map is simple: an adjunction is a finite-poset
homotopy equivalence and hence a composite, up to homotopy, of beat deletions and their
inverses; subdivisions are simplicial subdivisions. This proves strictness as well as failure of
the converse.

I.3 (Proved). One-point weak equivalences generate simple types. For finite posets
P, Q, their nerves have the same simple homotopy type if and only if they are connected by
a zigzag of one-point inclusions R − x ,→ R that are weak homotopy equivalences. Write
A = K(R − x) and L = K(R<x ) ∗ K(R>x ), the link of x in K(R), with the usual empty-
complex convention. Such a one-point inclusion is a weak equivalence exactly when L is
nonempty and integrally acyclic and, in the component of A containing L, the map π1 L → π1 A

                                                 75
is trivial. Every such inclusion has zero Whitehead torsion. Among these inclusions, the sieve
Dwyer maps are exactly deletion of a maximal downbeat point. Dually the cosieve version is
deletion of a minimal upbeat point.

Proof. We have K(R) = A ∪L (x ∗ L). If A ,→ K(R) is a weak equivalence, the relative
integral homology groups vanish, and Hj (K(R), A) ∼    =H e j−1 (L) shows that L is nonempty,
connected and acyclic. The image of its fundamental group is killed by its cone, so injectivity
of π1 A → π1 K(R) forces that image to be trivial. Conversely these conditions and van
Kampen give an isomorphism on fundamental groups and on components. Over the universal
cover of the affected component, the lifted relative chain complex is
                                    Z[π1 A] ⊗Z Ce∗−1 (L; Z).
It is acyclic, so the simply connected homology criterion proves a weak equivalence. The same
formula computes torsion. An acyclic finite free integral chain complex has torsion represented
by integral unimodular matrices, whose classes vanish after passage from K1 (Z) to Wh(π1 A).
Extension of scalars therefore gives zero torsion. Other components are untouched. This
also explains why one must not replace the condition by contractibility of the link: acyclic
non-simply-connected links are permitted.
     The forward direction for a zigzag follows by composing these simple equivalences and
their inverses. For the reverse direction use [5, Theorem 3.10(a)], whose exact finite-poset
statement is that simple homotopy of order complexes is generated by adding and removing
weak points, meaning points with a categorically contractible strict upper or strict lower poset.
These are among the inclusions in the statement: their links are joins with a contractible
factor. The theorem’s construction applies to every finite poset; it passes from a simplicial
elementary collapse to its face posets by deleting the free face and then its unique maximal
coface, and connects a poset to its face-poset subdivision by weak-point moves. No assumption
that P or Q themselves are face posets is imposed.
     For the last assertion, R − x ,→ R is a sieve precisely when x is maximal. The nonempty
link implies that x has an old predecessor, so the upward closure of R − x is all of R. A right
adjoint retraction to the inclusion then exists exactly when the strict lower set of x has a
greatest element. This is the definition of a downbeat point. If the deleted point is isolated
the inclusion can be Dwyer using a smaller cosieve, but it is not a weak equivalence; thus it
does not supply an omitted case. The dual statement follows by reversing the order.

10.1    Realization and the precise invariance of torsion
We use the convention in which a relative cellular complex concentrated in degrees 3, 2, with
differential an invertible matrix M , represents the torsion [M ] ∈ Wh(π). Reversing the
global torsion convention changes the signs in the realization assertion, without changing
any zero or nonzero assertion below. Fundamental groups are based and identified with the
indicated group; changing base paths acts by inner automorphisms, which act trivially on
the Whitehead group. The chain definition and its additivity are those of [46, Definitions 12
and 17, Exercise 14]; the equivalence between zero torsion and simple homotopy equivalence
is [47, Corollary 5 and Theorem 8].
I.4 (Proved). Every Whitehead class is realized by finite posets. For every finitely
presented group π and every τ ∈ Wh(π), there is a weak equivalence of connected finite posets
f : P → Q, with specified identifications π1 K(P ) ∼
                                                   =π∼= π1 K(Q), whose Whitehead torsion
is τ . Every composite of the maps in I.2 has zero torsion. More generally, every zigzag of
the moves in I.2, interpreted as a homotopy equivalence of marked finite complexes, has zero
torsion.


                                               76
Proof. Choose a finite presentation complex B for π and an invertible r × r matrix M
over Zπ representing τ . The empty matrix deals with τ = 0. Attach r two-spheres at the
basepoint, giving B 0 = B ∨ rj=1 Sj2 . In the universal cover the new spheres freely generate
                              W

the summand (Zπ)r of π2 (B 0 ). For each column of M , represent the corresponding element
by a map αj : S 2 → B 0 : pinch a finite collection of disjoint disks to spheres, give each
sphere the required integer degree, and join its basepoint to the global basepoint along a
loop representing the required group element. All coefficients and loops are finite. Attach a
three-cell along each αj , obtaining the finite CW pair (Y, B).
   The inclusion B ,→ Y induces an isomorphism on fundamental groups, since the new cells
have dimensions two and three and the two-cells were attached by constant maps. Its relative
universal-cover chain complex is exactly
                                    M
                      0 −→ (Zπ)r −−→ (Zπ)r −→ 0,          in degrees 3, 2.

Invertibility of M makes this acyclic. The induced map of simply connected universal covers
is a homology equivalence, hence a homotopy equivalence by [38, Corollary 4.33]. The
induced map on fundamental groups is already an isomorphism, so [38, Theorem 4.5] applies
downstairs. Thus B ,→ Y is a homotopy equivalence and has torsion [M ] = τ by the definition
of cellular torsion. This is the matrix realization of [47, Remark 6], with its dimensions and
marking specified here.
     We specify why this gives a finite simplicial, and then a finite-poset, realization without
losing the torsion. Replace the presentation circles and disks by finite polygonal models,
and replace every attaching map by a simplicial approximation on a finite subdivision of its
source [38, Theorem 2C.1]. Build attachments by mapping cylinders followed by cones. A
finite simplicial map has a finite polyhedral mapping cylinder, and the mapping-cylinder
replacement of an attaching map collapses onto its original target. Replacing a map by
a homotopic map changes its mapping cylinder by a simple equivalence relative to the
target. Both assertions follow by triangulating the prism of the homotopy and removing
its simplices successively from a free end. Consequently we obtain a finite simplicial pair
(Ys , Bs ) with simple identifications to (Y, B) and the same torsion. Equivalently, for two
homotopic attaching maps, attach their two disks and the intervening prism as one cell of
the next dimension; either disk is a free face of that new cell. The two resulting inclusions
are elementary expansions relative to the old target. This is the explicit comparison in [47,
Example 11], where the homotopy between the attaching maps is required.
     For completeness, the same construction can be made entirely with finite orders. For a
monotone map u : S → T let B(u) be the disjoint union S q T with their old orders and new
comparisons s ≤ t exactly when u(s) ≤ t. Deleting the elements of S in reverse linear order
contracts B(u) onto T by upbeat deletions. The comparison from the ordinary mapping
cylinder of K(u) to K(B(u)), supplied by s ≤ u(s), is the identity at both ends. Its torsion
is zero: both inclusions of K(T ) are simple, so the composition formula for torsion gives
this assertion. Coning off the source preserves this simple comparison. Indeed the relative
cellular chain matrices of the glued map are block triangular, with the old comparison on one
diagonal block and the identity on the cone block; the torsions therefore add, with the overlap
subtracted, and all three terms are zero. This also proves the assertion after the fundamental
group changes under coning, by extension of scalars along the induced group-ring map.
Applying this recipe to face-poset maps is a finite algorithm for the simplicial replacement
just used.
     Finally take P = F(Bs ), Q = F(Ys ) and let f be the inclusion of face posets. Their
order complexes are barycentric subdivisions, and the canonical subdivision homeomorphisms
are simple: stellar subdivision of a face is obtained by an elementary expansion followed by
elementary collapses, and barycentric subdivision is the finite iteration in decreasing face

                                              77
dimension. Hence τ (f ) = τ .
    A categorical homotopy equivalence of finite posets has zero torsion: the core retractions
of I.1 are beat deletions, their order complexes collapse, and the induced map between cores
is homotopic to an isomorphism. A subdivision map also has zero torsion by the preceding
subdivision argument. The formulas

                       τ (gf ) = τ (g) + g∗ τ (f ),    τ (f −1 ) = −f∗−1 τ (f )

now prove the last two assertions, component by component if needed.

I.5 (Refuted as literally worded). Torsion is an invariant of marked homotopy
classes. Whitehead torsion of a weak equivalence between specified finite posets is invariant
under natural transformations and under localization at the one-point maps of I.3. It is
also invariant under equality of its fixed-source, fixed-target class in Cat[W∞    −1 ]. Thus the

additional assertion “and on no fundamental localizer” is false if it denies this last invariance.
The correct obstruction is different: the class of simple equivalences of finite posets cannot be
the restriction of a fundamental localizer contained in W∞ . No fundamental localizer refines
W∞ so as to distinguish simple equivalences from other weak equivalences.

Proof. A natural transformation between poset maps realizes to a homotopy. Cellular torsion
of homotopy equivalences is invariant under homotopy: the mapping cones of the induced
chain maps are related by a block-unipotent chain isomorphism, of torsion zero, using the
chain homotopy. Thus the assertion includes a zigzag of transformations. This is also [47,
Proposition 1]; its Proposition 4 gives the composition formula in I.4, after transporting the
fundamental-group marking. Realization sends every inverted map of I.3 to a homotopy
equivalence of torsion zero, so it factors through the localization as a functor to the homotopy
category of spaces. Equality of two arrows in this localization implies equality of their realized
homotopy classes; their torsions are therefore equal under the specified markings. For a
zigzag, torsion can equivalently be computed by the composition and inverse formulas of I.4;
an inverted elementary map contributes zero. These statements require neither a calculus of
fractions nor a strict representative of each localized arrow.
    The identical reasoning works for equality of two arrows in Cat[W∞      −1 ]: this category is

equivalent, by nerve and realization, to the homotopy category of spaces, and maps between
the fixed finite CW complexes K(P ), K(Q) have their ordinary homotopy classes there. This
supplies a fundamental localizer for which the literal negative assertion fails.
    On the other hand, the minimal-fundamental-localizer theorem gives W∞ ⊆ W for every
fundamental localizer W in the relative-Theorem-A sense [31, Corollary 4.2.19]. If also
W ⊆ W∞ , equality is forced. I.4 supplies finite-poset weak equivalences with nonzero torsion
whenever Wh(π) 6= 0; the explicit C5 example in I.8b below avoids making that nonvanishing
conditional. Such a map must belong to W , so the simple equivalences cannot be its restriction.
This confirms the exact obstruction in POLES D.4–D.6: a class of maps declared invertible
by a fundamental localizer cannot encode the extra finite simple structure. It does not say
that torsion of a fixed marked map ceases to be homotopy invariant.

10.2    What the strict globular moves actually preserve
The disks in 312 are free disks of a Grothendieck coherator model, whereas those used below
are free strict globes. There is no identification of these two categories of models. We make
the strict interpretation explicit because it is the one in the proposed pushouts in Cat and in
strict ω-categories.



                                                  78
I.312 (Proved). Audit of the coherator expansion and its comparison theorem.
For the Grothendieck coherators defined in the supplied 312 source, the elementary expansion
of any cellular model along an arbitrary cell is a weak equivalence. Its left semi-model
consequence and the deduction of the homotopy hypothesis from Henry’s comparison are
valid with the stated cofibrant-source restrictions. These statements concern weak coherator
groupoids and do not identify their disks or pushouts with those of strict categories.

Proof. We checked the four proof sections of 312 and both attached course chapters. Here
are the separate steps needed to confirm the argument. In its preliminaries, division is
performed on homotopy classes of cells with specified parallel boundaries. The successive
boundary corrections are operations on globular pasting schemes, filled by admissible pairs of
the coherator. They provide left inverses for multiplication; all the multiplication maps are
injective by the same correction, so these left inverses also give the required right inverses.
This remains valid when a relevant hom-set is empty. Whiskering to iterated units gives the
fixed-boundary form of the weak-equivalence test. Surjectivity is the existence of a cell with
the prescribed boundary up to one higher homotopy, and injectivity is the same test in the
next dimension. Thus the test is equivalent to bijection on components and isomorphisms of
based homotopy groups. Its witnesses use finitely many cells. This proves the filtered-colimit
and retract closures used later. The lifting calculation gives I-inj = W ∩ J-inj: lift a disk up
to homotopy by the test, then lift that homotopy along J to correct the disk exactly. These
are the respective claims of 312, Section 2 (source file 01-preliminaries.tex).
    The cutoff argument in Section 3 retains cells through dimension q and gives every parallel
higher boundary exactly one filler. Its interpretation of operations follows the original free
order of the coherator generators. If an operation has output j ≤ q, its generating arity
has dimension at most j, so its tuple is an unchanged tuple of the original model. Its faces,
even if represented by composites with a larger apparent arity, are already respected by
induction on that original order. Above q, uniqueness of the filler supplies both the operation
and compatibility. This proves a genuine reflector preserving all cells through q; it is not a
reordering of generators by dimension. As a left adjoint it preserves the algebraic pushouts
and cellular presentations under discussion.
    At a fixed cutoff the proof descends in the expansion dimension. The top-dimensional
assertion holds because the new parallel q-cell is already joined to the old one by the unique
higher filler, while lower cells do not change. In the upper-cell lemma, an upper boundary-
cell weak equivalence is factored by upper expansions followed by their right lifting class.
The descending induction makes the first factor a weak equivalence. The exact boundary
test makes the second factor lift the upper boundaries, so the first map is a retract of an
upper expansion-cell map. Its splitting and pushout assertion follow from that retract. The
free-shape assertion uses its finite low-dimensional spine; higher attachments leave every fixed
lower-dimensional test unchanged. For tree-shaped diagrams, the contraction removes a last
branch and the backtracking comparison is supplied in dimension two higher. Each use of an
expansion in these arguments has dimension strictly above the current induction index. Thus
these shape and tree assertions do not assume the expansion being proved at that index.
    Section 4 constructs a partial cylinder with Pj = Dj below the current index n, Pn = Dn+1 ,
and upper boundary-cell completions thereafter. The skeletal gluing formula makes each
one-ended inclusion an upper boundary-cell weak equivalence, hence split by the preceding
lemma. Its realization on every generating pasting scheme is contractible by the tree assertion.
The path object is then interpreted operation by operation in the original coherator order.
For an operation of output j, its two endpoint values and already defined boundary operations
give a map from the two-ended boundary into that contractible realization. The disk lifting
property supplies its extension. This verifies the full operations and their prescribed faces,


                                              79
not just a map of the underlying globular sets. The endpoint maps lift all boundaries. For
the joint endpoint map, the displayed tree domain in the source is an upper boundary-cell
weak equivalence into Pn+1 ; its splitting supplies the required lift against Jn .
    We also checked the final arrow-retract argument, where a mistaken use of cancellation
would be consequential. For i : X → Y = X qDn Dn+1 the unit gives a strict retraction
r : Y → X. Choose a section h : X → P X of the first endpoint and put u = p1 h. Both
endpoints are trivial fibrations, so u ∈ W . The joint-endpoint lift gives v = iur ∈ W . The
arrow iu : X → Y is a retract of v : Y → Y by domain maps i, r and identity codomain
maps:
                             vi = iu,     (iu)r = v,      ri = idX .
Thus iu ∈ W and then i ∈ W . For a test in dimension k before cutoff, choose q ≥
max(n + 1, k + 1); its disk and its one-higher homotopy are in unchanged dimensions. The
cutoff result therefore proves exactly the original test, for an arbitrary attaching cell, including
a composite or a coherence cell.
    The semi-model transfer of Section 5 uses no expansion with a noncofibrant source. A
cofibrant model is an arrow retract of a cellular one. An expansion preserves that retract by
using the identity on its newly attached disk. A relative J-cell map with cofibrant source is
consequently a weak equivalence, at successors by the expansion theorem and at limits by
finite-witness closure. The small-object argument gives the two factorizations, with the second
required only for cofibrant sources. The identity I-inj = W ∩ J-inj and the arrow-retract
argument give the corresponding lifting axioms. Units fill every J-lifting problem to the
terminal object, so all objects are fibrant. This is precisely the left semi-model statement
asserted by the course.
    The external comparison is explicit. Henry’s Conjecture 5.3.3 is the finite cellular expansion
assertion just obtained. Under that hypothesis, Proposition 5.3.6 makes the finite cellular
models a cylinder category; Proposition 5.3.7 identifies its completion with the models
of the same coherator; Lemmas 5.3.9 and 5.3.11 give the lifting against fibrations and the
homotopy-terminal condition; Corollary 5.3.12 makes it a cylinder coherator. Theorem 5.1.2(5)
then states that its completion has a geometric-realization/fundamental-groupoid Quillen
equivalence with spaces. Its proof uses the explicit semisimplicial cylinder coherator and
Theorem 5.2.10, whose realization is acyclic: homotopy full faithfulness is Proposition 5.2.8 and
essential surjectivity follows by finite cell induction. These exact statements and proofs were
opened in the primary source [40]. Corollary 5.3.13 records their implication for Grothendieck
groupoids. The finitely cellular hypothesis is satisfied by the all-cellular theorem of 312.
We use this comparison with its hypothesis discharged, not the homotopy hypothesis as a
premise. In Lemma 5.3.11 of the opened version, the map from the homotopy slice is called
a “trivial cofibration”; Proposition 4.4.4 identifies the needed map as an acyclic fibration,
and the lifting argument uses the latter property. This is a terminological correction in that
paragraph, not a missing comparison assumption.

I.6 (Refuted). The four-point diamond obstructs the proposed moves in Cat. Let
                                                            (1)
τ≤1 : ωCat → Cat be the left adjoint to inclusion, and put Dj = τ≤1 Dj . Pushouts in Cat
     (1)     (1)       (1)      (1)
of D0 → D1 and D1 → D2 , and inverse moves, do not generate all adjunction zigzags
between finite posets. This remains false if both source and target face inclusions are allowed.
The four-point diamond 0 < 1, 2 < 3 is a counterexample.

Proof. Reflection forces every 2-cell to be an identity, identifying its parallel source and target
arrows. Hence Dj ∼
                 (1)
                     = [1] for j ≥ 1, and the reflected face maps between them are identities.
                         (1)      (1)
A source attachment D0 → D1             at x ∈ P adds a fresh maximal point y with strict lower

                                                 80
set ↓x. It adds precisely the Hasse edge x < y and changes no old Hasse edge. The target
version dually adds a fresh minimal leaf. Thus all nontrivial moves are additions or removals
of leaves of the oriented Hasse graph. For a finite poset put

                              λ(P ) = | Cov(P )| − |P | + |π0 K(P )|.

This is the cycle rank of its undirected Hasse graph and is unchanged by leaf moves. The
diamond has four vertices, four cover relations, and one component, so λ = 1; a point has
λ = 0. Nevertheless deleting the internal beat point 1, then the resulting chain, shows that
the diamond has point core. Its nerve is a cone because it has a least element. The finite
order, core and cycle-rank calculations are checked in I_finite_checks.py.
    With both endpoints allowed, the exact relation produced by these moves is equality of
the reduced oriented Hasse graphs obtained by successively pruning leaves, retaining a single
vertex in a tree component. This reduced graph is independent of pruning order: vertices on
cycles and the paths between cycles survive; every other edge lies in a tree attached to this
subgraph, or in a tree component, and can be removed from its ends. Isomorphic reduced
diagrams give back the original posets by the inverse leaf additions. With source faces only
the relation is still smaller, permitting only maximal leaf additions and deletions. Neither
variant is the core relation of I.1.

I.7 (Refuted). Higher strict globular expansions do not repair the obstruction.
For finite posets P, Q, simple homotopy of K(P ), K(Q) does not imply that c∞ N P, c∞ N Q
are connected by pushouts of strict Dn → Dn+1 and their inverse moves, even when all
dimensions and both face orientations are permitted. Again the diamond and a point give a
counterexample.

Proof. The reflector τ≤1 preserves pushouts. For every ordinary category P ,

                                    τ≤1 c∞ N P = c1 N P ∼
                                                        = P.

For n ≥ 1 it sends Dn → Dn+1 to the identity of [1]. For n = 0 its effect is the leaf attachment
just computed. Therefore any purported zigzag would yield a zigzag in Cat whose only
nonisomorphism steps add or remove a Hasse leaf. Starting at a poset, all terms remain
posets: a leaf attachment preserves being a poset, and its source is a full subcategory of
its target, so the same holds when the move is traversed backwards. The invariant λ of
I.6 excludes the diamond-to-point zigzag. Both starting nerves are simplicially collapsible,
so every required hypothesis is verified. This obstruction concerns existence of the zigzag
and hence is independent of the model-structure assertion of 317. The strongest uniform
necessary condition supplied by this argument is the precise leaf-equivalence condition on
the reflected 1-categories; mere simple homotopy type does not imply it. General finite
polygraphic elementary expansions must allow a free face in a composite boundary, not just
one entire globular source face, if they are to avoid this obstruction.

10.3    Cofibrations and an explicit torsion certificate

I.8a (Refuted). Dwyer maps strictly contain the finite-poset cofibrations. Every
Thomason cofibration between posets is a Dwyer map. The converse fails, including for
finite posets and for retracts of Dwyer maps. Let T have six elements a1 , a2 , b1 , b2 , c1 , c2 ,
with ai < bj < ck for every i, j, k and no other strict comparisons except those forced by
transitivity. Then ∅ ,→ T is Dwyer but is not a Thomason cofibration.



                                                81
Proof. Generating Thomason boundary maps are Dwyer maps; their pushouts and transfinite
composites are Dwyer maps. For posets, retracts remain Dwyer. To check the latter point
explicitly, write an arrow retract i : A → B of j : C → D, with sections A → C, B → D
and retractions in the other direction. Fullness and the sieve condition pass to the retract.
Let Z ⊂ B be the upward closure of A. The section takes Z into the upward closure of C,
where the Dwyer right adjoint is defined. Compose the section, this right adjoint, and the
retraction C → A. The resulting monotone map r : Z → A satisfies ri = id and ir ≤ id.
These identities give i a r. Thus every cofibration of posets is Dwyer, as in [49, Lemma 3.13].
    Every initial map ∅ → T is Dwyer: its cosieve neighborhood may be empty. We give the
noncofibrancy proof, rather than relying only on the exhibited six-element diagram in [49,
Proposition 6.2]. Consider attachment of one generating boundary cell

                                   Y = X qc sd2 ∂∆n c sd2 ∆n .

An object of c sd2 ∆n is a nonempty flag of nonempty subsets of {0, . . . , n}, and comparison
means inclusion of flags. The new objects are exactly flags containing the full subset V . Apart
from the singleton flag z = {V }, deleting V defines an order-preserving map p : Y − {z} → X
which is the identity on X and satisfies p(y) ≤ y. Mixed comparisons are exactly those
obtained by first passing to this old flag.
    Suppose T is an order retract of Y , via i : T → Y , r : Y → T . If both i(b1 ), i(b2 ) were
new, their union, as flags, would be a new flag contained in both i(c1 ), i(c2 ). Applying r
would give an element above both bj and below both ck , which does not exist in T . Thus
some i(bj ) is old. If either i(aj ) were new, all four images of the b’s and c’s would be new,
giving the same contradiction. Hence both i(aj ) are old. In particular z is not in i(T ): it is a
minimal new object with no old predecessor, so only a minimal element of T could map to it.
    The endomorphism rpi : T → T is defined and is below the identity. It fixes a1 , a2 and an
old bj . For the other bj its value is above both a’s and below bj , forcing that value to be bj .
Its values at the c’s are then above both b’s and below the corresponding c, so these are fixed
too. Therefore rpi = idT : any retraction of T from Y already gives one from X.
    If T were cofibrant, it would be a retract of a transfinite composition of such cell
attachments starting at the empty poset. Choose the first stage admitting the finite image of
the section T → Y . It is not a nonzero limit stage, since that finite image is contained in an
earlier stage. The preceding argument excludes a successor stage. The empty stage cannot
contain T . This contradiction proves noncofibrancy. The displayed order is explicitly checked
by I_finite_checks.py; the proof uses no unstated choice of arrows in the six-element
category.

I.8b (Refuted). Finite Thomason trivial cofibrations can have nonzero torsion.
Every class τ ∈ Wh(π) for a finitely presented group π is realized by a Thomason trivial
cofibration between finite posets. In particular there is an explicitly specified example with
π = C5 and nonzero torsion. The program code/I_torsion_witness.py and its JSON
output give all finite simplicial and order data for this example.

Proof. Take the finite simplicial inclusion Bs ,→ Ys constructed in I.4. It is an acyclic
cofibration in simplicial sets. The left Quillen functor c sd2 therefore takes it to a Thomason
trivial cofibration. Both resulting categories are finite posets, whose nerves identify with the
second subdivisions of the original complexes. Subdivision changes none of the torsion under
the canonical markings, so the result realizes τ . This uses only the dimension-one case of
317, namely the model structure and generating maps of [65].
    Here are finite data and independent checks for a nonzero instance. Let Cm denote the
simplicial polygon with vertices 0, . . . , m − 1 and edges {i, i + 1}, indices modulo m. The

                                               82
simplicial map C15 → C3 taking i to i mod 3 has degree five. Its face-poset map has 30 source
and 6 target objects. Form its order mapping cylinder and add a new element above every
source object, and incomparable with all target objects. Call the resulting 37-element poset
P0 . By the mapping-cylinder comparison in I.4, its order complex B is a finite model, with
its simple structure, of the presentation complex ht | t5 i. In particular π1 B = C5 .
    Wedge a tetrahedral boundary S 2 onto B at its target-circle vertex 0. The program gives
a simplicial map α : S → B ∨ S 2 , with S a specified triangulated two-sphere. It checks
that every edge lies in precisely two triangles, that all vertex links are connected cycles,
that the triangles admit a consistent orientation, and that the connected surface has Euler
characteristic two. The surface classification therefore identifies it as S 2 ; no homology-sphere
recognition is being assumed in higher dimension. Its numbers of vertices, edges and triangles
are respectively 388, 1158, 772.
    The attaching map is formed from nine degree-one or degree-minus-one sphere pieces,
connected by triangulated tubes following the powers of the target-circle loop. The output
records all vertex images and all oriented triangles. It also records a Z/5 edge cocycle of B,
of value one on t, and the induced deck label at every source vertex. Counting the lifted
images of one oriented face of the target sphere gives the relative cellular boundary

                             v = 2 − t − 3t2 − t3 + 2t4    in Z[C5 ].

This is verified directly from the finite map, independently of the intended tube construction.
The multiplication identity

                        v(−1 − t + 2t2 − 3t3 + 2t4 ) = 1    (mod t5 − 1)

is checked exactly over the integers. Thus attachment of the three-cell has an acyclic
relative universal-cover complex. The augmentation of v is −1, as is the determinant of its
regular-representation matrix; these provide additional checks.
    Let ζ = e2πi/5 . In C one has v(ζ) = −(1 + ζ)4 , because the difference is twice 1 + ζ + ζ 2 +
ζ 3 + ζ 4 . Consequently
                                   |v(ζ)| = (2 cos(π/5))4 > 1.
The determinant after the ring homomorphism Z[C5 ] → C, t 7→ ζ, kills elementary matrices.
Every trivial Whitehead unit ±tj has complex modulus one. Hence [v] 6= 0 in Wh(C5 ).
   To give the actual finite posets, put X = F(B ∨ S 2 ) and let s = F(α) : F(S) → X. Define
Y = B(s) ∪ {∗} by placing every source element below ∗ and leaving ∗ incomparable with
X. There are 266 + 2318 + 1 = 2585 elements. The map A = F(B) ,→ Y has, by the simple
mapping-cylinder comparison, the torsion [v] just computed. The desired trivial cofibration is

                                        sd2 A ,→ sd2 Y.

An element of either of these two finite posets is explicitly a nonempty inclusion-chain
of nonempty chains in the indicated base poset. Thus the JSON specification gives their
complete object and order sets without printing a redundant list of all double flags. The
verification enumerates the base complexes and the attaching map; the last subdivision step
follows from the exact displayed formula and the left Quillen property. It is not a claim that
all double flags were materialized in the computation.

10.4    Finite fibrancy and the countable rigidity question

I.9a (Proved). Every finite fibrant component has point core. Every connected finite
Thomason-fibrant poset is categorically contractible. In particular a finite Thomason-fibrant

                                               83
poset with weakly contractible nerve has a point as its core. More strongly, a connected finite
Thomason-fibrant poset with no upbeat points has a least element. The empty poset is allowed
as a disjoint union of zero such components.

Proof. We first construct a finite test diagram. Let P be a nonempty finite connected poset
with no upbeat points. Take one vertex vx for each x ∈ P and join these vertices by a spanning
tree of the undirected comparability graph of P , orienting each selected edge according to
the order in P . Label vx by x. At each vertex labelled x, attach a fresh upward tree having
a child labelled z for every z > x, and repeat this operation at each fresh child. Strict
increase of labels makes the recursion terminate. Only fresh vertices are used, so the resulting
undirected graph is a finite tree. Orient every new edge from its parent to its child, and let
T be the order generated by these arrows. Its label map a : T → P is monotone and onto.
Every vertex t labelled x has, for every z > x, a specified successor labelled z. The poset T
is categorically contractible: a leaf is a beat point, and deleting leaves reduces it to a point.
    Put D = sd2 T and write µ : D → T for the twice-iterated last-element map. We claim
that
                          h = aµ : D → P,        g ≥ h =⇒ g = h.                           (I.9.1)
An element σ ∈ D is a nonempty flag of nonempty chains in T . Let C be its largest chain,
let t = max C, and put x = a(t) = h(σ). We argue downward over the finite order P . If x is
maximal, g(σ) ≥ x implies equality. For every z > x choose the successor tz > t labelled z
and form
                                σz = σ ∪ {C ∪ {tz }} ∈ D.
It is a larger flag, and h(σz ) = z. The induction hypothesis gives g(σz ) = z, so x ≤ g(σ) ≤ z
for every z > x. If g(σ) > x, it would therefore be the least strict upper element of x, contrary
to the no-upbeat assumption. This proves (I.9.1) for every flag, including all intermediate
labels in the attached trees.
     Choose a linear order of the vertices of T extending its partial order, and add one extra
vertex. The finite ordered complex N T embeds in the simplex ∆V on this enlarged vertex
set, and every face of N T is a proper subset of V . Both spaces are contractible, so the
monomorphism N T ,→ ∆V is an acyclic simplicial cofibration. Applying c sd2 gives the
Thomason trivial cofibration

                                D = c sd2 N T ,→ Q = c sd2 ∆V .

If P is fibrant, h extends to a monotone H : Q → P . Inside Q retain the old flags σ ∈ D,
the flags σ + = σ ∪ {V }, and the singleton flag {V }. Inclusion of flags gives σ ≤ σ + and
{V } ≤ σ + ; also σ 7→ σ + is monotone. Thus

                 g(σ) = H(σ + ),      c = H({V })    satisfy   h ≤ g ≥ constc .

By (I.9.1), g = h. Surjectivity of aµ implies c ≤ x for every x ∈ P . Hence c is a least element
of P .
    Now let R be the beat-point core of an arbitrary connected finite fibrant poset. It is a
strict retract and therefore remains fibrant, since right lifting properties are closed under
retracts. It is connected and has no upbeat points, so the preceding result gives it a least
element c. If R had another element, a minimal element of R − {c} would have c as greatest
strict predecessor, contrary to the absence of downbeat points. Thus R = {c}. Fibrancy is
componentwise, because each generating horn and simplex is connected, so the result holds
componentwise as stated.
    The construction of T , all its order relations and the descending forcing argument (I.9.1)
are checked on the 22-element example of I.1a in code/I_fibrancy_checks.py. This also

                                               84
produces a finite trivial-cofibration lifting problem that the example fails. The general
argument above proves the result in every finite size; it does not infer general fibrancy or
nonfibrancy from a bounded list of horn dimensions.

I.9b (Proved). An exact order criterion for countable isolated identities. Let
P be countable and have finite principal ideals. Enumerate its elements p0 , p1 , . . . so that
strict predecessors occur earlier. For pj < y let Tj,y be the following countable tree. A node
of length m is a tuple (y0 , . . . , ym−1 ) satisfying
                   pi ≤ yi   (i < m),     y i ≤ yk   if pi ≤ pk and i, k < m,
and yj = y when j < m. The tree order is extension of tuples. Then the identity of P is joined
by a natural transformation in either direction to no other endofunctor if and only if P has
no downbeat points and every Tj,y is well founded. This is equivalently an explicit transfinite
test: successively remove terminal nodes from each tree, at limit ordinals intersecting the
remaining node sets; every node must eventually be removed. The test is independent of the
chosen enumeration.

Proof. Such an enumeration exists: starting with any listing of P , append at stage k the still
unlisted elements of ↓pk in a linear order extending that finite ideal. This lists every element
and places all predecessors earlier.
    If u ≤ idP moves an element, choose a moved x with |↓x| minimal. Every z < x is
fixed, so z = u(z) ≤ u(x) < x. Thus u(x) is the greatest strict predecessor of x. Conversely
a downbeat deletion defines a nonidentity map below the identity. This proves the exact
downward criterion.
    An infinite branch of Tj,y is exactly a compatible family of the displayed tuples. Its
union is a monotone map u : P → P with u ≥ idP and u(pj ) = y > pj . Every such
map conversely supplies this branch. A tree is well founded precisely when its transfinite
terminal-node deletion removes every node: surviving nodes at stabilization each have a
surviving successor, which recursively gives a branch; a well-founded tree admits the rank
assignment ρ(s) = sup{ρ(t) + 1 : t is an immediate successor of s}. Since these trees are
countable, all ranks used are countable ordinals. No finite-branching compactness assertion is
being used. This proves necessity and sufficiency, and also independence of the enumeration.
Natural transformations of poset maps are exactly pointwise inequalities. If none can leave
or enter the identity, its entire finite-zigzag component is a singleton.

   The finite-ideal hypothesis cannot be replaced by a claim that absence of both kinds of
beat points is sufficient. For example, let P = N × {0, 1}, with (m, i) < (n, j) exactly when
m < n. Its principal ideals are finite; neither strict lower nor strict upper sets have the
required unique extreme element. Nevertheless (m, i) 7→ (m + 1, i) is a nonidentity monotone
map above the identity. On the other hand the branching condition
              x<z      =⇒     some y > x has no common upper bound with z                (I.9.2)
is a convenient sufficient condition for all the trees in I.9b to be well founded. If u ≥ id and
u(x) = z > x, then u(y) would be a forbidden common upper bound. Condition (I.9.2) is
sufficient, not necessary: the six-element beat-free poset of I.8a has isolated identity by I.1,
but for x = a1 , z = b1 it fails (I.9.2).
I.9c (Proved). Audit of INDLOCAL D.16 and D.17. The finite-ideal and no-downbeat
invariant of INDLOCAL D.16 and its countable bifibrant example D.17 are valid. They satisfy
the exact criterion of I.9b. Their isolated identity is compatible with I.9a because the example
is infinite.


                                              85
Proof. We check the parts on which that conclusion depends. For Q = c sd2 ∆n , the horn
H = c sd2 Λni consists of flags containing neither M = {0, . . . , n} − {i} nor V = {0, . . . , n}.
The greatest old subflag of a new flag σ is σH = σ − {M, V }, when nonempty. For any
attaching map v : H → P , the pushout has the old objects and separate new flags, no
new-to-old arrows, and

                     x≤σ      ⇐⇒      σH 6= ∅ and x ≤ v(σH )        (x ∈ P ).

These formulas verify the universal property even when v identifies old flags. Hence old
principal ideals never change; a new ideal is a finite collection of new subflags together with
either no old objects or the finite ideal below v(σH ). A new common upper bound of two
old objects gives an old common upper bound v(σH ). These statements also hold for a
simultaneous family, whose new objects have separate cell labels.
     A singleton new flag is minimal. If a longer new flag σ had greatest strict predecessor
τ , choose a new face N ∈ σ ∩ {M, V }. The predecessor [N ] forces τ to be a new subflag
containing N . Choose S ∈ σ − τ . If S is new, [S] is not below τ . If it is old and |σ| ≥ 3, the
strict new subflag [S, N ] is not below τ . If |σ| = 2, the old object v([S]) is below σ but not
below the minimal new flag [N ] = τ . Each case contradicts greatestness. Thus no downbeat
point is introduced. This verifies D.16 without an injectivity assumption.
     Start at one point, and at each of countably many stages attach one fresh cell for every map
of every generating horn into the preceding stage, including maps which already have fillers.
There are countably many such maps at each stage. The resulting union F is countable, and
the initial inclusion is a relative generating-trivial-cofibration cell map. Thus F is cofibrant
and weakly contractible. Every finite horn map factors through a stage and is filled at the
next, so F is fibrant. Ideals stabilize at birth, giving finite principal ideals and no downbeats
in the union.
     For x < z already present, a fresh one-dimensional horn attached at x has a new maximal
vertex y > x whose old predecessors are exactly ↓x. Thus y, z have no common upper bound
on that pass; distinct simultaneous cells do not introduce one. The common-upper-bound
invariant preserves this absence on every later pass and in the union. This proves (I.9.2),
so I.9b proves isolatedness of the identity. These are the essential steps of D.16–D.17, now
checked directly.

I.9d (Proved). The core statement requires an existence hypothesis in the infinite
case. For posets with finite principal ideals, adjunction zigzags still imply categorical
homotopy equivalence. Any categorical homotopy equivalence between posets with isolated
identities is an order isomorphism. Thus I.1 remains valid on any class of posets admitting
isolated-identity retracts reached by finite adjunction zigzags. Such cores need not exist, even
for countable posets whose principal ideals have at most three elements. Mere absence of beat
points is also insufficient for identity rigidity, as the preceding example shows.

Proof. The unit and counit inequalities of an adjunction give the first assertion without
finiteness. For the second, if f, g are homotopy inverses, isolatedness gives gf = id and
f g = id, exactly as in I.1. If the stated retract cores exist, composing with their adjunction
zigzags proves the conditional classification in both directions and uniqueness of the cores.
    For nonexistence consider the ray with vertices 0, 1, 2, . . . and comparisons 2k < 2k + 1
and 2k + 2 < 2k + 1 only. Every connected subposet with at least two vertices is an interval
in this ray, and its first vertex is a beat point. Hence every connected subposet with isolated
identity is a singleton. The ray is not categorically homotopy equivalent to a point. A finite
zigzag from its identity to a constant map would, on each vertex, give a comparability path
of uniformly bounded length to the constant value, while distances in the ray are unbounded.

                                                86
    Indeed it has no categorical homotopy equivalent poset with isolated identity, whether or
not initially presented as a subposet. If R were such a poset, homotopy inverses f : P → R,
g : R → P would satisfy f g = idR by rigidity. Thus g identifies R with an induced connected
subposet of the ray, forcing R to be a point and yielding the contradiction just proved.
Infinitely many successive beat deletions of this ray therefore cannot be substituted for the
finite categorical homotopies in I.1.


11     Statement-by-statement comparison with the opened
       sources
This section distinguishes an assertion printed in a source from a consequence proved
here using that source. The latter description is not a priority claim. The accompanying
REVLOC_source_crosswalk.csv has one row for every numbered statement, including the
unresolved statements. Bibliographical opening notes specify the actual text consulted. In
particular, opening a publisher’s two-page chapter preview is not recorded as opening a whole
book.

manuscript scan. Mathematical corrections below therefore identify a problem in the supplied
text without attributing its origin to the author, editor, or transcriber.

11.2    The previously supplied returns
POLES F.14 contains two separate questions. Its first, accessible reversible ⇒ RT -fixed, is
refuted by B.3–B.4, and even the full proposed sphere-null class is accessible by G.7. Its second
question, identifying arbitrary spectral localizing subcategories with homological Bousfield
classes, is not a consequence of this answer. POLES C.10 gives bounds for homological
intrinsic fibrations. E.4 replaces those bounds by an exact criterion for integral homology;
E.1 explains why the shorter comma criterion in the present prompt fails. The unresolved
implication recorded as POLES A.16 is settled by F.1 and F.1.1. Its description of [1] as a
nonterminal test base is incorrect.
    For GALOIS B.7, the properness/aspherical-generation interface is checked in E.6 against
Cisinski’s Proposition 6.1.8 and Theorem 6.1.11. E.7 and E.9 settle respectively the Morava-K
and KU cases left among the spectral examples of GALOIS B.14. They do not claim a
classification for every spectrum or settle every unenhanced-derivator question listed there.
H.1–H.4 answer the accessibility questions specifically requested here, with the universes and
the class form of Vopěnka’s principle made explicit.

                                                  88
    The uses of PRETOT, INDLOCAL, 317 and 312 are likewise limited to checked interfaces.
C.7 rebuilds the acyclic binary-icosahedral witness and verifies its finite category independently.
H5.1 audits INDLOCAL B.3, B.4 and B.6; I.9c audits D.16–D.17. F.3a reads and checks
the higher Thomason transfer; I.312 checks the elementary-expansion argument and supplies
Henry’s exact comparison statements. The supplied prose claim that a Lean check exists is
not represented as an independently executed proof-assistant check. No assertion of PATHS
is needed.

11.3    The localization and excision literature
Bousfield’s 1994 nullity classes. The opened paper [13] constructs space nullification
in Theorem 2.2. Theorem 4.1 gives its mixed fiber sequence; Theorem 4.3 gives a fiber-
preservation conclusion under its additional base hypothesis. Section 9.9 defines a stable
comparison of space nullity classes by finite suspension. That terminology does not identify a
localizing subcategory of spectra. Theorems 9.12, 9.14 and 9.15 compare torsion suspension
spaces using stable nullity, connectivity and finite chromatic type. Those hypotheses differ
from the cofiber-defined reversible classes here. They neither state B.3 nor identify the
interval in D.3.2. The explicit retractions and convergence argument in D.1.7 supply the
single-target Moore-space result used in this manuscript.

Bousfield’s 1997 map localizations. In [15], Theorem 2.2 concerns localization at a
map, Theorem 4.2 permits small intersections, and Theorem 4.4 supplies a nullification with
the same acyclic objects. It does not identify the two classes of inverted maps; D.0 gives an
explicit reason that distinction matters here. Theorem 5.2 is the loop/localization comparison
used in E.7–E.9. Section 2.6’s cohomological existence result imposes the finiteness of the
groups π∗ (E/p); it is not a blanket existence theorem for arbitrary coorthogonality classes.
The separate compactness proof in G.7 is therefore necessary for the full sphere class.

Dror Farjoun 1996. The publisher previews actually opened are Chapter 1, pp. 1–2,
and Chapter 3, pp. 59–60 of [35]. Definition 1.A.1 gives map-locality, and Theorem 3.A.1
compares Lf ΩX with ΩLΣf X for connected X; 3.A.2 is its cellularization counterpart. These
statements concern specified map localization and cellularization functors. They do not say
that an arbitrary inverse mapping-space null class is desuspension-closed. The proposed
infinite-loop sufficient condition is already refuted by D.1.1. The rest of the book was not
opened, so no negative claim about what it might contain is made.

Berger–Ratković 2019. Definition 2.1 of [8] calls a left proper pointed model category
excisive when its derived suspension is conservative, and stably excisive when suspension
is fully faithful. Propositions 2.3–2.4 supply the associated cofiber tests; Proposition 2.6
compares homotopy pushouts and parallel cofibers. G.3, with existence supplied by G.7, gives
an excisive example whose suspension is neither faithful nor full. Their stronger embedding
discussion and the monad hypotheses of Theorem 3.13 cannot convert conservativity into
stable detection. Thus the accessible counterexample is compatible with their results.

Casacuberta–Scevenels–Smith 2005. Proposition 6.2 of [23] is the reduced-product
obstruction used in H.3. Theorems 6.3–6.4 give non-smallly-generated group and space
localizations when measurable cardinals are absent. F.3c adds the model localization
verification required by the literal wording of this prompt; H.3 constructs the derivator
and checks its locally constant coefficient class. H5.9 uses a different Heisenberg reduced-
product argument for finite-dimensional rational-unipotent tests. Those added conclusions


                                                89
are proved here, not attributed verbatim to the 2005 theorems. The positive accessibility
statement in H.2 is checked against Cisinski’s Scholie 4.2.17 with its universe qualification.

11.4    The set-size question that is not settled by these comparisons
The attached Hovey–Palmieri text [42], Section 9, Conjecture 9.1 and Proposition 9.2,
separates the assertion that every localizing subcategory is a homological Bousfield class from
its principal case. Its proof uses the set of homological Bousfield classes only after assuming
that extra identification. This does not bound all localizing subcategories unconditionally,
and does not bound the fiber of T on reversible localizers.
    The opened [26], Theorem 3.7, establishes coreflectivity for singly generated semilocalizing
subcategories; Theorem 3.9 supplies single generation and coreflectivity under Vopěnka’s
principle. Theorem 4.2 and Corollary 4.3 concern orthogonality and nullity classes. Its
discussion on pp. 26–27 distinguishes the known proper class of one-sided nullity classes
from the set-size question with all suspension shifts. None of those statements settles the
fixed-stable-part question D.3.2. We record what these opened theorems imply, without
asserting that the article’s historical open-problem list is an exhaustive report of the literature
in November 2026.
    The present return therefore does not infer a third point in the loc(S/2) interval from
any of these cardinality results. Its two distinct endpoints are proved, and every additional
strictness assertion must supply a further unstable separating witness. The current precise
state, attempted constructions and next tests are recorded at D.3.2 and in the research
report.

     Notices, (1), 2024. doi: 10.1093/imrn/rnad044. URL https://arxiv.org/pdf/2208.
     12844. Opened: primary preprint dated 26 August 2022. Corollary 1.4.1 (Lee), with its
     precise simply connected pi-finite hypothesis and proof. The original Lee 1992 article
     was not opened; the quoted form is taken from this opened modern proof.
